1.20.0 opens the 2027 world. A fresh 2027 world has three country tiers: the United States and Canada as player countries, the United Kingdom, Germany, Japan and China as NPP-run full simulations, and sixteen background countries that skip most turn phases. Canada ships end to end (provinces, 343-seat House of Commons, provincial legislatures, cabinet, law book, budget, currency, by-elections) with the Quebec sovereignty referendum and independence path. Cyber operations, Crisis v2 (emergent crises, stages, blame, scars, forced events, spillover), executive succession, leader legacies, party renewal and politician turnover arrive with the 2027 data, research tiers for 2039 and 2049, new laws through 2050, and value added growth.

Rollout: new systems that change a running world sit behind gameConfig switches that fresh worlds default on (`FRESH_WORLD_GAME_CONFIG_FLAGS` in `src/lib/seeds/reference/featureFlagDefaults.ts`: priceLevelEnabled, fiscalStabilizerEnabled, crisisV2EmergentEnabled, crisisV2StagesEnabled, executiveSuccessionEnabled, leaderLegacyEnabled, campaignLiveGdpBasisEnabled, byElectionFillEnabled, scotusNppNominationsEnabled, scotusPendingDocketEnabled, preeGenericFallbackEnabled, cyberOperationsEnabled) and running worlds leave absent, so nothing shifts on deploy. The wage, price and household-demand changes (#6349, #6370, #6373 and the economy notes below) are off until an operator turns them on. Era progression, sector tech trees, the Cold War, conflicts and the other retired switches are always on; they were already on everywhere.

## What landed in this release

586 changes shipped.

### Elections and campaigns

- **A shorter state panel in Campaign mode**
  The state panel on the presidential map now folds its sections away, and the help pages say to use Campaign mode on the race page.
- **Act on a state from the presidential map**
  In Campaign mode the presidential map sits above your campaign desk, and clicking a state opens that state's campaign actions: travel or camping, operations against rivals, and canvassing.
- **Campaign managers can build Campaign Presence**
  Campaign managers can now build Campaign Presence for their ticket, paid from the campaign's funds and actions as before.
- **Field offices now live on the presidential map**
  In Campaign mode you can colour the map by field offices, zoom into a state's counties, and open or close an office from a county, without leaving the map.
- **The presidential race page now has a Campaign mode**
  Your campaign desk now opens inside the presidential race page. A Race and Campaign switch sits beside the title, and clicking a ticket in the race opens that campaign without leaving the page.
- **Leaving office now leaves a legacy that colours the next election**
  When a head of government leaves office, the game scores their time in power from approval, laws enacted, how they handled crises and what those crises scarred. The legacy shows on the executive and party pages, and gives their party a small, fading boost or penalty at the next election and in party renewal.
- **Operators can switch off three economy and politics systems**
  Inflation-based nominal GDP, the AI austerity and debt restructuring system, and self-starting crises each have an on/off switch. New worlds start with all three on. The current world keeps them off until staff turn them on.
- **Passed Japanese laws now move regional political lean**
  Japanese national bills and regional laws now shift how voters in the affected groups lean over the following turns, as US laws already do.
- **The 2030s get a real fight over who pays for automation**
  Four new laws for the US and UK, retraining, a hiring subsidy, an automation pension levy and transition compacts, join the automation tax and wage insurance, and all six now change jobs, costs, unions, pensions and voters.
- **Crises now have consequences: blame, scars and forced events**
  Economic and political crises now open on their own when conditions hold for several turns, cost the officials who respond real action points, and leave lasting marks. Office holders who sat on their hands lose approval and their party loses votes. Severe crises force strikes, bank runs and states of emergency.
- **Canada's House of Commons election schedule is built for the 2027 world**
  Canada now has an election calendar for its 343-seat House of Commons and for its Premiers. It is not open to players yet.
- **Canada's opening political map is ready**
  New 2027 worlds can now build Canada's provinces, parties, voters and Parliament, ready for when the country opens to players.
- **Canada's Parliament shrinks when Quebec leaves**
  After Quebec becomes independent, Canada's House of Commons and Senate count only the seats that remain, so a majority means a majority of the members still sitting.
- **Canada's provincial legislatures hold elections**
  Each Canadian province and territory now elects its own legislature, on its own fixed election date, for 778 seats in total.
- **Canadian Commons seats are refilled by by-elections**
  When a Canadian MP resigns, retires or leaves office mid-term, the seat now goes to a by-election instead of staying empty until the next general election. Canadian snap elections also elect each province's full delegation.
- **Four more new systems can be switched off on a running world**
  The new crisis stages, the succession rules for empty leadership offices, leader legacies and campaign money that follows each country's economy each have an on/off switch. New worlds start with all four on. The current world keeps them off until staff turn them on.
- **Quebec's Premier gets a Sovereignty tab**
  The Premier of Quebec can set a stance on sovereignty, and the sovereignty mood now shifts votes in Quebec elections.
- **Committee proposals show how many yes votes they need**
  The committee vote card on a party now says how many yes votes are needed to pass, so you can tell how close a proposal is.
- **Seats no longer vanish when a party bloc's holder leaves**
  When the holder of a multi-seat bloc deleted their account, retired, resigned or moved region, the bloc kept its seats with no party attached, so those seats dropped out of every chamber count.
- **Primary headline no longer says "The The"**
  The presidential primary screen read "The The Mafia primary" for parties whose name starts with "The". It now reads "The Mafia primary".
- **Campaign manager map can highlight where your party is running**
  A new My party button on the campaign manager map outlines every state where your party has a candidate, so you no longer click each state to find them.
- **2027 US seats follow the November 2026 elections**
  A 2027 world now opens with the US Congress, governors and state senates set from the November 2026 election results instead of a pre-election projection and 2020 data. Other start dates and existing worlds are unchanged.
- **AI companies adopt breakthrough methods, party renewal news and party spending**
  AI-run companies can now retool onto 2049 breakthrough methods once their sector's research opens them, the strategy panel shows the discount for leaving a retired method, party splits, foundings and folds appear in the news, and AI-run parties spend surplus funds instead of hoarding them.
- **Background countries hold elections, run budgets and offer a national index**
  In a 2027 world the sixteen background countries now have governments that rise and fall with their economies, budgets that follow their output, bonds American players can buy, and one national index each to invest in. Other start dates and existing worlds are unchanged.
- **Computer-run parties can split, form and fold in new worlds**
  New worlds get a party renewal switch. In countries run by computer politicians, a party that dominates or governs badly for two terms can lose a faction, empty political ground can produce a new party, and parties that fall under 2% for two elections fold into their nearest neighbour.
- **Computer-run politicians grow old, retire and die in new worlds**
  New worlds get a politician turnover switch. Computer-run office-holders can now retire from age 70 or die of old age, and a younger member of the same party takes over the same office. Existing worlds are unchanged.
- **Full Bundestag and coalition refusals in the 2027 world**
  A 2027 world now seats the whole 630-member Bundestag from the 2025 election, and the established German parties refuse to govern with the AfD, so the first government is a Union and SPD majority.
- **New worlds use fictional politicians only**
  Every new world now seats fictional politicians and judges. No real politician is seated anywhere, and portrait photos are only ever used as generic faces. The live world is unchanged.
- **Newer start dates no longer miss out on older settings**
  Several settings were written as fixed lists of start dates or chambers and had not been updated as new ones arrived. The 2027 start in particular now gets the opening alliances, major parties and singleplayer option it was missing. The live world is unchanged.
- **Proportional parliaments stop handing every seat to one party**
  Parliaments elected by party share now split each region's seats between parties again, instead of giving all of them to the largest party.
- **Seeded presidents keep the terms they have already served**
  A president seated at the start of a world now counts the terms they had already served, so a 2027 world's sitting US president cannot run again in 2028. Computer-run politicians now follow the same term limits as players.
- **The 2027 world opens with a seated president and current interest rates**
  A 2027 world now seats a Republican president and vice president, opens the five main economies on interest rates close to the latest real ones, and drops the old launch-week pause on company bonds. Other start dates and the live world are unchanged.
- **A banned party officer no longer blocks the seat**
  A banned chair, vice-chair or treasurer now counts as an empty seat for approvals, payout allowances and party elections, so the party can refill it.
- **Campaign income and costs follow the real economy**
  Campaign income and the cost of Campaign, Advertise and Build Donor Base now compare each region with the current national average, not the average from the day the world began. New worlds start with the same numbers as before.
- **Crisis responses and aftermath fixes**
  Crisis responses now always appear on the site, leaders of other countries can see aid and shared contributions, and the political fallout of a crisis is no longer lost if something goes wrong while it is being applied.
- **Eight parliaments, China and Brazil run elections from the shared description**
  France, Italy, Spain, Sweden, Turkey, Greece, Austria, Finland, China and Brazil now schedule their elections the same way as Ireland and Nigeria. Election dates, seat counts and regions are unchanged.
- **Election resolution code moved into the elections folder**
  The code that counts votes, seats winners and runs by-elections and snap elections now sits with the rest of the election code. Results are unchanged.
- **Election scheduling code moved into the elections folder**
  The code that opens and times elections each turn now sits with the rest of the election code. Election dates and results are unchanged.
- **Election vote rules now live in one folder**
  The portable election rules (ranked-choice counting, spoiler transfers, seat eligibility, tenure retention and others) moved into one folder. Results are unchanged.
- **Germany, Japan and the upper chambers run from a shared description**
  Election scheduling for the Bundestag, the Landtage, Japan's two houses and the French, Italian, Spanish and Turkish senates now comes from the same chamber description as the other countries. Dates and seat counts are unchanged, with one small fix below.
- **Ireland and Nigeria elections run from a shared description**
  Election scheduling for Ireland and Nigeria now comes from the same chamber description the newer countries use. Election dates, seat counts and regions are unchanged.
- **Keep player votes voluntary**
  Party and caucus leaders can suggest how players vote, but can no longer cast or overwrite votes on bills, leadership, appointments, confidence motions or impeachment. Hard NPP whips remain available.
- **Party budget estimates now match treasury currency; several stuck items close properly**
  Parties outside the US budgeted turnout and registration spending against the wrong currency unit, so percent sliders spent far too little or too much. Defence orders whose factory or company vanished, proposals whose party was deleted, and stale takeover offers now reach a closed state.
- **Party leaders and heads of government now step down between terms**
  In worlds with NPP turnover, a prime minister, president or national leader who is old enough to retire waits for the end of their term instead of leaving in the middle of it. When one leaves, the news feed says so.
- **Party whip orders moved into their own tested module**
  The steps behind a national party whip now live in one place with their own tests. Whipping works exactly as before.
- **President, Vice President and Incumbent achievements for every route into office**
  Becoming Vice President by confirmation or President by succession now earns the matching achievement, and The Incumbent counts every election you won.
- **Presidential election engine moved into the elections folder**
  The presidential vote engine, including the Nigeria variant, now sits with the rest of the election code. Results are unchanged.
- **Retired voter archetype wording cleaned up**
  Bill previews, the carve-up panel, the running-for-office guide and two wiki pages no longer describe voters as the old twelve archetypes. They now say voter groups, matching the census groups the vote engine actually counts. Elections, approval and polls are unchanged.
- **Shareholder payouts, double clicks and party ballots behave correctly**
  NPP shareholders now receive their share when a company is wound up or bought out, several actions can no longer be applied twice by a double click, stuck crises and records close properly, and party and union ballots only open when your vote will actually count.
- **Succession, caretaker governments and filled by-elections**
  Leaders outside the US now have a defined line of succession, a government that has dissolved parliament governs with limited powers, and by-elections no longer leave seats empty.
- **Takeovers keep extraction contracts; court reversals recover compensation; campaign costs match income**
  Acquired companies hand their oil and mining contracts to the buyer. When the Supreme Court reverses a government takeover, the compensation comes back. Campaign presence and state attacks are priced on the same basis as campaign income.
- **The 2027 UK opens with by-elections**
  A new 2027 UK world now sees four seats fall vacant across its first year, so voters go to the polls before the next general election.
- **The Supreme Court keeps turning over and hearing modern cases**
  In a 2027 world the nine sitting justices no longer stay on the bench forever. From 2027 each one can leave office at the same steady rate as a justice you confirm, opening a vacancy for the President and Senate. The Court also hears new kinds of cases about algorithms, personal data, housing screening, the power grid, platforms, gig work and election rules, roughly three times as often as before, and old cases can come back after twelve years once the fresh ones run out.
- **US AI politicians stay put**
  AI-run politicians in the United States no longer age out, retire or get replaced. Other AI-run countries keep their turnover.
- **Voters keep realigning through 2050**
  Four slow realignments now move how groups of voters lean in a 2027 world: an education divide, generational replacement, an automation shock in high unemployment places, and migration as a sharper issue where it surges.
- **Approval decay runs on demographic buckets**
  The turn step that fades your standing with voters back toward neutral now names and reports itself in terms of demographic buckets, not voter groups.
- **Approval weighting uses the census electorate**
  How strongly each issue counts toward a region's government approval now follows the region's census groups, in every country.
- **Candidate approval by voter bucket**
  How each politician stands with voter groups is now stored and applied by voter bucket everywhere, with no change to what players see.
- **Canvassing and turnout targets use census groups only**
  Canvassing, party get-out-the-vote and voter suppression now aim at the same census groups the elections count, in every country.
- **Crisis approval changes apply exactly once**
  The approval gain or loss that follows a crisis can no longer be applied twice if the game server stops or loses its connection at the wrong moment.
- **Debt spirals, spilled crises and canvassing costs now work as designed**
  Background countries drowning in debt interest now restructure, crises that spread between countries now end, and canvassing costs follow the era's prices.
- **Election engine reads buckets only**
  The election engine and the primary carve-up now work purely from voter buckets. The candidate tag on the primary carve-up panel is gone.
- **Elections count census groups only**
  Every election and primary now counts votes from each region's census groups. The old stored voter-group fallback is gone.
- **Elections count voters by census group only**
  Get-out-the-vote effects outside the United States now land on the census groups they target, and the median voter shown on an election page is read from the same census electorate the vote is counted on.
- **National contests count every region's census groups**
  Contests decided across the whole country, such as a list tier or a direct national vote, now count the same census groups as the regional contests, weighted by each region's voters.
- **Orbital debris timing and party history coverage**
  The orbital debris cascade now waits until 2042, when satellite fleets are established, and party membership history covers every country that runs the full simulation.
- **Party and coalition pages now tell you when something fails to load**
  Several party hub, coalition and state party panels swallowed network failures and showed an empty or stale page. They now show a short error message.
- **Polls read the full electorate breakdown**
  Polls now work from the detailed electorate breakdown only. The old voter group version of the poll is gone, and the headline numbers come from the same breakdown the elections count.
- **Primary projections and admin demographics read the census only**
  The primary board, the per-state primary page and the admin demographic tools now work from the same census electorate that decides votes.
- **Region demographics no longer carry voter-group rows**
  Stored region demographics hold only census weights and live drift. The voter-group shape now exists only as a derived view of the census electorate.
- **Show legislative leans and complete Scottish and Welsh tax options**
  Display economic and social leans on proposal options and bills, revise misleading positions, and add independent Scottish and Welsh national tax options.
- **Special presidential elections now use two rounds**
  When the French, Romanian or Russian presidency is filled by a special election, a runoff decides it unless someone wins more than half the votes.
- **Successions now fill themselves**
  When a national leader's office stays empty past its acting period, the game now holds the election or vote that fills it, and presidential pages show who is acting.
- **UK manifesto effects read the census electorate**
  When manifesto pledges sway a UK general election, the effect is now worked out against the census groups the election counts.
- **Union strikes and crackdowns hit voter buckets**
  Union ban strikes and standing crackdowns now cost the executive standing with specific voter buckets (low income, less educated, young on one side, high income and older on the other).
- **Vacant national offices always get filled, and the wire says so**
  A vacant presidency, premiership or party leadership now fills even when nobody is in line or no one can be voted in, special presidential elections show up properly on election pages, and the wire reports each step.
- **Vote engines count census units directly**
  The vote allocation, median voter and turnout estimates now read the census electorate units directly instead of a stored voter-group table.
- **Voter drift now runs on census groups only**
  Laws, newsroom slant and historical realignments now move census groups directly, and the lean shown on region pages is read from the same census electorate that decides votes.
- **Voter groups now run on census buckets only**
  Approval, turnout and policy effects now apply straight to census groups such as age, education and income. The old translation layer from the twelve voter archetypes is gone.
- **A generated successor now belongs to the largest bloc**
  When no one is left to vote for and a successor is generated, that successor now joins the largest bloc instead of being created as an independent.
- **Country elections run as one turn step**
  The hourly turn now checks every country's election schedule in one step instead of dozens of separate steps. Election dates and results do not change.
- **Crisis responses refund in full, healed scars ease unemployment, successors get their own names**
  A crisis response that fails partway now returns all the money it took, population scars bring unemployment back down as they heal, and an office filled by succession shows the new holder's name.
- **Election code cleanup: old redirect files removed**
  Behind-the-scenes cleanup of where election code lives. Nothing changes for players.
- **Election code cleanup: vote engine moved into the elections folder**
  Behind-the-scenes cleanup of where election code lives. Nothing changes for players.
- **Election code reads country rules from data tables**
  Behind the scenes, election logic that was specific to one country now reads from a shared table instead of checking country codes in the code. Nothing changes in play.
- **Party leaders, splits and seats are checked together over several elections**
  A multi-election test now covers an elderly party leader leaving office and being replaced while a party splits, with seat shares staying close to vote shares.
- **Party leadership election code reorganised**
  Behind the scenes, the code for party, state party, committee and caucus chair elections now lives with the rest of the election code. Nothing changes in play.
- **Regional laws passed through bills now move their region's political lean**
  A regional law enacted through the newer bill system now pulls its region's economic or social lean each turn, left for a left choice and right for a right choice.
- **Regional starting scores for 1953 and 1991 are now checked data files**
  The per-region political scores that open the 1953 and 1991 worlds moved out of code into data files that the seed check validates before any world is built.
- **Successor parliament election dates move into a data table**
  The fixed election dates for the 1991 successor parliaments are now listed in one table instead of country-specific code. Dates are unchanged.
- **Canada's House of Commons election rules are checked like every other chamber**
  The checks that every country's chamber election rules are complete and consistent now include Canada's House of Commons.
- **Four more new behaviours wait for a switch on the running world**
  Filling empty by-elections, computer-picked Supreme Court nominees, the waiting period before surprise court rulings, and spare random events for players who have seen every dated one are on in new worlds and stay off on the running world until they are turned on.
- **Keep party expansion inside current borders**
  Party expansion respects current ownership of transferred regions.
- **Regional voter shifts carry over to the new release**
  How far each region's voters have moved left or right since the world began is kept when the server updates. Before this fix, every region would have snapped back to its starting views.
- **Restore successor-country account and metric continuity**
  Reconcile transferred savings without duplicating balances, connect Ulster to Irish party access, and keep successor-country metrics readable after conversion.
- **Reunited regions keep their savings accounts and can join parties**
  When a region moves to a new country and its currency changes, residents' savings accounts now move with it, and parties can recruit in the region.
- **Scotland and Wales get campaign cost baselines, and one country's late approval data no longer stops a turn**
  Scotland and Wales now have campaign income and cost baselines, and a turn no longer fails when one country's approval data is a turn behind the others.
- **US redistricting law types live in the US country folder, with no player-visible change**
  The three state redistricting law types moved into the United States country folder with the rest of the US data. Nothing in a running game changes.
- **Voter group approval records carry over to the new release**
  Politicians keep the approval they have built with each voter group when the server updates. Before this fix, the update would have read every record as empty.

### Government and legislation

- **Government buying no longer arrives in one burst**
  With staged release switched on, a government budget line that went unspent during a shortage spends its saved money over several turns instead of all at once when supply returns, so a seller's revenue no longer jumps for one turn.
- **Minimum wage laws can set the wage floor**
  Behind a new switch, off until it is turned on, a country's national minimum wage law sets the lowest wage its companies may pay, so low-paying industries pay more when a strong minimum wage is in force.
- **Redesigned bill pages**
  Bill pages have a new header with the live vote count at a glance, use the full width of desktop screens, and put the floor debate right under the vote.
- **GDP now grows with prices, so debt and revenue ratios stay honest**
  Each country now has a price level that rises with its inflation. GDP is measured in current prices, so debt, revenue and deficits are compared with a GDP that grows as fast as they do. Reported growth is still the real rate.
- **Automation levies now fund the treasury**
  The automation tax and the pension levy are now paid by the companies that automate straight to the national treasury, instead of making their inputs dearer for suppliers to collect.
- **Automation levy is tracked on its own**
  The money collected from the automation tax and pension levy now has its own line in the national books, separate from import tariffs, and shows on the country budget page.
- **Canada and an independent Quebec get their cabinet pages**
  Canada's Prime Minister and the Premier of an independent Quebec can now appoint, reshuffle and dismiss ministers from the cabinet page instead of seeing a "coming soon" notice.
- **Canada and an independent Quebec get their parliament pages**
  Canada's House of Commons and the National Assembly of an independent Quebec now have full legislature pages instead of a "coming soon" notice.
- **Canada's bills move through Parliament**
  Bills in Canada's House of Commons now pass or fail each turn, ready for when the country opens to players.
- **Canada's House of Commons opens with its 2025 members**
  When Canada joins a 2027 world, its House of Commons starts full, with seats split the way Canadians voted in April 2025.
- **Canada's laws and cabinet**
  Canada now has a full law book and a 19-seat cabinet, ready for when the country opens to players.
- **Canada's national budget**
  Canada has an opening national budget in Canadian dollars, ready for when the country opens to players.
- **Government budgets can collect taxes and pay debts in real money**
  New, switched-off steps let governments collect real taxes from households, pay for public purchases from their own cash, and pay bondholders. Players holding government bonds are paid in full and first. Nothing changes until the steps are switched on.
- **State laws now say when they are waiting for their first funding round**
  A state law passed partway through the fiscal year no longer shows "Implementation stalled" on the metrics board while it waits for its first annual funding round.
- **Connect v2 legislative votes to voter reactions**
  V2 legislative votes now affect policy positions and demographic approval when laws are enacted.
- **Party chairs can file governor impeachments while their legislator has a bill running**
  The "File articles through party legislator" option was hidden whenever the party's only legislator in the state was already sponsoring a bill, even though filing articles is not a bill.
- **Energy transition: storage, ageing plants and climate laws**
  In the 2027 world, wind and solar need storage to run at full strength, old plants cost more to keep running, and three new climate laws let countries price carbon, close coal and require clean power. Surveying worked-out deposits gets dearer from 2035, and a recycling duty cuts rare earth demand. Other start dates and the live world are unchanged.
- **New laws for 2035 and 2040, and the first laws to retire**
  Nine more laws open as the world reaches 2035 and 2040, in the United States, Britain, Germany, Japan, China and Ireland. Pension age indexation now saves real money, and two older laws close to new bills when their successors open.
- **The twelve 2027 and 2030 laws reach the United States and Britain**
  Congress and Parliament can now pass the same new laws as Germany, Japan, China and Ireland: six from 2027 and six more from 2030. The automation tax and the carbon border charge raise real money for the treasury.
- **Twelve new laws arrive in 2027 and 2030**
  Germany, Japan, China and Ireland gain six new laws from 2027 and six more from 2030, covering AI, data, housing, minerals, cyber security, online platforms, automation, wage insurance, carbon at the border, heat at work, the pension age and nuclear licensing.
- **Budget balance now uses the same GDP as the debt ratio**
  The national budget balance figure was measured against an older yearly GDP snapshot while the debt ratio beside it used smoothed GDP. Both now use the same GDP, so the two ratios agree.
- **Surprise Supreme Court cases wait on the docket before they are decided**
  A new surprise case is now filed to the pending docket and the Court rules on it a few months later, so you can see what is coming and react before the decision lands.
- **The 2027 world starts from 2027 numbers**
  A new 2027 world now opens with each country's projected 2027 population, GDP, government budget, debt, growth and inflation, and with 2027 regional conditions for the United States, the United Kingdom, Germany, Japan and China. Other start dates and the live world are unchanged.
- **Bill approval previews use demographic buckets**
  The approval preview shown when you propose or read a bill, and the district pressure on legislators, now work from the same demographic buckets the elections count.
- **Bill whip count follows the chamber that is voting**
  The Whip count on a bill page now shows the whips for the chamber currently voting, so a bill in the Senate no longer shows every party as a free vote.
- **Identify independence consent bills as referendum results**
- **Japanese laws now move regional lean on the live world**
  The lean effects given to Japanese laws are now written onto the live world's laws, so passed bills and governor orders move a region's lean.
- **New laws for 2045 and 2050, and more laws retire**
  Eight more laws open as the world reaches 2045 and 2050, covering long-term care, the end of fossil fuels, digital self-ownership, water, advanced AI, lifelong pensions, net zero and space mining. Seven older laws close to new bills when their successors open.
- **Recruiting and upgrading units now spend the charges your office shows**
  In Cabinet v2 countries, recruiting a unit, upgrading a unit or issuing an order now spends one of the action charges shown in your office header.
- **Country bill lifecycles run as one turn step**
  The hourly turn now advances every country's bills in one step instead of one step per country. Bills pass, fail and expire exactly as before.
- **Long-run budget tables for the five biggest economies are now tested**
  Growth, inflation, interest rates, debt and interest costs for the US, UK, Germany, Japan and China are now generated and tested out to 2050.
- **The country budget page now shows the austerity package and its approval cost**
  When a government enacts an austerity package, the sovereign health panel on the budget page shows its size, the tax and spending cut split, and the approval it cost.
- **The old Soviet and East German law lists are retired**
  The unused old law lists for the Soviet Union and East Germany are gone. Nothing players see changes.
- **US and UK opening budgets no longer depend on the old law catalogue**
  The opening tax rates and program spending for the US and UK now come from plain data instead of the retired law catalogue. Opening budgets are unchanged.
- **A check keeps old laws priced in running worlds**
  Internal check with no change for players: removing an old law type now fails the build unless running worlds keep a price for it.
- **China keeps paying for its existing programmes**
  In worlds started before China moved to its new law book, the programmes it already runs keep their cost in the national budget, and its minimum wage stays in force.
- **China now runs on one law book**
  The old separate list of Chinese laws is gone. New worlds use the single China law book, with only the provincial resource tax lever kept.
- **China switches to its own law book**
  China now plays with its own written laws, tax rates and starting political scores in new worlds.
- **China's 1953 opening budget keeps its earlier spending**
  New 1953 worlds give China the opening spending its old laws priced before it joined the law book, instead of a larger authored figure that opened with a deficit of almost a quarter of its economy.
- **China's new law book is written**
  The full set of China's laws for the single law book is authored and checked, ready to be switched on in a later change.
- **China's provinces follow the national corporate tax rate**
  Each Chinese province keeps a share of the corporate tax raised there. That share now uses the rate the national government actually sets.
- **Era, approval and priority tests follow the one law book, with no player-visible change**
  Four more test files used US law types the one law book replaced. They now use laws that still exist. Nothing in a running game changes.
- **Existing worlds get China's new laws**
  Worlds created before China joined the shared law book now receive its laws and their regional starting rows.
- **Existing worlds get Germany's new laws**
  Worlds created before Germany joined the shared law book now receive its laws and their regional starting rows.
- **Existing worlds get Japan's new laws**
  Worlds created before Japan joined the shared law book now receive its laws and their regional starting rows.
- **German Länder keep their share of income tax and VAT**
  The German states again receive their share of national income tax and VAT in worlds where the national tax rates have not been changed by a bill.
- **Germany joins the shared law book**
  Germany now has its own 110 laws in the same law book as the US, UK, Russia and the Divided Germany, with metric boards, opening tax rates and regional texture of its own.
- **Germany now runs on one law book**
  The old separate list of German laws is gone. New worlds use the single Germany law book, and the Länder budgets read its tax laws.
- **Germany, Japan and China get the later-era laws in their law book**
  The laws that open from 2027 onward, such as carbon pricing, the automation tax and the four-day week, now sit in the same law book as the rest of each country's laws.
- **Germany's new law book is written**
  The full set of Germany's laws for the single law book is authored and checked, ready to be switched on in a later change.
- **Germany's opening budgets spend what they did before the law book**
  New worlds from 1979 onward give Germany the same opening spending it had before its old laws were retired, instead of opening with large surpluses.
- **Japan and Germany keep paying for their existing programmes**
  In worlds started before Japan and Germany moved to their new law books, the programmes they already run keep their cost in the national budget.
- **Japan can still pass tax bills in running worlds**
  In worlds started before Japan's new law book, Japan's income, corporate, payroll, consumption and tariff tax bills work again through the bill wizard and the cabinet.
- **Japan now runs on one law book**
  The old separate list of Japanese laws is gone. New worlds use the single Japan law book, with only the two prefectural tax levers kept.
- **Japan switches to its own law book**
  Japan now plays with its own written laws, tax rates and starting political scores in new worlds.
- **Japan's new law book is written**
  The full set of Japan's laws for the single law book is authored and checked, ready to be switched on in a later change.
- **Law effect simulations test laws that still exist, with no player-visible change**
  The checks that a law moves its metrics over many turns used US and UK law types the one law book replaced. They now use Japan's equivalent laws. Nothing in a running game changes.
- **Merger rules in Germany, Japan and China stay as passed**
  Worlds started before Germany, Japan and China moved to their new law books keep the Competition Act they already had, and new worlds no longer offer a second competition law that does nothing.
- **Money checks cover crisis aid and lever refunds, with no player-visible change**
  The check that every treasury payment is classified now also watches the new refund path used by crisis aid and crisis levers. Several unreleased notes had invalid labels and were corrected.
- **Older laws in running worlds still wait for their year**
  In worlds started before the new law books, old laws such as China's Common Prosperity or Belt and Road can again only be proposed from their historical year.
- **One entry per old Chinese law in the running-world price table**
  Internal fix with no change for players: the price table for old laws in running worlds listed China's entries twice.
- **Russia and East Germany law books are now plain data files**
  Developer tooling only. The authored Russian and East German law books moved from code into data files that are checked on load.
- **Saved worlds keep Japan's and Germany's minimum wage**
  In worlds started before Japan and Germany moved to their new law books, the minimum wage those countries already enacted still sets the wage floor.
- **Search and policy effect tests follow the one law book, with no player-visible change**
  The legislation search and policy tick tests used US and UK law types the one law book replaced. They now use laws that still exist. Nothing in a running game changes.
- **Tests follow the one law book, with no player-visible change**
  Three test files still checked US law types the one law book replaced. They now test what the game does today. Nothing in a running game changes.
- **The US and UK law books are now plain data files**
  No change in play. The authored US and UK laws moved from code into data files that are checked when the game loads.
- **US and UK voter priorities now point at the current laws**
  The laws that US and UK voter groups ask for, and the court rulings that change policy, now use the same law book as the rest of the game.

### War and diplomacy

- **Modern forces, cyber resilience, new treaties and new media**
  Defence ministers can raise drone, counter-drone, naval drone, space sensing and directed energy formations once their industry can build them. Countries get a cyber resilience score, five new treaty organisations open between 2028 and 2034, and media companies gain social, creator, AI and immersive models.
- **New crises and everyday events for the 2020s to 2040s**
  Worlds that run past 2027 now get their own crises, personal events, shared historic moments and country stories. Nothing is guaranteed: each one is a chance, and most have a way to soften or prevent the damage.
- **Opportunities for the 2027 world, and a limit on piled-up crises**
  Worlds past 2027 now offer good news as well as bad: trade deals, tax and climate accords, science projects, hosting bids and cultural moments. Each one has a price. A country can also no longer be buried under more than two serious crises at once.
- **Three new world crises can break out after 2027**
  An AI labour shock, a Pacific strait crisis and cyberattacks on power grids and networks can now start in worlds that reach 2028 and beyond. None is scheduled: each needs the right conditions and a bit of bad luck.
- **Choose how much aid to pledge from the crisis page**
  The aid response on a crisis card now has a size slider that shows the share of your GDP and the amount in your currency, instead of always pledging the default.
- **Crises board with a world map**
  The Crises page now opens on a world map of active crises, shows how each one touches you, and lists them by urgency. Wars moved onto the same page, and the nav shows how many crises you can act on right now.
- **Events for the 2050s**
  The 2050s now have their own crises, offers and personal events, and the random event pool no longer goes quiet when dated events run out.
- **Four new long-running crises after 2027**
  A second pandemic, a wave of sovereign debt trouble, a power grid cascade and a critical metals squeeze can now open in the late 2020s and 2030s, and every one of them can be prevented or softened.
- **Respond to crises from the Crises page and Actions**
  Crises on the new engine now show their stage, severity trend and a forecast of what happens if nobody acts, and let you pull the responses your office allows. The nav count includes them.
- **A fusion breakthrough, election integrity crises and a longer calendar**
  Fusion power can arrive from 2042 and trust in elections can slip from 2028, both shaped by what governments choose. The content calendar now runs to 2059.
- **Choose your stance in the second pandemic and the AI labour shock**
  Heads of state, finance ministers and central bank chairs can now take a side in the second pandemic and the AI labour shock, and live with the results.
- **Climate migration, the Arctic, resistant infections and orbital debris**
  Four more long-running crises can open in the 2030s and 2040s. Each depends on the state of the world and each has choices that prevent or soften it.
- **Long-running crises now spill into the wider world**
  The new long-running crises can set off matching local emergencies, and two of them now react to what companies are actually doing.
- **Clearer naval and air supply and agreed friendly bases**
- **Countries can launch cyber operations against each other**
  A government can now attack another country's networks. Whether it works depends on its cyber strength against the target's resilience, and the target may be able to name who did it. Either side can stand down to open a truce.
- **Crisis stance choices: fewer side effects, clearer text**
  Stance choices in the second pandemic and AI labour shock no longer pretend to cut severity, no longer trigger instant bank panics, and AI governments take at most one per crisis.
- **Cyber operations get a console and AI governments join in**
  The head of intelligence can now plan, launch and stand down from cyber operations in the intelligence office, and the governments run by the game take part too.
- **Seizing a foreign-owned company now raises world tension**
  Nationalizing an asset owned by another country adds cold-war tension, more so when it is paid for badly or not at all. Domestic takings are unchanged.
- **Organisation screens match who can act**
  A head of government without the foreign affairs seat no longer sees organisation buttons that would be refused.
- **Background countries set tariffs, impose sanctions and pick sides**
  In a 2027 world the sixteen background countries now act abroad on the views of their ruling parties: they set tariffs, sanction countries at war with their partners, answer calls to war and respond to world crises. Their trade surpluses and deficits move their currencies, and the Cold War tension console tracks the rivalry between the US-led bloc, the China-led bloc and the non-aligned world. Other start dates are unchanged.
- **Background country pages and organisation votes**
  In a 2027 world each background country now has its own overview page, and background countries vote on the business of the organisations they belong to. Other start dates and existing worlds are unchanged.
- **Automatic crises and living conflicts are now always on**
  Automatic crises and disasters, and the living conflicts (wars, pandemics, terrorism and refugee flows), no longer depend on a world setting. They were already on in the live world and in every new world.
- **Crises grow out of the economy and spread into it**
  New crises can now start on their own when the economy stays in trouble, and an active crisis now cuts factory output, moves prices and borrowing costs, strains banks, pushes people to leave and costs the government support at the polls. Policy you set earlier changes how bad a crisis gets. Switched off until the new crisis system is turned on.
- **Crisis decisions can no longer be paid or applied twice**
  Fast double clicks, simultaneous contributions and aid pledges in a crisis now count once. A crisis that fails to close no longer holds up the others that expire in the same turn.
- **Crisis engine groundwork: crises that evolve each turn**
  The turn now has a phase that can run the new crisis model: a crisis with a severity that rises and falls from real economic conditions, moves through stages, records who acted and who did nothing, and applies its effects to the world. Nothing uses it yet, so no live crisis changes.
- **Groundwork for the crisis system rework**
  The building blocks for a single crisis system are in place and tested. No crisis behaves differently yet.
- **Admin emergency switches for crises, conflicts and eras work again**
  The admin toggles for automatic crises and disasters, living conflicts and the decade era system now actually stop those systems when switched off. They stay on by default.
- **Crises spread between countries and AI governments respond**
  A serious crisis can now spread to the countries tied to it through trade, finance, shared alliances, migration and climate. Governments run by the game respond to crises at home with the same kind of responses players have, and the smaller background countries can start a spread or make one bigger but never decide a response. Crisis content has not been connected to the new spread links yet, so nothing changes in play today.
- **Crisis responses by office: levers, costs and emergency bills**
  Office holders can now answer a crisis built on the new model with a real response that costs real resources, and a legislature can pass a fast-track emergency bill. No live crisis uses the new model yet, so nothing changes in play today.
- **Crisis wiki pages rewritten for the new crisis system**
  The player wiki now explains crises as they work: stages, severity and the do-nothing forecast, who can respond by office and what it costs, the emergency bill, how your record is kept, spread between countries, and how the crises page, nav count and inbox alerts behave. The old decision-card page is replaced.
- **Every new crisis now runs on the Crisis v2 model**
  Crises opened from now on, including disasters, economic and political shocks, and the events of long-running conflicts, run on the new crisis model. They look and hit the same as before, and they gain stages, a record of who acted, and responses by office. Crises already underway finish on the old path.
- **General profiles no longer show a branch**
  The general profile stops labelling a general as Army or Navy, since a general is not tied to a branch.
- **Air wings and fleets show their real supply after each turn**
  Air wings and fleets now show their real supply after each turn, instead of an old value left from an earlier posting.
- **Bank stress fades, and banking panics keep their old strength**
  Leftover bank stress now halves for every turn nobody pushes it, and the banking panic crisis keeps panicking banks for as long as it lasts, as before.
- **Climate paths are checked for different farm, energy and budget outcomes**
  The fast, central and delayed climate paths are now tested to leave farms, energy demand and disaster costs in clearly different places by 2050.
- **Crisis effects now reach population, capital, trade and refugees, and spread between countries**
  The new crisis model can now cause deaths, destroy a region's capital, restrict trade and move refugees, not just nudge statistics. Crises spread to other countries through trade, finance, migration and alliances, and the spread shows on the crisis map. Authored crises are answered through their responses, never twice.
- **Naval and air command explains why a region cannot host a fleet**
  The Station list now says a region is a land region instead of calling it "no port" when it has a port rating.
- **War losses now depend on how much force the enemy has at the front**
  An overwhelming army facing a thin defence now takes far smaller casualties, instead of bleeding in proportion to its own size.
- **Wartime seizure rules groundwork, with no player-visible change**
  The rules for taking an enemy company during a war now exist as a portable module. No order can be placed yet, so nothing in a running game changes.
- **A cyber operation sent twice at once is charged once**
  Two cyber launches sent against the same country at the same moment no longer both go through: the second is refused and its cost returned.
- **An independent Quebec's international relations minister counts as its foreign minister**
  If Quebec becomes its own country, its Minister of International Relations is now recognised as its foreign minister in diplomacy.
- **Crisis responses and failed aid refund the treasury reliably**
  A crisis response that fails partway, or a crisis aid pledge whose bill fails, now returns its treasury money in every world, and trying the response again charges it again.
- **Cyber operations wait for a switch on the running world**
  Cyber operations are on in new worlds and stay off on the running world until they are turned on.
- **Generated enemy forces live in their own rules module, with no player-visible change**
  The table and generator for computer-controlled enemy forces moved out of the main battle file into a small rules module. Battles resolve exactly as before.
- **One cyber attack crisis per country at a time**
  A country already dealing with a cyber attack can no longer be hit by a second one until the first ends.
- **Only an attacker can stand down from cyber operations**
  A country can now stand down only against a country its service has run an operation against since the last truce.
- **Two cyber attacks at the same moment open one crisis**
  When two countries hit the same target in the same instant, only one attack crisis opens and the other operation is refunded.
- **War page no longer says marines can be put ashore**
  The naval and air panel on a conflict page now says whether your marines are supplied across the water, instead of describing a landing action that does not exist.

### Economy and corporations

- **Cheaper goods find more buyers**
  With demand curves switched on, households buy more of a good when it gets cheaper, within the same budget, so building into a short market can sell more by pricing lower. Richer regions lean toward discretionary goods, and households save more when interest rates are high.
- **Choose prices for supplies sent to your own sites**
  CEOs can choose free, quoted production-cost, or discounted market-price accounting for goods delivered between their own sites. Matching site charges and income leave company cash unchanged, and appear in sector accounts.
- **Market books record how much demand buyers could actually pay for**
  Each country's market book now records units requested, units buyers had the cash for, and units they received. Nothing about prices or sales changes yet; this is the measurement the economy rebalance will be judged against.
- **Prices approach their ceiling instead of stopping on it**
  With the soft price ceiling switched on, a good in shortage climbs toward the price ceiling gradually instead of stopping exactly on it, so goods in different shortages no longer all show the same price.
- **Research breakthroughs no longer inflate mineral deposits without limit**
  A mining breakthrough now grows only the resource actually holding the mine back, and a region's deposit can no longer pass three times what it started with.
- **The sector page says why output did not sell**
  When part of a plant's output goes unsold, the sector page now says why: your price was above what buyers paid, buyers at your price were shared with other sellers, every buyer was already supplied, or nobody bought at all.
- **Workers can earn a larger share of what plants make**
  Behind a new switch, off until it is turned on, plants pay their workers more where last turn's sales leave room for it, so households earn and spend more and company margins come down from their very high levels.
- **Wages can follow the local job market**
  Behind a new switch, off until it is turned on, plant wages rise where workers are scarce and ease where jobs are scarce, a few points a turn, and each country's wage growth, and so its inflation, follows its job market.
- **Natural Gas Focus extraction strategy**
  Extraction sectors can now pick Natural Gas Focus, which makes mostly natural gas with some crude oil, instead of the oil-led Oil & Gas recipe.
- **Own-site supply wording on sector pages**
  Sector pages now say when supplies sent to your own sites were charged, and show their value.
- **Sites with construction loans can now change hands**
  A site built with a secured construction loan no longer freezes its owner in place. Acquisitions, takeovers, spin-offs and state takings now carry the loan to the new owner, who keeps repaying the bank.
- **The sector page says why your plants run where they do, and what would run more**
  A new report above the run bar names the real reason output is low, shows what each unit sold for against what it cost, says what next turn's plan is, and lists the fixes that would run the most capacity.
- **AI companies pay their dividend after tax and stop going broke**
  Behind a new switch, AI-run companies pay households their share of profit after tax instead of before it, keep the rest, and stop expanding while they have no savings, so profitable companies no longer run out of cash and shut their plants.
- **Internal supply is easier to find in the CEO Office**
  The CEO Office section bar now has an Internal supply link that jumps straight to your supply settings.
- **Sector Selling tile matches sold and made units**
  The Selling tile on the sector page now uses the same sold and made unit counts as the Offered, Sold and Unsold rows.
- **Shareholders can see private corporations they own**
  If you hold shares in a private corporation, you can now see its company information and Shares tab.
- **A 2049 research tier, frontier programmes and retiring old methods**
  Research continues past 2049 with a speculative frontier tier whose breakthrough methods need research across the whole sector, repeatable frontier programmes that keep R&D useful forever, and retirement of coal-heavy power and combustion vehicle lines with discounted conversion.
- **A new 2039 research tier with new production methods in twelve sectors**
  Research no longer stops at the 2029 tier. From 2039 every company has a new tier of Corporate and Sector research, and twelve sectors gain a new production method that costs more to run than the methods it replaces.
- **Choose which plants sell to outside buyers before your own plants**
  A new switch on a sector page lets you have that sector sell to outside buyers first, so your own plants only get what is left. Off by default.
- **Former Eastern Bloc countries get banks once they have market economies**
  Poland, Romania, Czechoslovakia, Hungary, Bulgaria, Yugoslavia and Russia now get their two AI-run banks automatically once they are market economies, so companies there can borrow to build.
- **Internal turn timing diagnostics**
  Turn processing now records how long each stage of the market step takes. There is no gameplay change.
- **Own trucks hauled figure on logistics rows**
  Logistics rows on the corporation page now show how much your own trucks hauled out of that state last turn.
- **Every country now charts population, GDP and GDP per capita over time**
  The economy page for any country, playable or economy-only, now has a national history chart for population, GDP and GDP per capita. It reads the per-turn levels the world already records, so the hourly turn gains no extra work.
- **Groundwork for measuring GDP growth by value added**
  Output growth is still measured from gross production. A tested value-added measure now exists beside it so the two can be compared before any switch.
- **New worlds measure GDP growth by value added**
  Fresh worlds now judge regional output growth by what each industry adds, not by everything it produces. The current world keeps its existing measure.
- **Background countries now post briefs, and restructure when their debt spirals**
  Each of the 16 background countries now posts a short brief to the wire and its country page when something changes: a credit downgrade or debt stress, an election, a big currency move, an austerity package or a debt restructuring. Every brief links straight to buying its bonds, its national index fund and trading its currency. The country page also shows its public finances.
- **Frontier programme races for the 2040s**
  Nations and corporations now race to finish the fusion, orbital and biotech programmes. First to the threshold takes the method.
- **Every sector gets a new production method in the 2040s**
  Finance, retail, vehicles, real estate and mining each gain a new production method that opens with the 2039 research tier.
- **Treaties change who trades with whom**
  While the Critical Minerals Club or the Arctic Agreement has terms in force, its members trade more with each other on the goods it covers, and at a slightly lower landed cost. Sanctions already close trade between the two sides; that is now covered by tests.
- **Canada and an independent Quebec track national figures**
  Canada and an independent Quebec now get a national summary of their economy each turn, which computer-run governments and central banks use to make decisions.
- **The Canadian dollar gets an exchange rate**
  The Canadian dollar now has its own exchange rate and home country, ready for when Canada opens to players.
- **Unpaid income tax is collected from your other currency wallets**
  An income-tax bill your assessed wallet could not cover used to stay unpaid forever. Collection now takes the rest from your other personal currency wallets at the current market rate.
- **Short inputs say why your own output did not arrive**
  When a plant is short of something your company makes elsewhere, the sector page now says why that output did not cover it: a market-first supply policy, no haul to that region, or a supply agreement holding that share of demand.
- **AI mines grow on the prices of what they actually dig**
  AI oil and gas wells kept adding capacity because coal and rare earth were short, while crude oil sat at the price floor. AI mines now decide whether to grow, and whether to reopen, from the prices of their own outputs.
- **Company contests fill in after an economy reset**
  The weekly company growth and comeback contests no longer stay empty for a whole week when they open right after an economy reset.
- **Country commodity markets show what buyers really ordered**
  A country's commodity market left out orders from government, military and investment buyers, so goods that were short could read as a large surplus.
- **Index funds no longer get stuck on holders that no longer exist**
  A deleted holder can no longer freeze an index fund. A wind-up pays everyone else and sends the missing holder's share to the central bank reserve, and a queued withdrawal nobody can receive is closed so the fund trades again.
- **Local services say which states are short**
  Healthcare, real estate, construction and freight sell only inside each state, so their world shortage could not tell you where a plant would find buyers. The commodities list now says how many states are actually short.
- **Mothball a sector from its row**
  The sector list's actions menu now has Mothball, or Reactivate on a stowed sector, so you no longer have to open the sector page to stow its plants.
- **National demand can count government and investment orders**
  Behind a new stage switch, a country's national demand includes the orders its government and investment buyers place, not only orders from buyers placed in a state. Off by default; nothing changes until it is switched on.
- **Sector input gauge now shows what actually held your plant back**
  The input availability figure on sector pages came from an old market model and sat at 85% for most plants, often blaming freight even when the company's own logistics site delivered every unit the plant needed.
- **Sector run chart separates output sent to your sites**
  The sector run chart shows goods sent to your own sites separately from unsold goods, so useful internal deliveries no longer look like wasted output.
- **Governments buy from their own plants first and share the rest fairly**
  Behind two new stage switches, a government's world-market orders take its own country's sellers first, and what is left on the world market is shared between governments in proportion to what each still needs, instead of in alphabetical order. Both are off by default; nothing changes until they are switched on.
- **Plants recover their output from their plan, and the page says what holds them**
  Behind a new switch, off until it is turned on, a plant that sells out plans next turn's output from what it planned, not from what a cash or input shortage let it make, recovers in steady steps, and needs two bad sales turns in a row before it drops to a minimum run.
- **Plants whose sales pay for them keep running after a big build, idle plants can restart, and builds keep a cash reserve**
  Behind a new stage switch, a company that spent its cash still runs plants whose sales cover their costs at market prices, instead of being held to the cash left over. Behind a second switch, an idle plant with no cash and no recent sales can run a small trial run financed by what it sells. Capacity builds paid from cash now stop before cash falls below three turns of the plants' running costs, unless the CEO confirms.
- **Priced deliveries between your own sites now show in the site accounts**
  When internal supply pricing is set to production cost or market price, the matching charges and income now appear on each site's accounts.
- **Refinanced government bonds no longer pile up into one giant maturity**
  When a government cannot repay a maturing bond in cash, the replacement paper it issues can now go into one bond per country and interest rate for that turn, not one new bond for every bond that matured. The debt, the interest and every holder's position stay the same. The setting starts off.
- **Short local service markets can reopen to new healthcare firms**
  Behind a new stage switch, AI and autopilot companies read room in a state's healthcare, real estate and construction market from that state's unmet demand, not only from an unowned pool that never refills. Off by default; nothing changes until it is switched on.
- **Substitution orders no longer make a good look short**
  When households cannot buy their first choice, part of that money goes to other goods as substitution orders. Those orders are now counted separately, so a cheap good that catches them no longer reads as badly short on country market pages. Nothing about trading, prices or money changes.
- **A plant feeding your own short factories keeps running**
  When your own factories cannot get enough of something your company also makes, what your plant sends them now counts at what they would pay for it, so the game no longer cuts that plant for a low market price.
- **AI companies can borrow to build in markets that are running short**
  Behind a new stage switch, an AI company whose plant sells out into a short market, and earns back a new unit in time, can ask a bank to fund more capacity even when its cash sits below its safety reserve. Off by default; nothing changes until it is switched on.
- **Builds due during an economy pause now arrive when it ends**
  New capacity you paid for is no longer lost if its build finishes while the economy is paused. It arrives in full on the first turn after the pause.
- **Choosing to sell on the market first now works**
  A company that chooses to sell on the open market first now sells to outside buyers before its own sites. Its own sites use whatever is left, then buy from other sellers.
- **Households spend from what they earn, not from everything they have saved**
  Behind a new switch, households budget from this turn's wages plus a share of their savings, instead of trying to spend most of all the cash they hold every turn. Shortages stop looking several times worse than they are.
- **Prices can climb again when buyers go short**
  Behind a new switch, off until it is turned on, a company that sold out in a market where buyers went short asks a little more next turn, so a shortage lifts the price instead of leaving it stuck below what goods cost to make.
- **Product development no longer takes the whole R&D budget**
  A product in development now takes at most half of the R&D budget each turn, so research always keeps growing, and earlier product projects are easy to find.
- **Reconcile financial and economy displays**
  Financial reports, portfolios and economy pages now use consistent units, holdings and published unemployment values.
- **Sites that supply their own company now plan for that company's needs**
  A site whose output goes to its own company's other sites now plans its run from what those sites need, instead of repeating last turn's tiny run while the company buys the rest on the market.
- **Sites that supply their own company now show what that output is worth**
  A site that sends its output to its own company's other sites can now be credited at the market price for it, so its accounts stop showing a loss for goods the company would otherwise have bought.
- **Sites that supply your own company no longer show a fake loss**
  Profit and margins on the company and sector pages now count what your sites send each other, and the Internal supply panel shows what your sites actually sent your other sites last turn.
- **Turns no longer stall after a retried turn changes its construction funding**
  When a turn failed after paying out construction funding, every retry worked the funding out again slightly differently and was refused, so turns stopped. A retry now corrects the earlier payment to its own figures and carries on.
- **Unpaid government bond interest is kept as a few records a turn, not one per bond**
  When a Treasury cannot pay its bond interest, what it owes can now be recorded as one claim per turn for players and one for the bond market, funds and NPPs, instead of one claim per bond. Every holder is owed exactly the same amount. The setting starts off.
- **AI companies can expand plants that make several goods when one of them sells out**
  Behind a new stage switch, an AI company's plant that makes more than one good is judged by its best-selling product, and goods it supplies to its own sites count as sold. Off by default; nothing changes until it is switched on.
- **Autopilot leaves a sector you restarted running**
  When you reactivate a parked sector by hand, autopilot no longer parks it again on the next turn. It stays hands-off for 24 turns.
- **Builds no longer starve a profitable company's next run**
  A build paid partly by a loan now keeps the running reserve for the part you pay in cash, plants count what your own sites pay them when judging whether their sales fund the run, and the build screens show how much of next turn's run the build would cut.
- **Companies ship all their goods on their own trucks first**
  A company's freight capacity in a state now carries everything it ships out of that state first, including goods sold to the general market. Rivals get the remainder.
- **Companies ship their own goods first on freight they own**
  Behind a new stage switch, a company that hauls its own output between its own plants gets first use of the freight capacity it owns in the state the goods leave. Only the rest of its capacity joins the shared pool. Off by default; nothing changes until it is switched on.
- **Corporation types split into two files**
  Internal housekeeping: the corporation sector types moved to their own file. Nothing changes in the game.
- **Fallback site margin rows say they count outside sales only**
  Where a site page falls back to the older margin breakdown, the margin line is now labelled as outside sales only, so it is not read as the site's full result.
- **Held freight capacity can no longer be spent by rivals in a crowded state**
  When a state's freight was nearly full, other companies could run past normal capacity and use the trucks held for the owner's own goods. Held trucks now stay held.
- **Lender-sale sites can be bought into a market you already run**
  Buying a site listed in a lender sale no longer fails when your corporation already operates that sector in the same state.
- **Own freight use is now recorded each turn**
  The shipping network record now shows how much of each owner's own freight capacity was used by their own shipments, per state.
- **Plants expect rivals to match their price when they price above households**
  Behind the next step of the household price switch, a plant judging a price assumes rivals asking more will come down to it, so a whole market no longer prices itself out of what households can pay.
- **Plants stop pricing food a fraction above what households will pay**
  Behind a new switch, an automatic plant that could earn almost the same at a price households can pay now picks that price, instead of one a hair above it that only other companies can reach.
- **Plants that supply their own company are no longer treated as losing money**
  When deliveries between a company's own sites are valued at market prices, a plant whose output goes to its own company is now judged on that value, so it is no longer marked as struggling or wound down for output its company would otherwise have bought.
- **Prices in short markets can rise past one cheap seller**
  Behind the next step of the shortage price switch, a plant that sells out in a short market raises its price from what it charged last turn, so one company selling cheap no longer holds every other seller's price down.
- **Retooling a sector now shows in the company's finance history**
  Changing a sector's strategy, or cancelling a change in progress, takes cash from the company. That charge now appears in the finance history instead of leaving the balance lower with no entry to explain it.
- **Sector "stuck" figure now reflects your own goods**
  The delivery warning on a sector used to show how much of the whole state's output was stuck. It now shows how much of your company's own output from that state was stuck.
- **Sector revenue, profit and margins use the same site accounts**
  Sector rows, expanded accounts and sector pages now use the same settled revenue, costs and profit, including priced deliveries to your other sites. Free-delivery estimates stay separate from actual earnings.
- **Supply chains finish planning before the market settles**
  Production planning continues until individual sites settle, within a pass limit and a cooperative time budget. Unfinished plans are recorded for investigation.
- **2027 exchange rates, the modern Turkish lira and new currency markets**
  A 2027 world now opens with real 2024 exchange rates, prices Turkey in the modern lira, and adds currency markets for Hungary, Poland and Romania. Turns in every world also spend less time filing election challengers.
- **A site that feeds your own other sites is now valued for the goods it hands over**
  Behind a new stage switch, a site whose whole output goes to your company's other sites is valued on those deliveries instead of at the paid cost of its capacity. Off by default; nothing changes until it is switched on.
- **AI companies short of cash can still borrow to build where goods are short**
  An AI company held at its cash floor can again ask a bank to fund new capacity in a market where buyers are going short, putting in at most a tenth of its own cash.
- **AI-run governments now act on runaway deficits**
  Countries run by AI governments now pass an austerity budget each fiscal year when their deficit is out of control, and restructure their debt when interest swallows more than half of their revenue for two years running.
- **An embargo no longer slows every company's turn**
  Trade route limits now apply only to the buyers an embargo actually affects, so one embargo no longer stalls production planning for the whole world.
- **Automation, ageing, housing and education pressures in the 2027 world**
  In a new 2027 world, automated factories and services need fewer workers, ageing populations raise pension and health bills, regions track how many homes they have for their households, and education levels drift with education funding. Other start dates and existing worlds are unchanged.
- **Background countries in the 2027 world trade in the markets**
  The sixteen background countries of a new 2027 world now buy and sell commodities as whole economies, sized from 2027 population and GDP, and grow year by year. Other start dates and existing worlds are unchanged.
- **Background countries trade in the 2027 producer market**
  In a 2027 world the sixteen background countries now buy and sell in the same market as companies, households and governments, paying and being paid in their own currencies. Other start dates and existing worlds are unchanged.
- **Building logistics and construction is priced against the whole country**
  The extra build cost for companies that already supply a big share of a good now compares freight and construction against demand across the country, where they sell, instead of one state.
- **Climate change bites in the 2027 world**
  A 2027 world opens with climate pressure already building, and how fast the world cuts emissions now decides how bad it gets by 2050. Rising pressure hurts farms in hot, dry places, pushes up power use in hot regions, raises insurance costs and leaves more disaster damage on government budgets. Other start dates and the live world are unchanged, apart from five regions that can now flood.
- **Countries run by the AI government can now hit a sovereign debt crisis**
  Countries not open to players and run by AI governments were never counted as AI-governed on the newest autonomy settings, so their failed bond auctions were ignored and debt crises never started. They now count.
- **Fairer access to scarce production inputs**
  Companies share scarce inputs proportionally instead of losing supplies to older order IDs. Automatic buying limits leave room for price movement above the previous clearing price.
- **Faster banking turn maintenance**
  Banking reuses turn-wide configuration and capacity reads, and NPC bank maintenance loads country eligibility and existing banks together.
- **Faster market clearing with trade restrictions**
  Markets reuse supplier-route lookups and store repeated supplier lists once, reducing processing and receipt costs while retaining fair input allocation and embargo enforcement.
- **Free transfer pricing no longer shows internal supply charges**
  A company on Free transfer pricing books nothing between its own sites, so site pages stop showing internal supply charges and own-site transfer income.
- **Freight and own-site labels say what they measure**
  The "stuck" freight warning and the "to your sites" share on sector cards now say which goods they cover.
- **Keep corporate income through turn timeouts**
  A corporation phase that finishes after a timeout retains its completed income result. Incomplete corporate processing stops the turn before dependent work.
- **Long-run growth, interest rates and score bands now follow a path to 2050**
  From 2027 on, the United States, United Kingdom, Germany, Japan and China drift along an authored path for trend growth and neutral interest rates, and score bands are defined all the way to 2050 instead of freezing.
- **Market processing reliability and clearer health counts**
  Market clearing avoids repeated supplier and route work, corporations have a five-minute processing window, and health displays distinguish failed work from phases blocked by a failure.
- **New technology reaches countries at different speeds, and 10 and 30 year government bonds in the 2027 world**
  In a new 2027 world, each country gets new production methods a few years after they first appear, sooner where research, education, energy and trade are strong. Governments can also borrow for 10 and 30 years, and their debt managers borrow longer when interest rates are low. Other start dates and existing worlds are unchanged.
- **Opening a savings account no longer fails when its record went missing**
  A savings account whose setup was already recorded but whose account record was missing could not be opened and kept returning an error. It is now recreated on the next open.
- **Record why AI companies' construction loans are turned down**
  The turn now records why each AI company's construction loan request failed, so we can see what keeps new plants from being financed.
- **Sector table total now shows how it relates to the rows**
  When a company's sites are charged for supplies from its other sites, the Total row now says what the rows add up to, so the two figures no longer look like a wrong sum.
- **Tariffs and blockades count in the producer market**
