- If you are on a campaign in the race, as the candidate, a campaign manager or the running mate, the race page opens in Campaign mode. Everyone else opens in Race mode. The switch always overrides that, and the choice is in the web address, so you can share it or come back with the back button.
- Anyone can open a rival's campaign. You see exactly what you could see before: the full desk for your own ticket, more for your party, and the public view for everyone else.
- Clicking a ticket in the race page's tickets table, or a candidate in a primary field, opens that campaign in Campaign mode on the same page.
- Old campaign links still work. They now take you to the race page in Campaign mode, keeping the tab and state they pointed at. Russia, the Political Operations campaign tab and races that have not opened yet keep the old campaign page.
- Links to a campaign from the sidebar, wire items and the field office shortcut on the map go straight to the new place.

#### Developer notes

- `src/components/campaign/CampaignWorkspace.tsx` holds the campaign desk (data, access logic, panels, retarget and opposition reset). `/campaign/[id]` is now a server page that redirects through `resolveCampaignRedirect` and otherwise renders the workspace.
- Mode resolution (`?view=race|campaign`, `&campaign=<id>`), the redirect decision and the link helper (`campaignPageHref` in `src/lib/campaignManager/links.ts`) are pure and unit tested.
- The race payload gains `myCampaignId`: the viewer's own campaign in the race as candidate, manager or running mate, read by `findViewerRaceCampaignId`. No access rules or API authorization changed.

### The sector page says why your plants run where they do, and what would run more

#### What changed

- Above the run bar, a short report says how much of your capacity ran, the
  one reason the rest did not, and next turn's plan. A plant stepping back up
  says how many turns until a full run.
- A new line compares what each unit sold for with what it cost in materials,
  wages and site running, so you can see when you are losing money on every
  unit.
- A plant that sells everything but loses money on each unit is now marked
  "Cut back: losing money". It used to say "Ramping up" while its output kept
  falling. This includes sites that send their output to your own other sites.
- "What would run more" lists up to three fixes, largest first, such as adding
  cash, finding a missing input, or the price sitting below your cost.
- The run bar's legend is grouped into what was made, what was planned but
  stopped this turn, and what was not in the plan. "Short of cash" now reads
  "Stopped: not enough cash", and "Held by cash" reads "Kept low after a cash
  cut", so the two no longer sound like the same thing.

### AI companies pay their dividend after tax and stop going broke

#### What changed

- AI-run companies pay part of each turn's profit to households. That payout
  was 60% of profit before tax, and tax took another half or more, so a
  profitable company paid out more than it earned, ran out of cash and had to
  stop every plant it owned.
- Behind a new switch, off until it is turned on: the payout is 60% of profit
  after this turn's tax. It is never paid from savings, and none is paid while
  the company's cash is below zero.
- At the next step of the switch, an AI company with no savings above its
  reserve stops founding new businesses and adding capacity until it has some.
- Player companies are not affected.

### Internal supply is easier to find in the CEO Office

#### What changed

- The CEO Office section bar gains an Internal supply link next to Budget, so the setting no longer has to be scrolled to on a long phone page.

### Redesigned bill pages

#### What changed

- A new header shows the bill's stage, the time left to vote, and the Aye, No
  and Abstain counts for the chamber that is voting, along with your own vote.
- Co-sponsoring, filibustering and withdrawing sit next to the bill title.
- On desktop the page is much wider, and the seating chart sits beside the vote
  tally and voting buttons.
- Provisions have their own section. The floor debate comes right after the
  vote, and the full text is folded away until you open it.
- Co-sponsors are listed in the side column.
- The discussion panel is now called Floor debate. The writing box grows with
  your text, shows how many characters you have left, and posts with Ctrl+Enter.
- Phones keep a single column in the same reading order.

### Sector Selling tile matches sold and made units

#### What changed

- The Selling tile reads units sold divided by units made, so it can no longer show 100% beside a lower sold share.
- Output sent to your own sites is still shown separately and is not counted as a sale.

### Shareholders can see private corporations they own

#### What changed

- Holding shares in a private corporation, directly or through a corporation you run, now shows you its financials and Shares tab.
- Only the CEO can manage the company. Shareholders get a read-only view.

### A 2049 research tier, frontier programmes and retiring old methods

#### What changed

- A new research tier opens in 2049 and stays current from then on. Main nodes cost 75 R&D score, specializations 150 and capstones 225.
- Every sector has a full 2049 Corporate and Sector lane, including specializations and capstones.
- Five breakthrough production methods, each unlocked by a 2049 Sector node:
  - Energy: Commercial Fusion.
  - Extraction: Space Resources.
  - Healthcare: Gene Therapy at Scale.
  - Real estate: Autonomous Districts.
  - Technology: Neural Interfaces.
- A breakthrough also needs research across its whole sector. Every company in the sector together must have spent enough R&D score on research from the 2039 tier onward before anyone can build it, so a breakthrough can arrive early in the tier, late, or not at all. The strategy picker shows "awaiting a research breakthrough" until then.
- None of the breakthrough methods is free. Each uses existing goods and carries a heavy input bill.
- Frontier programmes: once a company owns all nine core 2049 technologies in its track, it can fund repeatable programmes from the Tech tab. Each adds 0.4% plant output or cuts plant inputs by 0.6% for the primary sector, costs 15% more than the last, and stays inside the existing output and input caps. Programme effects never expire.
- Retired methods:
  - Conventional power retires in 2039. Successors: Advanced Fission or Renewables.
  - Standard combustion vehicle manufacturing retires in 2049. Successors: EV Focus or Autonomous Vehicles.
  - A retired method is closed to new sites and to retools. New sites in that sector start on the first successor the company can run.
  - Sites already on a retired method keep running, but their inputs cost 1.5% more for every year since retirement, up to 15%.
  - Switching a retired site to one of its successors costs 60% less than a normal retool. AI-run companies convert their own sites automatically.
- Worlds before 2039 see no change. Retirement and breakthroughs apply only where tech trees are on.

### A new 2039 research tier with new production methods in twelve sectors

#### What changed

- A new research tier opens in 2039. Main nodes cost 60 R&D score, specializations 120 and capstones 180.
- The Corporate lane covers agent-run operations, automated compliance and supply networks that survive disruption.
- Every sector has a full 2039 Sector lane, including a specialization and capstones.
- New production methods, each unlocked by a 2039 Sector node:
  - Energy: Grid-Scale Storage Utility and Advanced Fission.
  - Chemicals: Direct Air Capture.
  - Healthcare: Biofoundry Biologics.
  - Manufacturing: Humanoid Robotics.
  - Technology: Agentic Services.
  - Agriculture: Cellular Agriculture.
  - Construction: Printed Mass Timber.
  - Defense: Drone Swarms.
  - Telecommunications: Orbital Constellations.
  - Logistics: Orbital Logistics.
  - Media and Entertainment: Personalized Media and Personalized Entertainment.
- None of the new methods is free. Each one uses existing goods and leans heavily on energy, electronics, rare earths, chemicals or software, so it pays off only where those inputs are available.
- Worlds that have not reached 2039 see the tier as locked. Nothing changes for companies before then.

### Choose which plants sell to outside buyers before your own plants

#### What changed

- Until now, every plant of yours took its own company's output before any outside buyer, so a supplier site could end a turn with nothing sold.
- The sector page now has a "Sell to outside buyers before my own plants" switch for the CEO. With it on, outside buyers are served first and your own plants get what they leave, free of charge.
- The switch is per sector and off by default, so nothing changes until you turn it on. It applies from the next turn.

### Former Eastern Bloc countries get banks once they have market economies

#### What changed

- Countries that were planned economies when a world began got no AI-run
  banks, and nothing created them after the country moved to a market
  economy. In a world that started in 1991, these countries reached 2005 with
  no banks, so companies there could never get a loan to build.
- Each turn, any market economy without its two AI-run banks now gets them.
  Banks are only ever created once.
- These countries now have a seat for AI companies in their capital region, so
  AI mining companies can also open there.
- Banks are licensed in the currency their own accounts use, which fixes bank
  creation in worlds where these countries use their modern currencies.

### Internal turn timing diagnostics

#### What changed

- Internal timing diagnostics were added to the turn market step so slow turns can be traced. Nothing about prices, sales or results changes.

### Modern forces, cyber resilience, new treaties and new media

#### What changed

- **Doctrine for the 2050s.** The doctrine tree now has a 2050s era with new doctrines for maneuver, fires, defence, logistics, cyber and surveillance. Generals can train new traits from the 2020s, 2030s and 2040s, such as counter-drone defence, drone network command and autonomous logistics.
- **New formations.** Counter-drone battalions and loitering munition wings from 2027, autonomous naval drone flotillas and space sensing constellations from 2030, and directed energy air defence wings from 2040. Each needs modern materiel in the national arsenal, which the country's own defence industry must research and deliver first. They also need trained personnel, money from the defence budget and ongoing upkeep.
- **Cyber resilience.** Every modelled country now has a cyber resilience score from 0 to 1. It rises with a stronger Cyber Resilience Mandate, with research spending and with the state of the utilities. A higher score makes infrastructure cyberattacks less likely.
- **Treaty organisations after 2027.** The Critical Minerals Club (2028), the Climate Adaptation Finance Pact (2029), the AI Safety Accord (2030), the Arctic Agreement (2032) and Space Traffic Rules (2034) open empty in their year and countries can join. Each has its own treaty terms that members vote in. The terms run for five years and then lapse unless the members renew them.
- **Media models.** Social platforms (the established model in 2027), creator networks, AI-generated media from 2030 and immersive media from 2040 join the media operating models, each with its own running costs and a research step that unlocks it.

### New crises and everyday events for the 2020s to 2040s

#### What changed

- **Sixteen new crises, each with a start and an end year.** Heat domes, megafire seasons, megadroughts, water rationing, grid emergencies, a rare severe solar storm, orbital debris outages, a housing crash when cheap mortgages reset, a pension funding cliff, a run on a tech lender, a climate insurance retreat, a chip plant fire, an AI model failure scandal, a port workers strike and a depot lockout over automation. Every one gives leaders a choice, a shared recovery fund and a real outcome. Themes that fade, such as the housing reset or the chip fire, stop appearing after their years. Every year from 2027 to 2050 has at least six to draw from.
- **Thirty new personal events**, ten each for the 2020s, 2030s and 2040s. They only appear in their own decade: AI assistants at work, robotaxis, lab-grown meat, insurers pulling out, fusion plants next door, Mars shares and more. Some are for officeholders or company chiefs only.
- **Six shared historic moments.** Everyone gets the same one-time event: the return to the Moon, a hundred years of the World Cup, the first commercial fusion attempt, departure for Mars, a hundred years since 1945, and the half-century mark in 2050.
- **New country stories for the UK, Germany, Japan and China.** Three each, set from 2027 onward, covering heat networks, balcony solar, empty houses, megaquake advisories, group-buy chats and electric car price wars.

### Opportunities for the 2027 world, and a limit on piled-up crises

#### What changed

- **Eight new opportunities.** A trade deal offer, a global minimum tax accord, a climate finance offer from the Climate Adaptation Finance Pact (from 2029), an invitation to join a shared science megaproject (from 2032), Olympic and World Cup hosting bids, a cultural breakout moment and a World Expo bid (from 2033). Every year from 2027 onward has at least four that can come up.
- **Every opportunity costs something.** Leaders choose between two ways of taking it up, each with its own price in growth, jobs, confidence or approval, and can then put money into a shared fund. The payoff only arrives if the country took the offer. Saying no, or not answering in time, costs nothing and gains nothing.
- **At most two serious crises per country at once.** Crises and long-running conflicts now count together. If a third would start while a country already has two, it waits and arrives once one of them ends, instead of piling on or vanishing. Opportunities never count toward the limit.

### Own trucks hauled figure on logistics rows

#### What changed

- Logistics rows show "Own trucks hauled N TEU" under the Sold reading, counting goods your own trucks carried out of that state last turn, for any buyer.
- It is separate from "to your sites", which counts only freight your own plants buy as input. Nothing about who gets freight space has changed.

### Three new world crises can break out after 2027

#### What changed

- **AI labour shock (from 2029).** Countries where automation is widespread and unemployment is rising can be hit by a wave of AI layoffs. Leaders choose a retraining law, wage insurance, a robot tax or letting the market adjust. Doing nothing raises unemployment, cuts wage growth, costs approval and feeds an anti-automation backlash. The wave ends absorbed, reformed or entrenched.
- **Pacific strait crisis (2028 to 2045).** When world tension is high, a confrontation between China and the United States over a Pacific strait can begin. It climbs from posturing to exclusion zones, a shipping quarantine and an armed standoff, and every step has a way out through talks. Deterrence, talks, trade concessions and chip sanctions are the levers. Shipping disruption cuts electronics output, which pushes prices up. The crisis never starts a war on its own; a war still needs a formal declaration.
- **Infrastructure cyberattacks (from 2028).** Higher world tension and weaker power and network systems make attacks more likely. An intrusion can be contained before it causes outages. Outages hurt the grid, output and approval until repair crews restore service.
- A country is never caught in more than two serious ongoing crises at once. A third waits until one of them ends.
- Every crisis ends even if nobody acts, then rests for about two years before it can return.

### Choose how much aid to pledge from the crisis page

#### What changed

- Aid pledges on the crisis response list show a size slider from a small share
  up to the crisis's cap, with the amount in your own currency beside it.
- The size you pick is what gets pledged. Leave it alone and the default share
  is used, as before.
- Pledges above the cap are still refused and the response is refunded.

### Crises board with a world map

#### What changed

- The Crises page leads with a world map. Each crisis is a marker sized by severity and coloured by type (war, disaster, economic, political, opportunity). Lines join crises that belong to the same chain of events or the same war. Selecting a marker opens that crisis.
- "Your exposure" lists the crises that touch your country, your state, the sectors of corporations you run or hold shares in, and your office. Each one says whether you can act now and when the decision closes.
- Below the map, crises are grouped by what needs doing: needs your response, escalating, ongoing, winding down and recent history. Each card shows severity, turns left, who can act and a direct link to respond.
- Conflicts now live on the Crises page under the Conflicts filter. The old Conflicts page sends you there, and the war detail pages are unchanged.
- The Crises entry in the World menu shows a count of the crises you can respond to right now.
- You get an inbox message when a crisis that affects you moves to a new stage or opens a new decision, linking to that crisis. You get at most one per crisis per turn.

### Events for the 2050s

#### What changed

- Eight new crises for 2050 to 2059: heat collapse, coastal storm surge flood, aquifer collapse, autonomous freight stall, automation layoff wave, engineered crop blight, synthetic media market panic and longevity pension strain. Each has a response choice, a funded recovery step and a real outcome. Some are rare random rolls and some only arm when the country is already under strain, such as high unemployment or a weak power grid. Nothing is guaranteed, and a country that is run well can go the whole decade without seeing them.
- Four new offers for the same years: an orbital solar partnership, a living ocean accord, a longevity science compact and an interplanetary supply consortium seat. Taking one up always has a price, declining is free.
- Ten new personal events for the 2050s, some for politicians, some for company owners, plus two worldwide moments: the first crewed landing on Mars and a shared pledge to restore the oceans.
- When no dated personal event fits a player, the game now falls back to the undated pool instead of skipping that player. If even that is empty, the turn records it so the gap can be fixed.

### Every country now charts population, GDP and GDP per capita over time

#### What changed

- The country economy page gains a "National history" panel with three series:
  total population, national GDP and GDP per capita. Every country has it,
  including economy-only nations that have no playable government.
- The country overview page shows the same series as a compact sparkline under
  the country description, so the trend is visible without opening the economy
  page. Both forms share one component and one request.
- History comes from the levels the world already records once per completed
  turn, so the hourly turn gains no extra work, and there are no schema or
  balance changes.
- The panel shows the last 120 turns and builds itself after the masthead, so
  first paint is unchanged.
- Turns without a recorded value stay absent rather than being drawn as zero,
  and a thin window shows a coverage message instead of a flat line.

### Four new long-running crises after 2027

#### What changed

- A second pandemic can start from 2031 in countries with thin public health systems and crowded cities. Early travel measures, hospital surge capacity or a rapid vaccine platform each change how it goes.
- A sovereign debt wave can start from 2028, but only when at least three heavily indebted, slowing economies are in trouble at the same time. A contagion firewall, emergency lending or a debt restructuring each soften it.
- A power grid cascade can start from 2030 in summer, where the network is already thin. Storage, demand response and new links between regions each help.
- A critical metals squeeze can start from 2028 when world tension is high and an economy leans on electronics and defence. Alliance supply deals, a stockpile release or recycling and new mines each help.
- Each crisis comes in waves with an onset, a peak and a recovery, always ends on its own even if nobody acts, and rests for two years before it can return. A country already under two severe crises is skipped.
- Nothing here is scheduled. A country with strong health, grid or finances never rolls for the matching crisis.

### GDP now grows with prices, so debt and revenue ratios stay honest

#### What changed

- Each country keeps a price index that starts at 1 and follows its inflation rate every turn. National and regional GDP are now current-price figures: real output times that index.
- Reported GDP growth is unchanged and stays the real annual rate. Capital, productivity and the output gap still run on real output.
- Over decades, debt-to-GDP, revenue-to-GDP and deficits no longer drift just because prices rose. Before, tax bases and debt grew with inflation while GDP did not.
- Every budget figure that divides by GDP (debt ratio, spending envelopes, deposit returns, the inflation fiscal term) now uses one GDP, refreshed and smoothed every turn instead of once a year. Spending envelopes sized on GDP will sit slightly lower than before because the smoothed figure trails the live one.

### Groundwork for measuring GDP growth by value added

#### What changed

- Nothing in play moves. Reported GDP growth, budgets and ratios are unchanged.
- Added a value-added measure (output minus the inputs a sector buys) and tests that compare it with the gross measure on a mixed economy.

### New read-only staff role for junior developers

#### What changed

- A new staff role, junior dev, can open every admin and moderator tool in read-only mode. Every save, grant, ban, seed, heal, reset and turn control is refused by the server, not just hidden.
- Only admins can grant or remove the moderator and junior dev roles, and each change is recorded in the admin log.
- Junior devs get none of the moderator gameplay overrides. Moderator powers are unchanged.
- Full resets keep junior dev accounts, the same as other staff.

### New worlds measure GDP growth by value added

#### What changed

- In a freshly created world, regional growth is measured on value added: output minus the inputs a sector buys. Industries that pass most of their output on to others no longer count that output twice.
- Growth in a single industry now moves the regional figure by what that industry adds, so a technology boom counts for more than the same lift in retail.
- A world that is already running keeps the old measure, so nothing shifts in play.

### One control panel for admins, moderators and junior devs

#### What changed

- The admin panel and the moderator panel are replaced by one control panel at `/control`. The menu groups tools by area (players, safety and access, content and community, support and reports, politics, world, economy, system, diagnostics) and only lists what your role can open.
- Every tool from both old panels is in the new one. Moderators keep exactly the tools they had; admins get all of them; junior devs see all of them read-only.
- A jump-to-tool search opens with Ctrl+K.
- Junior devs get a notice that their access is read-only, and changes are refused before they leave the browser as well as on the server.
- The Staff roles tool replaces the old Moderators tool. Admins use it to give and remove both the moderator and junior dev roles.
- The staff menu in the navbar now has a single Control Panel link.
- Old `/admin` and `/moderator` links, bookmarks and notification links still work: they redirect to the matching tool in the control panel.

### Respond to crises from the Crises page and Actions

#### What changed

- Crisis cards on the Crises page and the Actions page show where the crisis is (early warning, onset, peak, aftermath), its severity with a small history line, and a projection of how bad it gets if nobody acts.
- If your office or company can respond, the card lists each response with its real cost (treasury money, company cash, approval, actions) and how long before you can use it again. Pulling one asks you to confirm first, and any refusal is shown on the card.
- Legislature responses raise an emergency bill instead of acting directly.
- The crisis page has a "Who did what" log: for each turn, which office holder used which response, or took no action, and how severe the crisis was then.
- Crises that leave lasting damage list it, with how long it takes to heal. Nothing is shown when there is none.
- The count next to Crises in the World menu now includes crises where you have a response available.
- Crises that have not moved to the new engine look and work as before.

### A fusion breakthrough, election integrity crises and a longer calendar

#### What changed

- A fusion breakthrough can happen from 2042 in research-heavy countries with a working energy framework. It is good news: fast-track licensing, demonstration funding or a grid build-out each bring cheaper power sooner, and doing nothing still helps, only less.
- An election integrity crisis can start from 2028 where trust in the count is already weak. Platform rules, independent audits or civic education each change how far it goes.
- Both come in waves, end on their own, and rest for two years before they can return. Neither is scheduled. Fusion does not count as a severe crisis, so it never blocks real crises from being tracked.
- The year-by-year content check now covers 2027 to 2059, and every year has living crises, events and offers.

### Background countries now post briefs, and restructure when their debt spirals

#### What changed

- A background country posts a brief when its credit rating changes, its debt
  interest spiral worsens, it holds an election, announces an austerity package,
  its currency moves 8% or more over a day, or restructures its debt. A quiet
  country still posts an outlook roughly every four days, staggered so they do
  not land together.
- Each brief goes to the wire ticker, the news feed and the country page, with
  buttons for its bonds, its national index fund, its currency market and world
  trade. The first look after the update records the current state without
  posting, so there is no backlog.
- The background country page gains a Public finances panel: the interest spiral
  count against the two years that trigger a restructuring, how much programme
  spending is left after cuts, tax rises so far and the latest austerity package.

### Choose your stance in the second pandemic and the AI labour shock

#### What changed

- During a second pandemic you can order a lockdown or keep the economy open. A lockdown protects public health but costs jobs, strains lenders and lifts borrowing costs. Staying open protects jobs for now, lets more people fall ill, and hurts the government at the next election.
- Finance ministers can issue emergency health bonds, and central bank chairs can backstop lenders and bond markets.
- During an AI labour shock you can bail out the hardest-hit sectors (jobs held, bond yields up) or push structural reform (jobs worse first, voters angry, better footing later). Finance ministers can fund a wage subsidy and central bank chairs can allow lender forbearance.
- Each choice shows its tradeoff on the crisis screen, spends action points and treasury or political capital like any other crisis response, and lands in the crisis record that decides blame afterwards. The effects hit your own country only.

### Climate migration, the Arctic, resistant infections and orbital debris

#### What changed

- A climate migration surge can start from 2035 in countries already seeing strong inflows when climate pressure is high. An orderly border policy, reception funding or integration funding each change how it goes.
- An Arctic opening can start from 2033 for countries with northern coasts, when climate pressure and world tension are both rising. A northern treaty, joint extraction concessions or a rescue fleet each help.
- Antimicrobial resistance can start from 2036 where public health systems are weak. A drug stewardship law, hospital infection control or new antibiotic research each help.
- An orbital debris cascade can start from 2040 in research-heavy economies, more likely when world tension is high. A space traffic law, backup ground networks or debris removal each help.
- Each crisis comes in waves, ends on its own even if nobody acts, and rests for two years before it can return. Nothing here is scheduled, and strong countries never roll for the matching crisis.

### Frontier programme races for the 2040s

#### What changed

- Three programmes open in the 2040s: fusion power (2041), orbital industry (2043) and biotech platforms (2046). A new public page, Frontier programme races, shows each one with a live leaderboard.
- A head of state or finance minister can fund a programme from the treasury for their nation, paid in the nation's own currency, and the national budget page links to the races. A corporation CEO can fund one from research points.
- The first entrant to reach a programme's threshold wins it. A winning nation gets an approval boost at home. A winning corporation gets a permanent plant output bonus of about 10% in its primary sector.
- Rivals that funded at least a quarter of the threshold lose a little approval at home. After a sunset period of 48 turns they receive a licence: nations regain a little approval (less than they lost), corporations get a half strength version of the method and 40% of their research points back.
- The winner is announced on the news wire, and the corporation tech tab links to the leaderboard.

### Leaving office now leaves a legacy that colours the next election

#### What changed

- When the head of government changes (term end, succession, resignation or
  removal), the departed leader gets a legacy score from -100 to 100. Approval at
  exit counts for 40%, crisis credit and blame for 30%, laws enacted for 20% and
  crisis scars for 10%. Terms under 24 turns count proportionally less.
- The legacy appears on the country executive page and on the leader's party
  page, with the numbers behind it and how much of its effect is left.
- It gives the leader's party a shift of up to 2 percent of its vote and a shift of
  up to 5 points in the approval that party renewal sees for a governing party.
  Both halve every 96 turns and stop after 288, and the election shift is bounded
  together with crisis credit and blame at 3 percent of the party's vote.
- A line goes to the news feed when a leader with a real term leaves.

### Long-running crises now spill into the wider world

#### What changed

- Each of the ten new long-running crises can now seed related local emergencies as it worsens, such as a freight stall after a pandemic, a lender run during a debt wave, or satellite outages after an orbital debris cascade.
- Orbital debris can only start in countries where companies run orbital constellations, and more of them raises the chance.
- Election integrity trouble is more likely where companies run personalised media feeds.
- A country already in its own sovereign default crisis is no longer hit by the debt wave on top of it.

### Operators can switch off three economy and politics systems

#### What changed

- Nominal GDP that follows inflation can be switched off. Off, prices stay at their base level and GDP is not inflated each turn.
- The austerity packages and debt restructurings of AI-run countries can be switched off. Off, no package is enacted and no restructuring fires.
- Crises that start on their own can be switched off. Off, no new crises open from economic conditions, while crises already running continue and resolve as normal.
- New worlds start with all three on. The current world keeps them off until staff turn them on, so nothing shifts in play today.

### Passed Japanese laws now move regional political lean

#### What changed

- Once a Japanese law is in force, the groups it touches (for example
  older voters for health laws, rural voters for farm laws) drift left or
  right in step with the option that passed. Economic laws move economic
  lean; social and security laws move social lean.
- The drift is gradual and capped, and it fades back toward the starting
  position if the law is repealed. National laws are spread across all
  regions; regional laws act on their own region.
- Option costs, scores and budgets are unchanged.

### The 2030s get a real fight over who pays for automation

#### What changed

- Two new laws open for filing at the start of the 2030 band: a retraining
  guarantee and a hiring subsidy. Two more open in the 2035 band: an automation
  pension levy and transition compacts with unions. All four come in five
  rungs, from no statute to a heavy version, for the US and the UK.
- Crew: automated plants no longer shed their full crew once the automation
  tax, the hiring subsidy or transition compacts are in force. The strongest
  version of each keeps a fifth to a quarter of the crew that would have gone,
  and no stack of laws keeps more than 45%. Fewer workers are displaced, and
  those plants pay more in wages.
- Costs: the automation tax and the pension levy raise the input bill of the
  four automation production methods, by up to 7% and 3% (10% together). The
  hiring subsidy cuts the wage bill of every firm in the country, by up to 5%.
- Jobs: retraining and the hiring subsidy speed up how fast displaced workers
  find new work. Wage insurance in the US and UK now does the same, which it
  did not before (only the other countries' version was read).
- Unions: transition compacts add up to 14 points to the union law bias that
  drives how many workers join unions.
- Pensions: the automation pension levy books a saving on the pension line of
  up to 0.28% of output at its top rung.
- Voters: enacting any of the six laws also moves the voter groups it helps or
  taxes (workers without a degree and low-wealth voters for the levies and
  subsidies, older voters for the pension levy, high-wealth voters pay), on
  top of the usual left and right swing. One enactment never moves a group by
  more than 6 points from these laws.
- Costs to the budget are small: the new laws cost between 0.005% and 0.45% of
  output at their top rungs, and the pension levy is a saving.

### Automation levies now fund the treasury

#### What changed

- Companies running automated methods now pay the automation tax and pension levy as a share of what they spend on inputs each turn.
- The money goes to the national treasury of the country that passed the law, and shows up in its revenue.
- Suppliers are no longer paid more because of the levy; the cost lands on the automating company's cash.
- A company that cannot cover the full levy pays what it has, up to its cash on hand.

### Automation levy is tracked on its own

#### What changed

- Automation levy money is recorded as its own kind of treasury income instead of being mixed in with tariff revenue.
- The country budget page shows the yearly automation levy when a country collects one.
- The levy is shown for information. The cash reaches the treasury when it is collected, so it is not added to the revenue total a second time.
- Worlds that still use the older sector model no longer make inputs dearer for the levy. That model has no input purchases to take the levy from, so the law has no effect there.

### Canada's data foundation lands for the 2027 world

Canada is being added to the 2027 world as a full country. This first step adds
the groundwork: the 13 provinces and territories, the 343 House of Commons seats
split between them, the 105 appointed Senate seats, opening party strengths for
five invented parties, and the starting economy, currency and central bank.

Nothing changes in running worlds, and Canada is not selectable by players yet.
Later steps add elections and government formation, laws and cabinet, the
economy hooks, Quebec's sovereignty politics, and the country page and map.

### Clearer naval and air supply and agreed friendly bases

Naval and air supply changes now save even when a formation is undamaged. Aircraft no longer lose supply to fleet berth crowding, and Airlift supports nearby regions. Fleets can draw on nearby home ports without being stationed on land, and sea and air routes cross the Pacific.

Defense holders can offer naval or air basing access for another nation to accept. Either side can revoke it, and wartime enemies cannot use it. Regional port networks support three berth units per port-rating point; host and guest navies share their physical capacity. The command page explains support bases, berth limits and estimated next-turn supply, with a color-coded comparison of stations.

### Countries can launch cyber operations against each other

#### What changed

- The head of intelligence can launch a cyber operation against another country from the intelligence console routes. It costs money from the intelligence appropriation, like other covert action.
- Success depends on your cyber strength and your service's tradecraft against the target's cyber resilience, which comes from its resilience mandate, research spending and utilities. A better defended target is harder to hit.
- A successful operation causes a Cyber Attack crisis on the target: grid outages, lost output and growth, and a response decision for its head of state. A failed one does nothing to the target.
- Separately, the target may trace the operation to its sponsor, whether it worked or not. When it does, world tension rises, the news names the sponsor, and the sponsor's government loses approval for a few turns.
- Operations between the same two countries need a long gap between them.
- Either government can publicly stand down. That opens a truce that blocks operations between the pair in both directions for a long stretch, and eases world tension.
- In a 2027 world, hawkish background governments can probe the leader of the rival bloc when the odds favour them, and stand down when they turn dovish or the world cools.
- Other start dates are unchanged.

### Crises now have consequences: blame, scars and forced events

#### What changed

- Recessions, banking panics, debt crises, regional job shocks, energy shortages and waves of unrest now open by themselves when the conditions behind them hold for several turns in a row. The same kind of crisis cannot reopen in the same place for 24 turns after it ends.
- A crisis response now spends the holder's own actions for the turn instead of a separate currency, so responding competes with campaigning, legislating and running a business. Bigger responses cost up to three actions. A failed response gives the actions back.
- Blame follows exposure. A governor answers only for a regional crisis in their own state, a CEO only if the company operates in an affected sector and region, and party leaders and the legislature only for national crises.
- When a crisis moves into its aftermath, every office holder is judged on what they did. Acting against a serious crisis earns approval, and sitting on available responses while it was severe costs approval. Heads of state move national approval and governors move their state's approval.
- The same verdict moves the holder's party in elections where the crisis hit, by up to three percent of vote share, fading after 48 turns.
- Severe crises leave scars that heal slowly over time: lost regional output, damaged plant capacity, a loss of trust in institutions, people leaving a region, or a weaker credit standing.
- A crisis that stays severe long enough forces events: workers strike, depositors run on banks, lenders push a country toward default, the legislature calls for accountability, or a state of emergency is declared. A state of emergency unlocks stronger responses and slowly drains government approval while it lasts.

### Crisis stance choices: fewer side effects, clearer text

#### What changed

- Stance choices (lockdown, stay open, bailouts, reform and the rest) no longer say they cut severity, because they never did. The line is hidden for them.
- Small pushes on bank stress now build up. A bank only enters a panic once the stress adds up to a full turn, so a single stance choice no longer panics every bank. Lender forbearance no longer adds bank stress at all.
- Governments run by AI take at most one stance per crisis, picked by how interventionist the government is. They no longer repeat the same stance every cooldown.
- Crisis response costs paid from the treasury are now converted to the country's own currency instead of being charged as raw anchor-currency units.
- A stance is marked as used before its effects are applied, so an interrupted turn cannot apply the same package twice.

### Cyber operations get a console and AI governments join in

#### What changed

- The intelligence office has a Cyber operations panel for the person who directs the service. Pick any country, see the chance the operation gets through and the chance you are named, see the cost, and launch.
- The panel lists active truces and recent operations by and against your country. A sponsor is named only when the target traced the operation. You can stand down from any country to open a truce.
- Full countries run by the game (such as the UK, Germany, Japan and China) now probe the rival bloc leader when their leader is hawkish and the odds favour them, and stand down when the leader is dovish or tension falls. Each government is limited to two operations in a long stretch, with a gap between them, and pays from its intelligence appropriation.
- A country with a player directing its intelligence service is never run by the game for cyber operations.

### Every sector gets a new production method in the 2040s

#### What changed

- New methods: Agent Asset Management (finance), Agentic Storefronts (retail), Solid-State Vehicles (vehicle makers), Agentic Property Operations (real estate) and Autonomous Deep Mining (extraction).
- Each is unlocked by the first research node of its sector in the 2039 tier and needs its own inputs.
- Every sector now has research to do and a recent new method in 2045 and in 2051. Countries with less ready industry still adopt each method years after the leaders.

### Seizing a foreign-owned company now raises world tension

#### What changed

- Taking a foreign-owned corporation or sector now raises world cold-war
  tension, once per taking. The tension ledger names both countries, so the
  incident is visible to everyone rather than only to the government that
  ordered it.
- The size follows what was paid. A fair-value buyout adds a little, a
  discounted taking more, and a seizure with no compensation the most. Taking a
  whole sector adds the sum of its individual takings.
- Domestic takings are unchanged, and no eligibility, compensation, or approval
  numbers moved in this change.

### Treaties change who trades with whom

#### What changed

- While the Critical Minerals Club has terms in force, its members send more of their rare earth, iron and coal surplus to each other, and the goods arrive a little cheaper than from outside countries.
- While the Arctic Agreement has terms in force, its members do the same for oil, natural gas and freight.
- When the terms lapse and are not renewed, trade between members goes back to normal.
- Trade with countries outside the treaty is not changed, so a buyer outside the club gets a smaller share of a member's surplus.
- A sanction between two countries still closes trade between them, and the exporter's surplus goes to other buyers. This is now checked by tests.

### Canada and an independent Quebec get their cabinet pages

#### What changed

- Canada's cabinet page lists the federal ministers, from the Deputy Prime Minister to the Government House Leader, and lets the Prime Minister appoint and dismiss them.
- If Quebec becomes independent, the Premier gets the same page for the Executive Council of Quebec.

### Canada and an independent Quebec get their parliament pages

#### What changed

- Canada's legislature page shows the House of Commons: its members, bills, leadership, tariffs and subsidies. Seated MPs can propose bills from it, and the Prime Minister can call a snap election.
- If Quebec becomes independent, its National Assembly gets the same page, led by the Premier, with MNAs proposing bills.
- The seat count in the page header is the live size of the chamber, so Canada's Commons shows 265 seats after Quebec leaves.
- Independent Scotland and Wales use the same page as before. Its leadership note now says the First Minister leads the government rather than a devolved government.

### Canada and an independent Quebec track national figures

#### What changed

- Each turn, Canada's regional figures are rolled up into national ones, such as GDP growth, as they are for the other player countries. An independent Quebec gets the same after it secedes.
- A computer-run Bank of Canada chair now sets rates from Canada's measured growth instead of assuming the economy is on target.
- Computer-run governments in Canada and Quebec can now see how their economy is doing when they choose what to do.

### Canada has a province map

#### What changed

- The Canada map page draws all thirteen provinces and territories instead of the economy-only placeholder.
- Map views: party organization, Commons seats by party, Premier by party, government approval, political lean, sector specialization and logistics.
- Clicking a province opens it, like on the other country maps.

### Canada opens to players in new 2027 worlds

#### What changed

- A new 2027 world now starts with Canada open to players. You can create a character there, run for the House of Commons, found corporations and trade.
- Canada's 13 provinces and territories elect 343 MPs. Premiers' offices start vacant and fill at the first provincial elections.
- The Canadian dollar trades on the currency exchange.
- Canada appears on the world map and has its own seal on the Prime Minister's office.
- Existing worlds do not change.

### Canada's bills move through Parliament

#### What changed

- A Canadian bill is decided by one House of Commons vote. If it passes, it receives Royal Assent and becomes law. If it fails, it dies.
- The appointed Senate does not vote on player bills.
- Canada still cannot be joined. This only prepares Parliament for when it opens.

### Canada's founding seats are checked against its parties

#### What changed

- Nothing changes in play. The check that every founding seat names a real party now covers Canada as well as the other countries.

### Canada's government pages show Parliament Hill

#### What changed

- The Office of the Prime Minister banner shows Parliament Hill in Ottawa.
- The Bank of Canada page shows the bank's head office in Ottawa.
- Canada still cannot be joined.

### Canada's House of Commons election schedule is built for the 2027 world

Canada's elections now run on a fixed four-year term, with the first regular
House of Commons election due in 2029 in the 2027 world. Each province and
territory elects its own share of the 343 seats, and a snap election restarts
the four-year clock. Premiers are elected on the shared four-year provincial
cycle.

Canada is still switched off for players and running worlds. Government
formation, laws and the cabinet follow in later steps.

### Canada's House of Commons opens with its 2025 members

#### What changed

- Canada's 343 Commons seats start filled, province by province, matching the April 2025 federal result: 169 for the governing party, 144 for the opposition, 22 for the Quebec party, 7 for the left party and 1 Green.
- That gives the opening government a working Commons from the first turn instead of an empty chamber.
- Canada still cannot be joined, and live worlds are not changed.

### Canada's laws and cabinet

#### What changed

- Canada has its own law book: six tax laws plus the same program laws as the other countries, written for Canada (health transfers, pensions, supply management, child benefits and more).
- Canada's opening tax rates are set from combined federal and provincial rates.
- A 19-seat cabinet with departments, metric panels and two ministerial orders per seat.
- Merger review for Canada is handled by the Minister of Industry.
- Canada still cannot be joined. This only prepares the rules for when it opens.

### Canada's national budget

#### What changed

- Canada's national budget covers federal and provincial government together, so it lines up with the combined tax rates in its law book.
- Revenue opens at about 40% of the economy and spending at about 42%, with defence at 2%.
- When a world opens with Canada, its law book is fitted to this spending mix and sized to what the government collects, the same way the United States and Britain open in 1991.
- Canada still cannot be joined. This only prepares the budget for when it opens.

### Canada's opening political map is ready

#### What changed

- Canada's 13 provinces and territories, its five parties and its national budget are set up when a 2027 world is created, once Canada is switched on.
- Each party starts with a local organization that matches how it did in that province at the April 2025 election. The Quebec party starts strong in Quebec and from scratch everywhere else.
- Canadian voters are grouped into nine blocs, such as suburban families, Prairie conservatives, Quebec francophones and new Canadians, each with its own turnout and leanings.
- The 343-seat House of Commons opens with a government still to be formed. A party needs 172 seats for a majority.
- Canada still cannot be joined. Live worlds are not changed.

### Canada's Parliament shrinks when Quebec leaves

#### What changed

- When Quebec secedes, its 78 MPs leave the House of Commons and the chamber is counted as 265 seats. A government now needs 133 seats for a majority instead of the old 172.
- The Senate is counted province by province, so Quebec's 24 senators leave with it and the Senate shows 81 seats.
- The United Kingdom works as before: the Commons still shrinks when Scotland or Wales leaves, and the House of Lords is unchanged.

### Canada's provincial legislatures hold elections

#### What changed

- All 13 Canadian provinces and territories elect a provincial or territorial legislature, sized to its real seat count.
- Each legislature votes on its own four-year schedule, starting from its next fixed election date: Alberta, Manitoba, Prince Edward Island and the Northwest Territories in 2027, British Columbia, Saskatchewan, Nova Scotia and New Brunswick in 2028, Ontario, Newfoundland and Labrador, Yukon and Nunavut in 2029, and Quebec in 2030.
- Seats are shared out by each party's vote in the province, the same way Canadian Commons seats are.
- The legislatures start empty in a new 2027 world and fill at their first election.
- The Northwest Territories and Nunavut use party contests in the game, although their real legislatures run without parties.

### Canadian characters get their own events

#### What changed

- Smoke Over the Valley: help at a clean-air shelter, buy air purifiers for seniors, or wait for the wind to change.
- The Trucking Permit (politicians only): campaign against trade barriers between provinces, help a brewery with its forms, or buy a stake in it.
- The Northern Housing Visit: visit a remote community, fund building materials, or decline.
- The Old Arena: back a new rink, patch the roof, or invest with a developer.
- These events only reach characters in Canada.

### Canadian Commons seats are refilled by by-elections

#### What changed

- A Canadian House of Commons seat that falls vacant mid-term opens a Commons By-Election in that province, contesting only the empty seats. The vote is weighted by the share of the province's seats that are empty.
- Sitting MPs keep their seats while the by-election runs, and an MP already holding a seat in the province cannot stand in it.
- If nobody files, a candidate from the seat's previous party is entered so the seat is not left empty.
- No by-election is held when the province's general election would fill the seat first.
- Canada has no recall petitions, so Canadian MPs cannot be recalled.
- A snap election in Canada now elects every seat in each province, the same way a regular Canadian general election does.

### Four more new systems can be switched off on a running world

#### What changed

- New crises can run on the old crisis rules. Off, a new crisis has no stages, blame, scars or forced events, and wars are not shown as crises.
- The rules that fill empty presidencies and prime ministerships can be switched off. Off, only the United States Vice President steps up when the presidency is empty, as before.
- Leader legacies can be switched off. Off, no legacy is recorded when a leader leaves office.
- Campaign income and costs can stay measured against each era's fixed figure instead of the live economy. Off, campaign money is priced as before.
- New worlds start with all four on. The current world keeps them off until staff turn them on, so nothing shifts in play when the update arrives.

### Independence no longer assumes the United Kingdom

#### What changed

- When Scotland or Wales leaves the United Kingdom, nothing changes for players. The same steps run in the same order.
- Each region that can become independent now records which country it becomes. The handover of land, money, parties, officials and elections reads that record instead of assuming a British region.
- Quebec still cannot hold a sovereignty referendum. That opens when the Quebec successor country is ready.

### Independence speaks for each country it leaves

#### What changed

- An independence consent bill is now sponsored by the departing country's own government and names that country, rather than always saying the United Kingdom.
- Game event posts about independence name the right capital: Westminster for Scotland and Wales, Ottawa for Quebec.
- The handover that turns a region into a new country can now split Quebec into Montreal, Laval, Laurentides and Lanaudiere, Monteregie, the Quebec City region, Estrie and Centre-du-Quebec, the Outaouais and Abitibi, Mauricie and Saguenay, and Eastern and Northern Quebec.
- Quebec still cannot call a sovereignty referendum. That opens once an independent Quebec can hold its own elections and pass its own laws.

### Quebec gets a sovereignty mood

#### What changed

- Quebec opens the 2027 world with sovereignty support at 35 out of 100, in line with recent polling.
- The mood moves each turn with the Premier's stance on sovereignty, how popular the provincial and federal governments are, and inflation.
- Referendum requests, Prime Minister decisions and the stance rules now work for any country with such a region, not only the United Kingdom. Nothing changes for UK players.
- Quebec cannot hold a referendum yet. That opens once an independent Quebec can actually be formed.
- Canada still cannot be joined.

### Quebec is ready to exist as its own country

#### What changed

- An independent Quebec now has its own country record: a 125-seat National Assembly, a Premier, eight regions from Montreal to Eastern and Northern Quebec, a cabinet, and the Canadian dollar.
- Its voters keep the same groups and leanings as Quebec voters in Canada, so elections after independence pick up where the province left off.
- Quebec does not appear in any world and cannot be joined. A sovereignty referendum still cannot be called; that opens once independence is wired to this country.

### Quebec's Premier gets a Sovereignty tab

#### What changed

- The Premier of Quebec's office has a Sovereignty tab. It shows the current mood, what is moving it and a forecast, using Quebec's own terms: Federalist, Autonomist and Pro-Sovereignty.
- The Premier can change their stance for office action points, with the same cooldown as the UK First Ministers.
- In Quebec elections, a high sovereignty mood moves a small share of votes toward the Laurentian Alliance and away from its two main federalist rivals. A low mood does the reverse.
- A Pro-Sovereignty Premier whose party leads a high mood gets the same stepped election bonus that UK First Ministers get.
- The referendum request button explains that a vote cannot be held until an independent Quebec can be formed.
- Canada still cannot be joined.

### The Canadian dollar gets an exchange rate

#### What changed

- The Canadian dollar starts 2027 worlds at 1.37 per US dollar, the 2024 average.
- The Canadian dollar now belongs to Canada instead of being treated as a stand-in for the US dollar.
- Admin readiness checks now know what a healthy Canada looks like: 13 provinces and territories, five parties and a 343-seat House of Commons.
- Canada still cannot be joined, and currency trading is unchanged in live worlds.

### The referendum feed names a newly independent country

#### What changed

- The referendum feed entry for a completed independence vote uses the new country's name, such as Quebec, Scotland or Wales, instead of a short code like QC or SCO.

### The wiki explains what Canada loses when Quebec leaves

#### What changed

- The Canada page and the Referendums page explain that after Quebec secedes the House of Commons has 265 seats with 133 needed for a majority, and the Senate keeps 81 senators.
- Both pages say Quebec takes the share of Canada's federal debt and treasury cash that matches its share of Canada's GDP.

### The wiki has a Canada page

#### What changed

- The wiki's Countries section has a Canada page: seats by province, how a Prime Minister is chosen, how laws pass, the Premiers, Quebec's sovereignty mood and the five parties.

### The 2027 world gets its own landing page

#### What changed

- The 2027 landing page has its own headline text and era tags: automation, climate pressure, cyber operations, treaty trade, ageing populations and the Quebec question.
- A news ticker runs along the globe from January 2027 to 2050, with each headline filed from a place on the map.
- The idle globe tour now visits the years ahead: Canada opening to players, the Quebec referendum, cyber operations, the AI labour shock, treaty trade, the research tiers and the shared moments such as the return to the Moon.
- The country list shows the real 2027 world: the United States and Canada are open to players, the United Kingdom, Germany, Japan and China run on their own, and sixteen more countries are marked as background nations.
- The globe colours those countries the same way, instead of showing the 2019 roster.
- Other start dates are unchanged.

### Unpaid income tax is collected from your other currency wallets

#### What changed

- Income-tax settlement still pays the assessed wallet first. When that wallet
  is short, the remainder is bought from the payer's other personal currency
  wallets at the live market rate, in the same rate direction the assessment
  itself uses.
- Converted debits are charged in whole cents or yen, and the tiny rounding
  excess is retired rather than left owing forever.
- Savings wallets are never touched. A payer with no money in any personal
  wallet still keeps the debt.

### Government budgets can collect taxes and pay debts in real money

#### What changed

- A government can collect a share of its book taxes, sized to the real
  economy, from households into its treasury, never more than a tenth of
  household cash in one turn.
- Public purchases can be paid from treasury cash instead of created fresh,
  and unspent money goes back to the treasury.
- Bond interest owed to players is paid at full value and before anyone
  else. Interest owed to the market and to funds is paid at the scale of the
  real economy, oldest first, so long unpaid interest starts clearing.
- Countries without a real economy of their own use the middle value of the
  other countries' scales for their debts.
- Clearing old unpaid interest is limited per turn so the hourly turn stays
  fast, and interest owed to companies is paid in one combined payment.
- When a government bond matures, the market's share is repaid at the same
  scale the market paid for it, so the bond market neither drains nor grows
  from government refinancing. Players are still repaid in full.
- Household taxes are collected in one batch per country, which keeps the
  hourly turn fast.

### Short inputs say why your own output did not arrive

#### What changed

- The company commodity table nets output against use across every site, so it
  could show a surplus of an input that one of your plants was short of. The
  sector page's "What goes in, what comes out" panel now explains the gap for
  the CEO, under any short input your other sites made last turn.
- It names the regions that made the input and gives each reason that applied:
  your supply policy sells to the market first; the input is made only in other
  regions and none was hauled to this one; a supply agreement covers part of
  this demand, which your own plants do not fill; or some of your own output
  arrived but not enough.
- No game rules changed. This only reports what last turn's records show.

### AI mines grow on the prices of what they actually dig

#### What changed

- AI-run mines used to judge expansion against the whole raw-materials basket
  (iron, coal, oil, rare earth, gas and timber), whatever they produced. A
  shortage of coal or rare earth therefore counted as a reason to build more
  oil and gas wells, even while crude oil was selling at its lowest allowed
  price and unsold stock piled up.
- A mine running a focused method (oil and gas, coal, iron, timber, rare
  earth) now reads only the prices of what that method produces when it
  decides to grow, how much worn capacity to replace, and whether a closed
  mine should reopen. Mines on the standard mixed method, and every other
  industry, are unchanged.
- Mines that players manage by hand are not affected; mines left on autopilot
  follow the same rule. Existing capacity is not removed; it stops growing
  into markets that do not need it.

### Ask drawer research spinner is a ring again

#### What changed

- The spinner around the research step card is now a circular ring that fits the card, so it no longer looks like a broken, tilted card while Ask works.

### Ask Keir research row is one tidy line

#### What changed

- The research card no longer has a second "1 step" line. The step count sits on the right as "Show 3", which opens the full list.

### Committee proposals show how many yes votes they need

#### What changed

- Each committee proposal card now shows the number of yes votes needed to pass.
- The card also reminds you that seats which have not voted when the window closes count as no.
- No game rules changed.

### Company contests fill in after an economy reset

#### What changed

- The company growth and comeback contests take each company's starting value
  from its recent history. Right after an economy reset there is none, so these
  contests opened with nobody in them and stayed empty all week, with no prize.
- A contest that opens empty now enters companies on the first turn their
  history exists, and growth counts from that turn.
- No game rules changed.

### Country commodity markets show what buyers really ordered

#### What changed

- A country's commodity market now shows the orders placed on last turn's
  market: supply is what its producers offered and demand is what its buyers
  ordered, government, military and investment buyers included. Before, those
  buyers were missing from country demand, so goods such as ordnance and
  building materials could read as a deep surplus while they were short.
- The commodity page says when a country's figures come from last turn's
  orders and shows how much its buyers actually received.
- The map's country and reachable views, the wiki's country breakdown, the
  country economy outlook and the public API read the same orders.
- World totals do not change: they already counted every order.
- No game rules or prices changed. Countries fall back to the old figures on
  a turn with no market orders.

### Deleted fund holders no longer block rebalancing

A deleted holder no longer leaves an already-burned withdrawal blocking its fund forever. The claim is cancelled with a warning; fund cash and unit supply stay unchanged. Legacy claims with unburned units remain queued for reconciliation. An unfinished settlement keeps ownership of its claim.

### Fewer false alt-account matches for moderators

#### What changed

- Many unrelated players on the same phone model and browser share one browser
  fingerprint. The duplicate-account panel and the suspicious-players list no
  longer treat a fingerprint shared by more than eight accounts as a link. The
  alt-detection engine already ignored them.
- Two accounts with the same browser and graphics card but different
  fingerprints were flagged as a medium device match. That is now a low lead to
  check against other evidence.
- No game rules changed.

### Index funds no longer get stuck on holders that no longer exist

#### What changed

- If a fund holder (an NPP, character or pension scheme) no longer exists, its share of the wind-up goes to the country's central bank reserve instead of blocking the whole wind-up.
- Every other holder is paid and the fund delists as normal.
- A queued withdrawal whose holder no longer exists is closed instead of being retried every turn, which used to stop the fund rebalancing.

### Local services say which states are short

#### What changed

- Healthcare, real estate, construction and freight are sold only to buyers in
  the state where they are produced. Their world supply and demand add every
  state together, so a good could read deeply short worldwide while the state
  you built in was already fully supplied, and the new plant found few buyers.
- On the market's commodities list, the Balance column for these four now reads
  "Short in N of M states", or "Supplied in every state", with a note that they
  sell only inside each state. Other goods keep their world balance.
- The new plant and build capacity screens already measure these goods against
  the plant's own state; this brings the market list in line with them.
- No game rules changed.

### Mothball a sector from its row

#### What changed

- The three dot menu on each row of a corporation's sector list now offers
  Mothball next to Details, Build capacity and Abandon. On a mothballed sector it
  offers Reactivate instead (suggestion 412).
- The phone layout of the row gets the same button.
- It uses the same action as the sector page, so the rules are unchanged: CEO
  only, plants tier only, and mothballed plants make nothing and still cost
  upkeep.

### National demand can count government and investment orders

#### What changed

- Government and investment buyers order from national accounts that have no state. Their orders reached the world total but never a country's national demand.
- With `producerNationalPublicDemandStage` at 1, those orders count in their own country's national supply and demand. State figures and the world total are unchanged.
- Everything that reads national demand then sees them: the national price, the national part of the sector margin, latent shortage and where NPPs found plants for goods that trade across borders (defence, manufacturing, technology).
- On the latest turn the biggest changes are ordnance (United States 513 to about 60,000 a day, from a deep surplus to 59% short), building materials, steel, electronics, software and construction services.

### Old page addresses redirect straight away

#### What changed

- Old addresses from before pages moved under a country, such as /parties,
  /politicians, /budget, /policy, /approval and old party and coalition links,
  showed a blank page for about a second before moving on. Link previews and
  search engines only ever saw the blank page.
- They now redirect straight to the country page. A `?country=` in the link
  still picks the country. Otherwise it goes to the United States.
- No game rules changed.

### Organisation screens match who can act

#### What changed

- Only the foreign minister can act for a country in international
  organisations. A head of government who does not hold that seat still saw
  join, leave, vote, posture, fund and create controls, and every one of them
  was refused.
- Those controls are now hidden for them, and the diplomatic action counter
  follows the foreign minister. They still see their own country's delegation
  read-only. Controls for an organisation they lead are unchanged.
- No game rules changed.

### Quieter turn log for routine index fund holds

#### What changed

- An index fund that has redemptions waiting skips its daily rebalance and
  catches up on the next turn. That one-turn hold is routine, so it no longer
  appears as a turn warning. A fund that misses a whole day of rebalancing
  still warns, once a day.
- A repair check written for state-owned sectors in the previous world failed
  every time it ran on the current world, because those records no longer
  exist. It now reports that there is nothing to repair.
- No game rules changed.

### Seats no longer vanish when a party bloc's holder leaves

#### What changed

- In the House of Commons, Bundestag and Seanad, one record holds a party's
  seats in a region. When its holder deleted their account, retired, resigned
  or (for party-run politicians) moved region, the record lost its party but
  kept its seat count, and those seats disappeared from the chamber totals.
- Vacating such a record now clears its seat count too, so the next election
  fills the seats. Commons seats that are waiting on a by-election keep their
  count, because the by-election uses it.
- No game rules changed.

### Sector input gauge now shows what actually held your plant back

#### What changed

- Each turn the sector input gauge was still filled in by the market model
  used before the producer market, which never saw producer market fills or
  deliveries from your own sites. Most plants showed 85%, its lowest setting,
  with an input such as freight named as the bottleneck while the plant ran
  at full rate.
- The gauge now comes from the producer market settlement: 100% when every
  input arrived, otherwise the share of the run the plant could make and the
  input that ran short. AI companies read the same figure when deciding to
  sign supply deals, so they no longer chase shortages that are not there.
- Own-site supply itself was already working: a company's logistics site
  fills its own plants' freight orders first. Those deliveries are internal
  transfers, so they count as delivered rather than sold, and by default they
  carry no price. A logistics site feeding its sister plants therefore shows
  low sales and a site loss while the company as a whole does not pay for
  that freight.

### Sector run chart separates output sent to your sites

#### What changed

- Added a separate chart segment for output sent to the company's own sites.
- Unsold output now excludes those deliveries while external sales retain their own segment.

### Single player autosaves keep their spacing on fast turns

#### What changed

- Autosave counts turns from the most recent autosave. When two saves were made
  in the same instant, it could count from the older one and save again a turn
  early. The newest save is now chosen by turn when the times match.
- No game rules changed.

### State laws now say when they are waiting for their first funding round

#### What changed

- A newly passed state law programme now reads "Awaiting first funding" on the metrics board, with how many turns remain until the first annual funding round.
- "Implementation stalled" is kept for programmes that have been funded but are not delivering.
- No game rules changed. Funding amounts, timing and metric pressure are the same.

### Connect v2 legislative votes to voter reactions

V2 law votes now affect a legislator's recorded policy positions. When a law is enacted, final For and Against voters receive opposite reactions from voter demographics, using the country's policy preferences. Abstentions are neutral. National and regional laws share the same rules, and enactment retries cannot award the reactions twice.

Demographic reactions now scale with the policy change and account for overlapping voter identities. Reversing the same policy change reverses its reaction. Household relief, childcare and reproductive laws have more specific preference mappings.

Corporate tax votes react to the change in the enacted rate or scheduled target. Cuts lower approval among low- and middle-income voters and raise it among high-income voters; increases reverse those effects. Rate updates and voter reactions settle together, and replayed enactments cannot award them twice.

Bill-detail demographic indicators use the same reaction rules as enactment, including the current corporate-tax rate or scheduled target.

Fixed demographic approval decay getting stuck on small modifiers. Stored balances now retain their precision, fading by 0.5% per turn until they fall below the existing zero threshold.

### Governments buy from their own plants first and share the rest fairly

#### What changed

- Government orders on the world market were filled in alphabetical order of country code. When the world was short, the governments early in the alphabet bought out the supply before the later ones were reached.
- On the latest turn the United States government asked for about 62,000 units of ordnance a day and received none. United States plants offered about 19,700 a day, and almost all of it was exported to France, Japan, Germany, Italy and Spain. The United States order was reached after the market was empty. Its price limit and budget were not the problem.
- With `producerPublicHomeFirstStage` at 1, each government's world-market order first buys from sellers in its own country, at its usual price limit and budget. What is left then goes to the shared market.
- With `producerPublicFairShareStage` at 1, government orders on the same world market split each price level in proportion to what each still wants. A government that hits its price limit or budget takes what it can afford, and its unused share goes to the others. No country is favoured by its name.
- On the latest turn home-first would deliver about 19,700 units a day to the United States government. Governments that build little ordnance of their own, such as France, Germany and Italy, would import less. Fair share then spreads the small remainder: the United Kingdom would get about 60 a day instead of 3.

### Plants recover their output from their plan, and the page says what holds them

#### What changed

- Behind a new switch, off until it is turned on, a plant plans each turn's
  output from the output it planned last turn. A turn where short cash or a
  short input cut what it made no longer lowers next turn's plan, so a plant
  that is a little short every turn keeps its plan instead of sliding down.
- A plant that sold everything and covered its costs grows by a fixed step of
  15% of its capacity a turn, or faster when its market is short. A plant at
  its minimum run is back to a full run in about seven turns instead of
  seventeen or more.
- One turn of almost no sales now steps a plant's plan down by a tenth. Only a
  second such turn in a row drops it to its minimum run.
- The sector page no longer says "Ramping up" when a plant sold out but was cut
  by cash or a missing input. It says "Held by cash" or "Held by" the input
  that ran short.

### Plants whose sales pay for them keep running after a big build, idle plants can restart, and builds keep a cash reserve

#### What changed

- When a company is short of cash, its plants can borrow against the sales the run will make in the same turn. That only happened when expected sales covered the run's costs, and the costs were counted with every input at the highest price the company would bid, often three to ten times what the input actually sold for. At those prices almost no run ever covered its costs, so a company that spent its cash on construction ran on its leftover cash alone and earned too little to rebuild it.
- In one case a company spent most of its cash on new capacity. Its plants then ran at about 12% of plan for turns on end: inputs counted at the bid ceiling came to 23.7 million a turn, against 6.2 million at last turn's market prices, and its sales fell from about 7 million a turn to about 1.2 million.
- With `producerSalesAdvancePriceStage` at 1, the check uses last turn's market price for each input, never above the bid ceiling. The amount borrowed is still capped by the run's expected sales, and anything the sales do not cover is still owed as an ordinary bill, so no money is created. Off by default; nothing changes until it is switched on.
- Replaying the latest turn with the switch on: 198 companies were held back by cash, and 119 of them would run their full plan. The company above would make about 207,000 units instead of 32,000 and sell about 7.7 million instead of 1.2 million, with nothing left owed. Total cash in the world is unchanged.
- A company with plants, no cash and no sales last turn could never start again: no cash means no run, no run means no sales, and borrowing against sales needs last turn's sales. With `producerZombieRestartStage` at 1, such a plant may run a trial at 5% of its capacity. The trial is paid for by borrowing within the turn against what its output sells for at today's market price, and the trial's own sales repay it before the turn closes. Anything they do not cover is owed as an ordinary bill, so no money is created.
- A plant only runs a trial when another buyer has money to buy its output at the market price, and when the trial pays for itself with its inputs at market prices. A plant whose trials fail to pay for themselves three turns in a row stops trying. Off by default; nothing changes until it is switched on.
- Replaying the latest turn with both switches on: 60 idle companies run trials on 246 plants, 59 of them sell in the first turn, for about 545,000 of sales in total. Total cash in the world is unchanged and no account goes below zero.
- Capacity builds, one at a time or in bulk, now stop if they would leave less cash than three turns of the plants' running costs (wages, site bills and inputs from their last turn). The refusal states the reserve and the largest build that keeps it. The CEO can confirm to build anyway. Builds paid by a construction loan are not affected. `capacityBuildReserveTurns` changes the number of turns; 0 turns it off.

### Priced deliveries between your own sites now show in the site accounts

#### What changed

- The turn already worked out the charge for goods one of your sites delivers
  to another, but it was dropped before it reached the site accounts, so every
  site showed no charge and no income. The charge and the matching income are
  now recorded and shown.
- Company cash and the figures used by tax, salary and valuation are unchanged.
