### Refinanced government bonds no longer pile up into one giant maturity

#### What changed

- When a government bond matures and the Treasury is short of cash, up to three things can refinance it: the bond market swaps part of its holding for new paper, the rest of its holding is rolled into new paper, and the government sells a new bond to raise the cash it owes everyone else. Each of these used to create its own new 48-turn bond for every bond that matured, so a turn with 9,460 maturities issued 25,976 new bonds, all due on the same later turn.
- That pattern repeated and grew every 48 turns: 1,679 bonds, then 3,597, then 9,460, then 25,976. Each bond costs work every turn for interest and several settlement steps when it matures, so the turn where they all came due took 53 minutes instead of about 8.
- With the new `sovereignRolloverConsolidationEnabled` stage switched on, all three refinancing paths add their new paper to one shared bond per country, currency, interest rate, term and turn. The face value, the interest the government pays, the cash raised and paid out, and the holdings are identical to before. Only the number of bond documents changes.
- A one-time cleanup (`AHD-sovereign-rollover-wall`) folds bonds already split this way into one bond per series, keeping each holder's position, so the next maturity wall shrinks from about 26,000 bonds to about 300. Its dry run is `scripts/ops/sovereignRolloverWallDryRun.ts`.
- The stage defaults to off. With it off, refinancing behaves exactly as before.

### Short local service markets can reopen to new healthcare firms

#### What changed

- The unowned market pool in a state never grows back once the first firms use it up: its growth and the yearly reseed both multiply it, and zero times anything is zero.
- Healthcare, real estate and construction sell only in their own state, so a used-up pool closed the state to every new company and to autopilot growth, however short it was. On the latest turn California, New York, Florida, Pennsylvania and four Japanese regions had healthcare pools of zero while their healthcare was hundreds of thousands of visits short.
- With `nppStateServiceHeadroomStage` at 1, the AI pass reads room in these markets as the larger of the pool and the state's unmet demand, less capacity already being built there. The stored pool is unchanged.
- On the latest turn this opens 11 closed healthcare states and raises the combined founding size limit across healthcare markets from about 54,000 to about 445,000 units a day. Cash and the other entry checks still apply.

### Substitution orders no longer make a good look short

#### What changed

- When a household's first-choice good sells out, the market moves 70% of the money it could not spend onto other goods it buys that still have stock (spillover). Those extra orders were counted as ordinary demand, so a cheap good that caught a lot of spilled money looked badly short even though every unit made was sold.
- On the latest turn, households placed 155,128 units of chemicals orders as substitution, out of 176,565 chemicals units ordered in total. Chemicals read as 14% filled. Counting first-choice orders only, every first-choice unit was delivered.
- Each household order now records how much of it came from spillover, and each country's market book records the total. Country market views read demand as first-choice orders only, and show substitution orders on their own line ("plus N substitution orders"). Units bought through substitution still count as received.
- This is measurement only. Deliveries, prices, cash, settlement receipts, the price phase and the shortage signals that drive company growth and restarts are unchanged. Replaying the latest turn with and without this change gives an identical settlement.

### A plant feeding your own short factories keeps running

#### What changed

- Your plants send their output to your own sites first. The game judged those
  deliveries at the market price, so a mine feeding your own steel plants could
  be cut to a sliver of capacity when the market price fell below what mining
  cost, even while your steel plants could not get enough iron.
- When your own sites are left short of a good your company also makes, what
  your plant sends them now counts at what those sites would pay for it, up to
  the good's normal price. A plant like that keeps running, and steps up until
  your own sites have what they need.
- Nothing changes when your own sites can get all they need from the market.

### AI companies can borrow to build in markets that are running short

#### What changed

- Demand for food, retail, vehicles and advertising has grown much faster than the plants that make them. Most of that capacity belongs to AI companies, and most of those companies hold less cash than their safety reserve, so they stopped building even where their plants were profitable and buyers were going without.
- With `nppShortageGrowthFinanceStage` at 1, an AI company's plant qualifies when buyers on its main market got three quarters or less of what they ordered last turn, the plant made a profit, and a new unit earns back its build price within 100 turns at current prices.
- A qualifying plant can ask a bank to fund up to one growth step (half of what it currently runs). The company still pays its own share of the build, up to a quarter of the cash it holds, and the order is sized so that share fits.
- Banks still decide every loan with their usual checks. A company's construction loans may not pass 24 turns of its average profit, and up to 128 of these requests a turn are considered on top of the existing ones, deepest shortage and fastest payback first.
- Player companies are not affected, and AI companies on autopilot are left out.

### Builds due during an economy pause now arrive when it ends

#### What changed

- When the economy was paused, a build that was due to finish during the pause
  was removed from your queue without adding its capacity. You paid for it and
  got nothing.
- Builds that ran through part of a pause also arrived short, because the
  paused turns were skipped.
- Now every bit of capacity that came due during the pause arrives on the
  first turn the economy runs again. A build still under way keeps going from
  where it should be.
- Normal turns work exactly as before.

### Choosing to sell on the market first now works

#### What changed

- Your company's own sites take its output first by default. A company that
  explicitly chose to sell on the open market first was still sending its
  output to its own sites before any outside buyer.
- That choice now works. Outside buyers get your output first. Your own sites
  then take what is left, free as before, and buy anything else they need from
  other sellers.
- Companies that never changed their supply setting keep own sites first.

### Faster data repair backups

#### What changed

- The pre-repair backup reads touched documents 500 at a time instead of one at a time.
- The sovereign bond consolidation repair logs why a series was left alone.

### Households spend from what they earn, not from everything they have saved

#### What changed

- Households used to try to spend 90% of all the cash they held, every turn.
  Money they could not spend because shelves were empty stayed in their
  wallets and was ordered again the next turn, on top of new wages. Orders grew
  to about four times what households could really spend, so every shortage
  looked much worse than it was and prices were pushed up harder than needed.
- Behind a new switch, off until it is turned on, households now spend 90% of
  this turn's wages plus 30% of their savings. Orders match real spending
  power, and savings are spent over several turns instead of all at once when
  shelves refill.

### Prices can climb again when buyers go short

#### What changed

- Automatic prices follow what traded last turn, and nothing could raise them.
  One cheap seller pulled the average down and every automatic seller followed,
  even in markets where buyers could not get what they ordered.
- Behind a new switch, off until it is turned on, a company that sold all it
  offered in a market where buyers asked for more than they got now asks a step
  above last turn's price. The step grows with how short the market was, is at
  most 5% a turn by default, and never passes the market's price ceiling.
- A market that clears in full keeps its price. A company with goods left over
  gets no raise, and unsold stock still marks down as before.

### Primary headline no longer says "The The"

#### What changed

- The primary headline drops a party name's own leading "The" before adding its own.

### Product development no longer takes the whole R&D budget

#### What changed

- A product line or media title in development now takes at most half of your R&D budget each turn. The rest always goes to research, so your R&D score keeps growing while the product is paid for. Before, a large project could take the whole budget for days and research showed +0 at any budget.
- Development still gets everything it is owed, just spread over more turns. Nothing is lost: what development does not take goes to research.
- Earlier product projects, started before the product studio, now show at the top of the studio on the Sectors tab, with how much of their cost is paid, a note that they use part of the R&D budget, and the Retire button.
- The R&D line in the CEO Office budget and the Tech tab now name the project in development and the share of the budget it takes.
- Product studio projects are not affected. They are paid from cash, not from the R&D budget.

#### Developer notes

- `allocateProductDevelopmentBudget` (`src/lib/products/rules/productBudgets.ts`) caps development at `PRODUCT_DEVELOPMENT_MAX_RD_SHARE` (0.5) of the per-turn budget; generic research receives the remainder, so the two parts still sum to the budget. The turn (`sectorCalculations.ts`), `genericResearchDailyAnchor` and the new `productDevelopmentDailyAnchor` (`strengthProjection.ts`) all go through it, so the budget panel, Tech tab and income statement projection match the turn.
- Payment receipts, the per-turn paid markers and the cash cap on development spend are unchanged.
- `GET /api/corporation/[id]/tech` returns `rdFundedProduct.sharePct` and now reports the funded product whenever development takes a share, not only when research is zero.

### Reconcile financial and economy displays

#### What changed

- Show debt-to-GDP ratios as percentages and convert quarterly report rates to the selected financial period.
- Include investment dividends in reported earnings and gross bond interest in cost charts.
- Count unsold corporate share reservations and use the held company's explicit currency in balance-sheet valuations.
- Include index funds and unpaid income tax consistently in personal portfolio totals. Keep the loan deduction when the separate loan-controls request is unavailable.
- Include corporate index funds in the portfolio, its breakdown and future snapshots. New snapshots also retain unsold share ownership and all outstanding issued bond liabilities.
- Use the same own-site supply valuation for sector list and detail margins, with the non-cash amount explained separately.
- Read national unemployment from the current published metric rollup when v2 metrics are enabled, across all countries. Missing or stale v2 data is shown as unavailable.

#### Compatibility

The optional corporate history `fundValue` field is written only to new snapshots.
Existing snapshots are preserved without fabricated fund history. The existing
world-reset policy wipes the entire portfolio history collection, so no new
collection, index or backfill is required. These changes do not alter settlement,
payouts, taxation or economic balance.

### Sites that supply their own company now plan for that company's needs

#### What changed

- A site that made a sliver of its capacity and sent all of it to its own
  company's sites used to plan the same sliver again every turn. Meanwhile the
  company kept buying the rest of that good on the open market.
- Now the site plans at least enough to cover its own company's demand for the
  good, counting what the company's sites asked for in total, whether it was
  filled by the site or bought on the market. It ramps up to that level the next
  turn.
- The run is still capped at the site's capacity, and short inputs or cash still
  cut it when it settles.
- When a company has several sites making the same good, the demand is shared
  between them by capacity.
- Sites with no demand from their own company, and companies that buy on the
  market before their own supply, plan exactly as before.

### Sites that supply their own company now show what that output is worth

#### What changed

- A site that delivered its output to its own company's sites booked no income
  for it unless those sites had run short. A mine feeding its own steel plant
  could show a large loss every turn while the company would otherwise have
  paid the market for the same ore.
- When this is switched on, every delivery between a company's own sites is
  valued at that turn's market clearing price for the good, or at the selling
  site's asking price if the good did not trade that turn.
- The selling site's accounts show that amount as income from its own sites and
  the receiving site shows the same amount as a cost, so the company's totals,
  cash and profit do not change. No money moves.
- Companies that already set their own internal transfer price keep it.

### Sites that supply your own company no longer show a fake loss

#### What changed

- When you price internal supply at production cost or market price, the income
  your supplying site earns and the charge your receiving site pays now count in
  profit and margin on the company page and the sector page. A site that mostly
  supplies your own company no longer reads as -999%.
- With free internal supply, a supplying site books no sale. Its row and its
  sector page now show what it sent your own sites, valued at its own asking
  price. The margin counts that value, so a plant that feeds your other sites
  shows what it is worth to you.
- Your company's cash, tax, pay and share value do not change. This is what the
  screens show, not what the game books.
- The Internal supply panel in the CEO office now lists each good your sites
  made last turn, how much went to your own sites, and how much was sold to
  others. It used to say nothing was reserved for every company.
- The supply priority choice now reads the way it works: own sites first is the
  default, and Open market first sends your output to outside buyers first,
  with your own sites using what is left.
- When a plant is short of something your company also makes, the note under
  its inputs now says what happened: your other sites used all of it, your sites
  made only so much, or outside buyers took it first because you chose the open
  market. It no longer tells you to change a setting or haul freight when that
  would not help.

### Turns no longer stall after a retried turn changes its construction funding

#### What changed

- Turn 697 failed once after construction buyers had already been funded. Each retry computed slightly different funding, the game refused to pay the same turn twice, and turns stopped advancing.
- A retry now tops up or takes back the difference against what was already paid, once per retry, so nobody is funded twice and the turn can finish.
- The error for a sector whose plant records changed mid-turn now names the sector, so that kind of failure can be traced quickly.

### Unpaid government bond interest is kept as a few records a turn, not one per bond

#### What changed

- Each turn, every government bond's interest was recorded as its own claim, and a claim only clears when the Treasury has the cash. Heavily indebted governments rarely do, so the unpaid claims piled up: about 1.5 million of them, growing by about 31,000 a turn, and the Treasury step read the whole pile several times every turn.
- With the new `sovereignCouponGroupedAccrualEnabled` stage switched on, a Treasury records each turn's interest as one claim per currency for player holders (characters and companies) and one for the bond market, index funds and NPPs. Each holder's share is the sum of what its per-bond claims would have been, at the same exchange rate, and it is paid the same way when the Treasury has the cash.
- A one-time cleanup (`AHD-sovereign-coupon-arrears`) folds the unpaid claims already recorded into the same shape. Its read-only dry run (`scripts/ops/sovereignCouponArrearsDryRun.ts`) folds 1,516,610 open claims into 7,582 and moves no money. Claims a payment already in progress owns are left alone.
- The stage defaults to off. With it off, interest is recorded exactly as before.

### AI companies can expand plants that make several goods when one of them sells out

#### What changed

- Defense, construction and logistics plants make two goods each. If one good sold out while the other sat unsold, or went to the company's own sites, the plant was treated as not selling its output and the AI company would not expand it, even where buyers were short of the good that sold out.
- With `nppGrowthPerProductFillStage` at 1, an AI company's plant passes its sell-through check when any one product it makes sold at least 85% of what it put up, counting units handed to the company's own sites as sold.
- The same switch makes the shortage reading follow the plant's most short product instead of the one it delivered the most units of.
- The existing market, cash and profit checks still apply. Player companies are not affected.

### Autopilot leaves a sector you restarted running

#### What changed

- A corporation on autopilot used to park the sector you had just restarted on the very next turn, because its run of losing turns was never cleared. Restarting a sector yourself now clears that count and the count of turns with no sales.
- After a manual restart, autopilot will not park that sector for 24 turns, whatever the reason: a glutted market, a plant stuck at its demand floor, or a long run of losses. You can still park it yourself at any time.
- The sector page shows "Autopilot will leave this sector running until turn N" while the hold is active.
- AI companies and corporations without autopilot are unchanged.

### Builds no longer starve a profitable company's next run

#### What changed

- A build funded in part by a loan or bond is held to the three-turn running
  reserve for the part you pay from cash. Before, it skipped the reserve
  entirely, so a big loan-backed build could still empty the cash that runs
  the plants. You can still confirm past the reserve.
- When a company is short of cash, a plant whose sales pay for its running
  costs keeps running. That check ignored what your own sites pay each other
  for deliveries, so a company that feeds its own plants looked less profitable
  than it was and its plants were cut anyway. Those deliveries now count.
- The single and bulk build screens now show, before the confirm button, the
  cash you will have left and how much of next turn's run would be cut, or that
  it will be kept.

### Campaign manager map can highlight where your party is running

#### What changed

- The campaign manager map has a My party button. Turn it on and states where
  your party has a candidate in the races shown are outlined, and the rest fade.

### Companies ship all their goods on their own trucks first

#### What changed

- Own shipments first now covers goods you sell to the general market, not only
  hauls between your own sites. Your trucks in a state carry your share of what
  leaves that state before other companies' goods.
- Goods carried on your own trucks are not billed: no freight charge to the
  buyer and no haul income for the state fleet. Anything beyond your own
  capacity bills as before.
- Companies with no freight in a state see no change there.
- Rivals shipping out of a state where one company owns most of the freight get
  the remaining capacity, so their shipments can be tighter.

### Companies ship their own goods first on freight they own

#### What changed

- Until now every company's outbound bulk hauls from a state drew on one shared freight pool, first come first served. A company that had built logistics there could still see its own steel and building materials stuck behind other companies' loads.
- With `ownFreightFirstStage` at 1, a company's hauls to its own plants in other states use the freight capacity that company owns in the origin state first. Capacity it plans to use is kept back from other companies for that turn. Capacity it does not plan to use stays in the shared pool.
- The part of a haul carried on the company's own capacity is internal supply: it is not billed to the company and the state's freight fleet does not earn it. The rest bills and pays exactly as before.
- Companies with no freight capacity in a state keep using the shared pool, and so does every haul that is not an own-supply route. Applies only when own-supply routes run (freight settlement active).

### Corporation types split into two files

#### What changed

- The sector and build order type definitions now live in their own file so the corporation types file stays under the size limit.
- No behaviour change.

### Fallback site margin rows say they count outside sales only

#### What changed

- The "effective margin" line in the older site margin breakdown now reads
  "effective margin (outside sales only)". It never counted goods a site sends to
  its own company's other sites.

### Held freight capacity can no longer be spent by rivals in a crowded state

#### What changed

- A company's own trucks in a state stay reserved for its own goods even when
  the state's freight is crowded. Before, rival goods served earlier could use
  the extra congested capacity and take the trucks held for the owner, so the
  owner's building materials or other market goods got no cover at all.
- States that are not crowded behave as before.

### Lender-sale sites can be bought into a market you already run

#### What changed

- A site sold by its lender because of a defaulted construction loan now merges into your existing site in that market when you buy it.
- Your price pays the lender first. You take on no debt from the site, and the sector page shows how much of the loan your price clears before you confirm.
- Your purchase is still blocked while your own site in that market is in the middle of another ownership change or has a construction loan that is not yet stable.
- Both sites must run the same strategy to merge. If your existing site in that market uses a different strategy from the one for sale, the purchase is refused before any money moves, and the sector page names both strategies and asks you to switch your site first. This applies to ordinary listings as well as lender sales.

### Maintainers can approve contributor PRs from a comment

#### What changed

- A maintainer comment of the form `/approve <commit>` on a pull request now counts like an approving review for that exact commit. A later push needs a fresh comment.
- Promotion pull requests from `development` to `staging` and from `staging` to `main` in this repository skip the contributor governance checks, since their content was checked when it entered `development`. Pull requests from forks never qualify.
- The governance check re-runs when such a comment is posted.

### Own freight use is now recorded each turn

#### What changed

- Each turn's shipping record now lists, per state and owner, how much of the
  owner's own freight capacity their own shipments used. This makes it possible
  to check that the own-shipments-first rule is working.
- No gameplay change.

### Party chairs can file governor impeachments while their legislator has a bill running

#### What changed

- A party chair who directs a state legislator to file articles of impeachment
  against a governor no longer loses that option while the legislator has a
  bill in progress or a governor bill waiting for them.
- Bill proposals through a party legislator still use one legislator per bill.

### Plants expect rivals to match their price when they price above households

#### What changed

- An automatic plant guesses how much it would sell at each price it could
  ask. It assumed every rival kept last turn's price. In a short market those
  prices sat above what households will pay, so each plant thought it alone
  could sell to the companies bidding more, and every plant priced there.
  Households bought none of it and the food went unsold.
- At the next step of the switch, a plant assumes rivals asking more than a
  price would match it. A price only a few companies can pay then looks as
  crowded as it really is, and the plant stays where households can buy.
- Prices set by players are not affected.

### Plants stop pricing food a fraction above what households will pay

#### What changed

- An automatic plant picks its asking price from the prices buyers have said
  they will pay. When another company's top bid sat a fraction above the
  household limit, every plant in the market picked that slightly higher
  price. Households could not pay it, so they bought nothing from those plants
  and the food went unsold while shoppers went short.
- Behind a new switch, off until it is turned on: when two prices would earn
  within 1% of each other, the plant takes the one more buyers can pay.
- Manual and cost-plus prices set by players are not affected.

### Plants that supply their own company are no longer treated as losing money

#### What changed

- With own-site deliveries valued at market prices, a plant that hands its
  output to its own company's other sites books that value, and the site that
  receives it books the same amount as a cost. Until now the operating
  decisions ignored both, so a supplying plant read as a loss even when its
  company was saving the full market price.
- The transition "losing" and "no sales" marks, restart trials for idle plants,
  and the loss counts AI-run companies use to mothball sites now read the
  plant's result including those deliveries. The receiving site carries the
  matching cost in the same decisions.
- Company cash, company totals, taxes, valuations and profit payouts are
  unchanged: the two entries cancel inside the company, so no money is created.
- Nothing changes until own-site deliveries are valued at market prices.

### Prices in short markets can rise past one cheap seller

#### What changed

- In a market where buyers go short, a plant that sold everything asks a
  little more next turn. The raise started from the market's average price.
- When one large seller prices well below the rest, the average barely moves.
  Ordnance buyers got about a tenth of what they ordered while its price rose
  only about 2% a turn.
- At the next step of the switch, a plant that sold out raises from the higher
  of the market price and its own last price. A plant with goods left over
  still gets no raise, and the existing price ceiling still applies.
- Prices set by players are not affected.

### Retooling a sector now shows in the company's finance history

#### What changed

- Switching a sector to a different strategy costs a quarter of the sector's daily revenue. Cancelling a switch part-way costs a share of revenue too. Both charges were taken from company cash without a finance history entry, so a large charge looked like missing money.
- Both now record a "Sector Retooling" entry with the amount, the sector and the old and new strategy. The price itself is unchanged.

### Sector "stuck" figure now reflects your own goods

#### What changed

- A sector's "stuck" percentage is now worked out from your company's own goods
  leaving that state, not every seller's. If your goods ship on your own routes
  or trucks while rivals' goods are stuck, your sector no longer shows the
  rivals' number.
- Companies that are not tracked for their own shipments in a state still see
  the state-wide figure, as before.
- Only the number shown changes. Prices, offers and revenue are unaffected.

### Sector revenue, profit and margins use the same site accounts

#### What changed

- Internal deliveries priced at cost or market price count as income for the supplying site and a matching charge for the receiving site across the company table, CEO operations, overview cards, sector header and money and margin panels.
- Revenue and profit sorting follow the figures shown in the rows. Totals remove transfers between the company's own sites, so those transfers do not inflate company earnings.
- Estimated value of free deliveries no longer changes net margin. A site with no revenue shows no percentage; its actual profit or loss remains visible.
- Saved funded site accounts carry their settled turn. Detailed costs are joined only to a matching turn, so a new delivery receipt cannot be mixed with older accounts.
- Stock at cost and transition support appear as separate credits. Transfer pricing preferences, production allocation, company cash, tax and valuation rules are unchanged.

### Supply chains finish planning before the market settles

- The live market can use up to 64 planning passes, stopping early when sites and credit allocation stop changing materially.
- A 30-second cooperative budget is checked between passes. An in-progress pass and the final cash and inventory clearing always complete.
- If a safety limit is reached, turn warnings identify the largest changing sites. The frozen receipt retains all materially changing sites and the actual pass count for offline replay.
- Existing receipts remain valid. No tax, price, recipe, market priority or capacity rules change.
- Offline replay accepts sites without state or household records and preserves the country attribution recorded in frozen trade receipts.

### 2027 exchange rates, the modern Turkish lira and new currency markets

#### What changed

- In a 2027 world, the pound, yen, real, yuan, naira, krona and lira start at their 2024 average rates against the dollar instead of older placeholder values. Sweden's krona, for example, now opens near 10.6 to the dollar rather than 4.3.
- Turkey's 2027 budget, bonds and currency use the modern lira (TRY). Turkey's and Sweden's government budgets were resized to the new rates, so their economies are the same size in dollars as before.
- The Hungarian forint, Polish zloty and Romanian leu can now be traded in a 2027 world. Hungary also gets its own central bank, since its budget and bonds are in forints. Poland and Romania will get theirs once their national budgets are written.
- A 2027 world no longer creates an East German mark market, since East Germany does not exist in that world.
- Russia's regional budgets are now skipped outright when Russia is a background country, instead of starting and doing nothing.
- In every world, filing new election challengers takes far fewer database calls per turn, so turns with many open primaries finish sooner. Who gets filed, and for which party, is unchanged.
- Apart from that speed-up, worlds started from any other date, and the live world, behave exactly as before.

### 2027 US seats follow the November 2026 elections

#### What changed

- The US House opens with 256 Democratic and 179 Republican seats, state by state, following the races called after the November 2026 midterms.
- The US Senate opens with 54 Democratic-aligned and 46 Republican senators, so Democrats hold the Senate outright.
- Governors now follow the 2026 results: 29 Democratic and 21 Republican.
- State senates now use the newest available party split in each state instead of 2020 numbers.
- A few races were still being counted when this was set. Those seats keep the party the earlier projection or the sitting chamber had, and will be corrected once results are certified.
- Every seat holder is still a made-up politician. Only the party split comes from the real elections.
- The 2027 president and vice president are unchanged, and worlds started from any other date, and the live world, behave exactly as before.

### 2027 world setup fixes

#### What changed

- In a 2027 world, Reform UK's five Commons seats now belong to Reform UK. They were previously seated as independents.
- The setup check that runs after a new world is created no longer flags background countries for having no local businesses. Background countries run outside the full simulation by design, so a fresh 2027 world now passes the check and records its starting snapshot.
- Worlds started from any other date, and the live world, behave exactly as before.

### A site that feeds your own other sites is now valued for the goods it hands over

#### What changed

- A mine, mill or plant that sells nothing because your own sites take everything it makes used to be valued at the paid cost of its capacity, while the sites it feeds got the goods free. That left it looking like a plant with no earnings in the company's share price and credit rating.
- With `fundedValuationOwnSiteCreditStage` at 1, each such site is valued as if its deliveries were worth this turn's price for that good, and the site receiving them carries the same amount against its value. Across one company the two cancel out, so a company that runs everything itself is neither better nor worse off than before.
- Valuation only: no cash moves, and the site profit and loss pages, sales figures and internal supply lines do not change.
- Companies that already book their own internal supply entries, through an internal transfer price or the market-value setting, are left as they are, so nothing is counted twice.

### AI companies adopt breakthrough methods, party renewal news and party spending

#### What changed

- AI-run companies can now retool a plant onto a 2049 breakthrough method, such as commercial fusion, once the world's companies in that sector have done enough frontier research and the company has the matching technology.
- The operating strategy panel now shows the lower switching cost when your current method has been retired and you move to one of its successors. The confirmation shows the discounted fee too.
- When an AI-run party splits, a new party is founded, or a small party folds into a larger one, a news post now announces it.
- Parties that no player founded or leads now spend part of any treasury above a reserve on Political Strength each turn, so their funds no longer pile up without limit. Parties led or founded by players are unchanged.
- Sovereign debt news headlines read more cleanly.

### AI companies short of cash can still borrow to build where goods are short

#### What changed

- AI companies with no savings above their reserve stopped expanding, so they
  would not spend the cash their plants need to run. That also stopped them
  asking a bank to fund plants in markets that are short of goods, which was
  meant to work even below the reserve.
- A company held at its reserve can now ask for that loan again. It puts in at
  most a tenth of its own cash, and it still cannot found new businesses or
  build with its own money.

### AI-run governments now act on runaway deficits

#### What changed

- Countries run by AI governments could run a primary deficit of more than 10% of GDP for years, because the only response was the occasional budget bill.
- At the end of each fiscal year, an AI government with a primary deficit beyond 3% of GDP now enacts an austerity budget. When debt is high and still rising, it aims for a balanced primary budget instead.
- Each year's package closes about a third of the gap, and at least 2% of GDP or the whole gap if smaller, so a deficit of around 11% of GDP closes within five years.
- Left-leaning governments lean on tax rises, right-leaning governments lean on spending cuts, and centrist governments split it evenly. Tax rises arrive gradually, a point at a time, like any other tax change.
- No spending area is cut by more than 15% in a year or below half its normal level, and defence and debt interest are never cut.
- Austerity costs the government a little approval, more for a bigger package, fading over a year. It appears as an "Austerity budget" item on the approval page, and each package is announced in the news.
- When debt interest takes more than half of government revenue for two fiscal years in a row, the country restructures its debt through the existing sovereign debt crisis rules: bondholders take a haircut, the country is locked out of bond markets for a while, and then it recovers.
- Countries run by players are unchanged.

### An embargo no longer slows every company's turn

- Before, the first active block embargo gave every buyer in the world an explicit seller list, even buyers who could still reach every seller. The market then rebuilt millions of entries on every clearing, and turns slowed from about 8 minutes to 20 to 30 minutes.
- A buyer now carries a seller list only when the embargo closes at least one route for that buyer and good. All other buyers clear exactly as they did before the embargo.
- Embargoes still block the routes they name. Nothing changes for buyers an embargo affects.

### Automation, ageing, housing and education pressures in the 2027 world

#### What changed

- Humanoid robotics, agentic services, autonomous factories and autonomous freight now staff each unit of capacity with roughly half the usual crew or less. Workers they no longer need lose their jobs at once, so regional unemployment rises.
- Those workers find new jobs over a few years. A wage insurance law, an economy running above trend and a better educated workforce all speed this up.
- Pension and health spending now grow as the population ages. Japan starts with a heavier pension bill than the United States and both rise toward 2050. A pension age indexation law that ties the pension age to life expectancy keeps the pension bill much lower.
- Each region has a new Housing Pressure reading: households against the homes available. New construction adds homes, and housing supply and zoning reform decides how much planned building gets finished. When there are more households than homes, the cost of living rises.
- Each region has a new Adults With Degrees reading. It moves slowly toward a level set by university enrolment and education funding.
- Worlds started from any other date, and the live world, behave exactly as before.

### Background countries hold elections, run budgets and offer a national index

#### What changed

- Each background country has a ruling party or coalition, a named head of government and an approval rating. Leaders age, retire and are replaced by their party.
- Approval follows the economy: growth above the country's normal pace helps, while high inflation and rising unemployment hurt. Long-serving governments slowly lose favour.
- A national election for the lower chamber is held on each country's own term, with seats shared out by that country's voting system. A government whose approval stays very low for half a year faces an early election.
- New parties can appear when part of the political spectrum has been left empty for two elections, or as a protest against a very unpopular government. Parties stuck under 2% for two elections merge into their nearest neighbour.
- National budgets now come from each economy: tax take is a share of national output and spending covers public programmes plus interest on the debt. When the deficit gets out of hand the government passes an austerity package, and its approval pays for it. Left-leaning governments lean on taxes, right-leaning ones on cuts.
- Their deficits keep issuing government bonds in their own currencies, which American players can buy like any other country's bonds.
- Each background country has one national index fund that tracks its output and profits. Players can buy and sell it like any other index fund.
- Worlds started from any other date, and the live world, behave exactly as before.

### Background countries in the 2027 world trade in the markets

#### What changed

- Each background country in a 2027 world (Ireland, Brazil, Nigeria, Russia, Poland, Hungary, Romania, Bulgaria, France, Italy, Spain, Sweden, Turkey, Austria, Finland and Greece) now starts as a whole economy built from its 2027 population, GDP, industry mix and natural resources.
- The traded part of that economy enters its own national market. American goods can sell into French or Brazilian demand, and their surpluses can compete in the United States, subject to the usual tariffs, embargoes and shipping.
- Background economies grow at their own long-run rate each year. Wars, sanctions and crises cut their output and slow that growth until they pass.
- Ireland no longer appears among the 2027 globe's economic powers; it is a background country like the other fifteen.
- Worlds started from any other date, and the live world, behave exactly as before.

### Background countries set tariffs, impose sanctions and pick sides

#### What changed

- A background country's ruling parties now decide its stance abroad. Statist and nationalist governments raise tariffs, market liberals lower them. EU members keep the common EU tariff.
- Outside the EU, a protectionist and hawkish government can put an extra tariff on goods from the leader of the rival bloc. Tariffs move a little at a time, never all at once.
- Governments lean their country toward or away from a bloc over the years. A country that leans far enough can apply to join an alliance, and one that drifts away can leave, through the same rules every other country follows.
- When a country goes to war against one of its partners, background governments can sanction it. Sanctions last half a year, are only renewed while the war goes on, and are lifted early when the war ends or a more dovish government takes over.
- When an alliance calls its members to a war they are not bound to join, a background government decides once. Weak or dovish governments say no, and a refusal stands. Background countries never start wars of their own.
- In living conflicts, background governments now answer the world's call: hawks commit most, doves least, and the rest take the usual course.
- A background country that sells more abroad than it buys sees its currency strengthen over time, and one running a deficit sees it weaken.
- From 2027 the Cold War tension console also tracks tension between each pair of blocs: the US-led bloc, the China-led bloc and the non-aligned countries. Wars and sanctions between two blocs raise their pair, and readiness answers to whichever is hotter, the world's overall tension or the hottest pair.
- Worlds started from any other date, and the live world, behave exactly as before.

### Background countries trade in the 2027 producer market

#### What changed

- Each background country in a 2027 world now has its own trading account in its own currency. Every turn it offers the traded part of what its economy makes and bids for the traded part of what it needs.
- American companies can sell to these countries and buy from them in the ordinary market, at market prices. Sales and purchases are paid through the normal money system and currency exchange.
- Embargoes and closed trade routes stop this trade, the same as for any other buyer or seller. Local services such as healthcare, property and haulage stay at home.
- A background economy follows its real trade: if its exports stop selling, its industry slowly shrinks; if they keep selling out, it slowly grows.
- Worlds started from any other date, and the live world, behave exactly as before.

### Background country pages and organisation votes

#### What changed

- Opening a background country in a 2027 world shows one overview page: its economy (GDP, growth, inflation, unemployment, debt and credit rating), its currency chart, its trade with the United States and the tariffs each side charges, its government and seat chart with the next election date once national politics are running, the government bonds and national index you can buy, and its alliances and active crises.
- Background countries have no regions, legislature or politicians to play, so links to those pages now take you to the country's overview instead of an empty page. Its currency market still works as before.
- Background countries now vote on sanctions, trade agreements, statements, aid, dues, posture, agency funding and chair elections in the organisations they belong to. Each vote follows the country's bloc alignment, its government's economic stance, what the measure would cost its economy and how the rest of the bloc has voted. A country never votes to sanction or condemn itself.
- Admissions, war declarations and joining a war are still decided by player countries only.
- Worlds started from any other date, and the live world, behave exactly as before.

### Behind-the-scenes cleanup of unused code

Unused helper functions, types and three leftover election display pieces were
removed. Gameplay, numbers and screens are unchanged.

### Births and life expectancy follow country trends in the 2027 world

#### What changed

- In a 2027 world, births per woman start at 1.60 in the United States, 1.45 in the United Kingdom, 1.38 in Germany, 1.15 in Japan and 1.00 in China, and drift slowly toward their 2050 levels. Other countries start at 1.50.
- Family and demography policy can move a country's birth rate by up to 0.3 births per woman either way. Regions keep their own differences around the national level.
- Life expectancy rises by about 0.15 years for every in-game year from 2027 until 2060. Healthcare policy still makes people live longer or shorter on top of that.
- Worlds started from any other date, and the live world, behave exactly as before.

### Building logistics and construction is priced against the whole country

#### What changed

- Building gets more expensive when your company could already supply a large
  share of what buyers want, so one company cannot corner a market.
- Freight and construction now sell anywhere in their own country, but this
  build cost still compared them with a single state's demand. In a small state
  a normal logistics arm looked like several times the market, and building
  more cost up to 45 times the usual price.
- It now compares freight and construction with demand across the whole
  country, and counts your sites across that country. Other goods are not
  affected.

### Climate change bites in the 2027 world

#### What changed

- A 2027 world opens with climate pressure at 0.20 instead of zero, and each ton of emissions above a safe level now pushes it up faster. A world that cuts emissions quickly ends near 0.35 by 2050, one that follows current policies near 0.45, and one that keeps emitting at today's level near 0.6.
- Farms in hot and dry regions lose up to 15% of their harvest as pressure climbs toward 0.6. Precision agriculture halves the loss and cellular agriculture avoids it entirely.
- Households in hot regions spend more on power as summers get hotter: about 5% more at pressure 0.5.
- Farms and property companies in regions exposed to storms, floods, fire or drought pay rising insurance premiums, which eat into their margins.
- When a weather disaster strikes an exposed region, more of the damage lands on the national budget as pressure rises, because insurers pull back.
- Every country in the 2027 world now has hazard information: each region of the United States, United Kingdom, Germany, Japan and China, and a national profile for each of the other sixteen countries.
- Washington DC, Berlin, Saarland and the East and West Midlands had no hazard data and could never see a flood. They are now flood-prone in every world.
- Apart from those flood regions, worlds started from any other date, and the live world, behave exactly as before.

### Code comment guard

A new architecture check keeps code comments short and free of dates, ticket
numbers and change history. Existing comments are baselined and can only be
reduced.

### Computer-run parties can split, form and fold in new worlds

#### What changed

- In countries run by computer politicians, the same parties used to hold power for decades. Nothing new was ever founded and nothing ever went away.
- After each national general election, a party that has governed two terms in a row with approval under 40%, or held more than 55% of the national chamber for two terms, may split. The faction forms a new party next to its parent on the political map, takes about a quarter of the parent's politicians (those closest to its views) and keeps any seats they hold.
- When part of the political map has had no party within reach for two elections, a new party may be founded there with a small group of politicians in one region.
- A party under 2% of the national vote in two general elections in a row folds into the closest party. Its politicians, seats and offices move with it.
- At most one split and one new party per country per election. None of this is guaranteed: whether and when it happens depends on approval, seats and votes.
- A party holding more than half the seats no longer gets a bigger incumbency boost for every extra seat it holds.
- A party that held a region in either of the last two election cycles may recruit there again, even if it was wiped out.
- Player-created parties never split or fold this way. Worlds without the switch are unchanged.

### Computer-run politicians grow old, retire and die in new worlds

#### What changed

- Computer-run politicians in office could only leave by losing an election, and only died of old age at the highest autonomy setting. Over decades the same faces held every seat.
- A new turnover switch, on for every new world, lets them die of old age whatever the autonomy setting, and adds retirement: from age 70 a politician has a 5% yearly chance of stepping down, rising to 35% a year at 80 and above.
- A politician who retires or dies is replaced at once by a younger member of the same party in the same office. Their cabinet posts, government role and leadership pass to the successor, as deaths already did.
- Politicians seeded without a birth year are given one (age 35 to 70) so they age like everyone else.
- Player characters do not age. Worlds that do not have the switch keep the old behaviour.

### Countries run by the AI government can now hit a sovereign debt crisis

#### What changed

- On worlds running the two newest AI government levels, countries closed to players were not recognised as being run by an AI government.
- Because of that, a country deep in debt could fail every bond auction for years without its failed-auction count moving, so no sovereign debt crisis ever began and nothing forced a response.
- These countries are now recognised correctly. Three failed auctions in a row start a sovereign debt crisis, and the AI head of government responds through the existing crisis choices.
- The same fix lets players browse the political pages of those countries read-only again, as intended.
- Countries still need very high debt (at least twice their GDP) before an AI-run country can enter a crisis. Player countries are unchanged.

### Energy transition: storage, ageing plants and climate laws

#### What changed

- In a 2027 world, renewables lose up to 15% of their output in regions where they supply most of the power and nothing firms them. Grid storage plants, and smart grids at half strength, remove the loss once there is about one unit of storage for every four of renewables.
- Power plants older than 40 years and factories older than 30 cost more to run each year past that age, up to 20% more. Building new capacity brings a fleet's average age down. Opening fleets start with a realistic age, so old coal power starts to feel it around 2040.
- Three new laws can be filed from 2027 in the United States, the United Kingdom, Germany, Japan, China and Ireland:
  - **Carbon Price**: coal and gas power, chemical works, factories, mines and builders pay a charge on their carbon. In the United States and United Kingdom the charge also raises budget revenue.
  - **Coal Phase-Out**: sets a closure date for coal power. Running costs for coal plants climb over the ten years before the date.
  - **Clean Power Standard**: requires a share of national power to be clean. Coal and gas plants pay for the shortfall when the country misses it.
- In a 2027 world these laws also lower the emissions that drive climate pressure, so a country that passes them helps slow the warming everyone shares.
- The top two levels of the critical minerals law now include a recycling duty that recovers a tenth or a fifth of the rare earths firms use.
- From 2035 in a 2027 world, surveying a deposit costs more the more of it has already been mined, up to three times as much for a worked-out field.
- In other worlds the new laws can still be passed and move the usual political measures, but the costs on firms, plant ageing and storage effects stay off.

### Fairer access to scarce production inputs

- Ordinary producer input orders of equal priority share available supply according to their remaining demand, price limits and budgets, including orders with trade-route eligibility lists.
- Plants sharing company cash also share that budget fairly within each allocation, instead of the first plant spending its siblings' shares.
- Own-site delivery priority, local freight priority, restricted routes and supplier reservations remain in effect. Route-constrained receipts retain the exact supplying offers.
- Input buying limits apply existing price headroom to the last clearing price, bounded against an independent reference. Output reference prices do not change.

### Faster banking turn maintenance

- Bank servicing batches financial-sector capacity and configuration reads while retaining live balance reads and existing money-movement locks.
- NPC bank maintenance still checks eligibility each turn, including countries opening to private banking, with fewer repeated queries.
- Bootstrap includes lookup indexes for bank loans, NPC bank identities and savings holders in every currency. Existing worlds have a separate index installer.

### Faster market clearing with trade restrictions

- Shared route and supplier lists are compacted in immutable market receipts. Existing receipts remain readable.
- Fair allocations reuse route eligibility and supplier cohorts without changing priority, budgets or blocked trade routes.
- A separate execution deadline bounds market clearing. Once a market is fully computed, its receipt can finish encoding and durable settlement instead of discarding that completed work.

- Market preparation shares one supplier-route index across producers and final buyers, retaining domestic access and embargo restrictions.
- Receipt encoding batches plain supplier IDs and deadline checks. Staging verifies freshly encoded chunks against their exact stored bytes; existing encodings still receive full checksum verification.
- Receipt chunk reads use larger bounded batches without changing compression, chunk identities or recovery compatibility.
- The corporation phase has a fixed six-minute timeout. The market pre-settlement deadline leaves 90 seconds for settlement and other corporation work.

### Free transfer pricing no longer shows internal supply charges

#### What changed

- Sites of a company on Free transfer pricing showed an "Internal supply
  charges" line and "Own-site transfer income", valued at market prices, even
  though Free is meant to book nothing. The charge made some sites look like
  they were losing money when they were not.
- Free now books no entries between a company's own sites. Cost and Market
  pricing are unchanged.
- No cash moved before and none moves now; only the site accounts change.

### Freight and own-site labels say what they measure

#### What changed

- The freight warning on a sector now reads "up to N% stuck". The number is the
  worst-affected good the sector makes, not a share of everything it makes, and
  the tooltip says so.
- The "to your sites" figure on a sector card now reads "N% of all output to
  your sites". It is out of every product the sector makes, so a logistics
  sector that also sells consulting can show a lower share than its freight
  alone. Goods your own trucks haul to other buyers are not part of it.
- Only wording changes. No numbers, prices or deliveries are affected.

### Full Bundestag and coalition refusals in the 2027 world

#### What changed

- A 2027 world seats all 630 members of the Bundestag in the shape of the February 2025 result: CDU 164, CSU 44, AfD 152, SPD 120, Greens 85, Linke 64 and one SSW member sitting as an independent. Before, only 201 seats were filled against a 630-seat majority line, so every German government started as a minority.
- Parties can now refuse to govern with one another. In a 2027 world the CDU, CSU, SPD, Greens, Linke and FDP all refuse to form a government with the AfD. A computer-run government leaves refused parties out of its cabinet, and a party will not quietly prop up a minority government led by a party it refuses. When that costs a natural partner, the leading party reaches further across the spectrum for a majority, such as a Union and SPD coalition. If none exists, it governs as a minority.
- Refusals can wear off. If a party that refuses another loses seats at two elections in a row while the refused party is the largest in parliament, it may drop the refusal.
- In older start dates, Germany's seeded Bundestag is smaller than the full chamber until its first election. Until then, a majority is now measured against the members actually seated.
- Worlds started from other dates otherwise behave as before.

### Keep corporate income through turn timeouts

- Completed corporate income, including each character's per-currency income, is stored with phase completion and restored after a restart.
- A successful late completion can feed line-of-credit processing instead of discarding its income result.
- Retryable market failures retain their retry behavior, including failures that arrive after a timeout. Other corporate failures hold the turn for repair.
- A completed phase without a valid stored income result cannot resume using zero income. The repair command enforces the same requirement.

### Lighter pages for region conditions, corporations and campaigns

#### What changed

- The region conditions card no longer download every country's region and census data just to list and sort approval effects.
- The corporation strength charts in the wiki and the corporation strength forecast no longer download the full technology tree.
- The canvassing panel no longer downloads the United States era voter tables to list the voter groups.
- On character creation, the name lists for every country are now loaded the first time you press Randomize instead of with the page.
- A new automatic check stops these heavy server modules from being pulled back into pages by accident.

### Long-run growth, interest rates and score bands now follow a path to 2050

#### What changed

- In a 2027 world, trend growth for the United States, United Kingdom, Germany, Japan and China starts at 1.9%, 1.3%, 1.0%, 0.6% and 3.8% and eases toward 1.5%, 1.2%, 0.9%, 0.5% and 2.5% by 2045.
- The neutral interest rate rises slightly through the 2030s, then eases back. Inflation targets stay where they are today.
- Score bands for growth, jobs, life expectancy, crime and poverty keep moving to 2050, and every other band is explicitly held at its modern value so nothing is cut off at 2040 or 2019.
- Years 1999 to 2026, older start dates and the live world behave exactly as before.

### Market processing reliability and clearer health counts

- Fair allocation and embargo restrictions are preserved while repeated supplier accounting is pooled.
- Corporation processing has a five-minute timeout, with three quarters reserved for pre-settlement market work.
- After a retryable market failure, independent electoral and party work can complete. Financial dependants wait for settlement, and retries retain completed phases.
- Health cards show the relevant turn and separate failed phases from blocked phases, including historical snapshots.

### New laws for 2035 and 2040, and the first laws to retire

#### What changed

- From 2035, legislatures can pass a climate adaptation fund, a managed retreat programme for homes on coasts and floodplains that can no longer be defended, a universal basic income pilot, rules on human gene editing, and a space traffic act for crowded orbits.
- From 2040 they can also pass an automation dividend, a four-day work week, a central bank digital currency as legal tender, and a sovereign wealth fund.
- Every law has winners and payers. Managed retreat protects people from floods but costs homeowners and old coastal towns. A basic income pilot gives security but some people work less. A four-day week gives families time but costs employers. Digital currency makes payments cheaper but every payment leaves a record.
- In the United States and Britain, the automation dividend raises a levy and pays it back out to households, a digital currency earns the state a margin once it is legal tender, and a sovereign wealth fund pays part of its returns into the budget.
- Tying the pension age to life expectancy now cuts pension spending in the United States and Britain. A periodic review costs a little to run; each stronger level saves more.
- From 2040 the automation tax closes to new bills, because the automation dividend replaces it. In Japan, the older work culture reform law also closes to new bills that year, when the four-day week opens. A law already in force stays in force with its costs and effects.
- All nine new laws start with no statute in force, so nothing changes until a bill passes.

### New technology reaches countries at different speeds, and 10 and 30 year government bonds in the 2027 world

#### What changed

- Each country now has a technology readiness score built from its research spending, how many adults have degrees, whether it produces the energy it uses, and how open it is to trade.
- A new production method reaches a country 3 years after its research tier opens where readiness is high, 7 years where it is middling and 12 years where it is low.
- If a company in another country already runs the method, a player company can license it and open it up to 3 years sooner, though never before the tier itself opens. AI-run companies wait for their country.
- The production method list shows the year a locked method reaches your country.
- Governments can issue 10 year and 30 year bonds. Longer bonds pay a higher coupon.
- A finance minister can include 10 and 30 year bonds in the bond maturity profile.
- When nobody has set a profile, the country's debt manager chooses the mix itself, borrowing for longer when interest rates are low compared with recent years and shorter when they are high.
- Worlds started from any other date, and the live world, keep the 1, 2 and 5 year bonds and get new methods on the calendar as before.

### New worlds use fictional politicians only

#### What changed

- The 1991 and 2019 start dates no longer seat real officeholders. Every seated politician now gets a fictional name, the same as any other start date.
- The 2027 world opens with a fictional Republican president and vice president, two years into their term.
- Every Supreme Court that opens with a world now has fictional justices. Each seat keeps its leaning and the year it falls vacant.
- Politician portraits are generic faces only. A portrait never comes with the name of the person in the photo, and the image address no longer gives it away.
- The start-of-game introductions describe each era without naming real leaders.
- The live world is not changed.

### Newer start dates no longer miss out on older settings

#### What changed

- New 2027 worlds open with the same authored world alignments as 2023, and 1999 and 2007 worlds open with the 1991 ones, instead of every nation starting with the same lean toward Washington. Members of alliances and other organisations now get their opening alignment in these worlds too.
- In new 2027 worlds the Liberal Democrats, Japan's Liberal Democratic Party and Brazil's Workers' Party start as major parties, as they do in 2019. Germany's Greens start as major parties in 2023 and 2027 worlds as well. Party tiers still update every turn from how each party is actually doing.
- Singleplayer now offers 2027 as a start date.
- Election results pages for the Austrian, Finnish and Greek parliaments, the Italian and Spanish senates and the Brazilian Chamber of Deputies now show how many votes a seat cost, as other proportional chambers already did.
- Existing worlds, including the live world, are unchanged. The alliance and party changes apply when a world is started.

### Opening a savings account no longer fails when its record went missing

#### What changed

- Opening a savings account in a currency whose setup receipt was already applied, but whose account record was missing, replayed the receipt, inserted nothing and answered with a retry error every time.
- The account is now rebuilt from its own receipt, with the same balance and holder it was set up with, and the open or deposit continues.
- Nothing is booked twice: the bank and central bank totals recorded by the original setup are left as they were.

### Operators get a repair command for a turn held after a mid-economy restart

#### What changed

- A turn held because the company-profit step was interrupted can now be
  inspected and repaired with one command. It shows what the step saved for
  that turn and recommends one of three outcomes.
- If nothing was saved, the step is marked to run again. If everything was
  saved, the step is marked done so the turn skips it. If only part was saved,
  the command refuses and a manual fix is needed.
- It does nothing unless told to apply, and it never releases the turn itself.
  The normal resume carries on from there.

### Proportional parliaments stop handing every seat to one party

#### What changed

- Several parliaments (including the Polish Sejm, the Bulgarian and Romanian
  assemblies, the Czechoslovak chambers, the Soviet congress and the Italian,
  French and Spanish senates) required a party to win 20% of a region's vote
  before it could take any seats there. In a field of five to seven parties
  usually only the largest cleared that bar, so it won every seat in the
  region. Poland's Sejm ended up entirely one party on about a quarter of the
  vote.
- These chambers now use the same 10% bar as the other proportional
  parliaments.
- A party with less than half of a region's vote can no longer take all of
  its seats. If the bar leaves too few parties to cover half the vote, the
  next largest parties are let back in until it does.
- Applies to each chamber's next election. Seats already held are not
  changed.

### Record why AI companies' construction loans are turned down

#### What changed

- AI companies ask banks for construction loans to build new plants where
  goods are short. Most requests are turned down, but the reason was not
  recorded.
- Each turn now records how many requests failed for each reason. Nothing about
  how loans are decided has changed.

### Sector table total now shows how it relates to the rows

#### What changed

- Each sector row's profit includes the charge for supplies sent from the company's other sites. The Total row removes those transfers, so it could sit well above the sum of the rows with no explanation.
- When the two differ, the Total row now shows a small "Rows add to" figure under its profit, with a tooltip giving the charges and credits that account for the gap.
- Row and total figures themselves are unchanged.

### Seeded presidents keep the terms they have already served

#### What changed

- In a 2027 world, the sitting US president starts with both of his terms counted. He cannot enter the 2028 presidential race, and his party puts forward another candidate, such as the vice president, who remains eligible.
- In a 2019 world, the sitting Irish president starts with both of his terms counted, so he cannot stand again for Uachtarán.
- Computer-run politicians now follow the same executive term limits as players. A term they win in play counts toward the limit, just as it does for a player. Founding and acting terms do not count.
- Player term limits are unchanged, and worlds from other start dates seat their leaders exactly as before.

### Tariffs and blockades count in the producer market

#### What changed

- When a company, household, government or background country buys goods from another country, it now pays that country's import tariff on top of the price. Country-wide, sector and country-targeted tariffs all count, and free trade agreements still remove them.
- Buyers judge an import by its price including the tariff. If that is more than they are willing to pay, they buy at home instead or go without, so raising a tariff really does cut imports.
- The tariff money goes into the importing country's treasury cash each turn. Background countries pay and collect tariffs the same way as everyone else.
- A full naval blockade of either country now stops that trade route, like an embargo already does.
- This is on for newly started worlds. Existing worlds keep their current behaviour until it is switched on.

### The 2027 world opens with a seated president and current interest rates

#### What changed

- In a 2027 world the presidency and vice presidency open with a fictional Republican ticket, two years into the term that began in January 2025, instead of unnamed placeholders.
- The United States, United Kingdom, Germany (through the European Central Bank), Japan and China open a 2027 world on interest rates close to their latest real decisions: 3.6%, 3.75%, 2%, 0.75% and 3%.
- The opening-week pause on company bond issues, which ended in August 2026, has been removed. Companies could already issue bonds normally, so nothing changes in play.
- Worlds started from any other date, and the live world, behave exactly as before.

### The 2027 world splits countries into player, NPP-run and background tiers

#### What changed

- A 2027 world now starts with the United States as the only country players can join.
- The United Kingdom, Germany, Japan and China run the full simulation, run by NPPs. Each can be opened to players later without a reset.
- Ireland, Brazil, Nigeria, Russia, Poland, Hungary, Romania, Bulgaria, France, Italy, Spain, Sweden, Turkey, Austria, Finland and Greece are background countries. They keep their country page data, currency and exchange rate, but start with no companies, sectors, unions, NPP politicians or elections, and the hourly turn skips their elections, bills, governments, crises, disasters, world events and regional budgets.
- This should make each 2027 turn much faster. Background countries do not yet buy or sell in the markets; their trade, elections, budgets and bonds arrive in later updates.
- Worlds started from any other date, and the live world, behave exactly as before.

### The 2050s now begin on schedule, and the last research tier no longer shows a stale date range

#### What changed

- The era calendar now runs past 2049. A game reaching 2050 is in the 2050s and gets the new-decade news post, instead of staying in the 2040s.
- Once a game passes the last research tier's authored years, that tier is labelled "2029+" rather than "2029 to 2039". Nothing you own or have researched changes.
- A research tier with no cost set now stops the build instead of quietly costing less than every other tier.
- Politician photos dated 2030 or later are now recognised by their year.

### The twelve 2027 and 2030 laws reach the United States and Britain

#### What changed

- From 2027, Congress and Parliament can pass laws on AI accountability, data privacy, housing supply and zoning, a critical minerals reserve, cyber security for critical services, and duties for large online platforms.
- From 2030 they can also pass an automation tax, wage insurance, a carbon border charge, heat safety rules at work, a pension age tied to life expectancy, and faster nuclear licensing.
- Each law has four strengths above "no statute". Every one has winners and payers: building more homes helps renters and first buyers but costs open land, an automation tax protects workers but slows productivity, and a carbon border charge rewards clean producers while raising prices in the shops and straining trade ties.
- The automation tax and the carbon border charge now bring in revenue for the treasury. The other laws cost money to run.
- All twelve start with no statute in force, so nothing changes until a bill passes.
- A law cannot be proposed, by players, cabinets or parties run by the game, before the year it opens. A law that has passed stays in force for good, even if a later era closes it to new bills.
- These are national laws only. US states and UK regions cannot pass their own versions.

### Turn processing spends less time sharing scarce inputs between buyers with route limits

#### What changed

- When many companies compete for the same scarce input and some can only buy from certain suppliers, the turn now works out who can reach which supplier once, rather than repeating it at every price level.
- Who gets what is unchanged. This only shortens the time the corporation step takes on busy goods such as steel.

### Turns no longer skip the economy after a mid-turn restart

#### What changed

- A restart in the middle of the company economy step used to be treated as if that step had finished, so a turn could complete with every company's profit for that turn missing.
- The turn now refuses to continue in that case and is held for a repair, so no one loses a turn of profit without it being noticed.
- Any other step that was cut off by a restart is now shown as failed in the turn record, not as already applied.

### Twelve new laws arrive in 2027 and 2030

#### What changed

- From 2027, parliaments in Germany, Japan, China and Ireland can pass an AI Liability and Transparency Act, a Data Protection Overhaul, Housing Supply and Zoning Reform, a Critical Minerals Stockpile Act, a Cyber Resilience Mandate and a Platform Content Law.
- From 2030 they can also pass an Automation Tax, a Wage Insurance Act, a Carbon Border Adjustment, Heat Labour Standards, a Pension Age Indexation Act and Nuclear Licensing Reform.
- Each law runs from a strong version on one side, through "no statute", to a strong version on the other. Every choice has winners and losers: building more homes helps renters and young people but lowers home values, a robot tax protects jobs but slows investment, and a fixed pension age protects older workers while costing the budget more every year.
- New laws start with no statute in force, so nothing changes in a country until a bill passes. Laws that open in 2030 cannot be proposed before then.
- Governing parties run by the game can sponsor the new laws too.
- Worlds that start before 2027 are unchanged until their calendar reaches these years.

### Two new settings to get idle plants running

#### What changed

- A company with no cash can still run a plant when the plant's expected sales
  pay for the run. It could only borrow against 90% of those sales, so a plant
  with a thin margin stayed shut. A new setting lets that share be raised.
- A bankrupt AI company kept its plants idle for 30 turns before they passed to
  a funded owner. A new setting lets that wait be shortened.
- Both keep today's values until they are set.

### A banned party officer no longer blocks the seat

#### What changed

- A banned party officer no longer counts toward the two-officer approval rule or the higher payout allowance.
- Treasury approvals no longer get stuck waiting on a banned officer. With the seat empty, the chair or vice-chair covers it as they do for any vacant treasurer seat.
- The party page and the server now agree on how many officers are seated.
- Party election pages no longer list a banned player as the current office holder.

### A finished market is no longer thrown away at the last step

- The market's time limit now applies to working out the market, not to saving the finished result.

### A new world starts clean

#### What changed

- When the world resets, the starting map, budgets, political boards and other starting data are written fresh instead of being merged over the old world's records. Leftovers such as an old defence budget balance or old political momentum can no longer carry into the new world.
- Your account keeps your login, settings, supporter perks and history. Links to your old characters, old corporation cooldowns, old wire limits and corporations starred in your watchlist are cleared, because those belonged to the old world.
- Heads of state keep their identity and profile, and start the new world with an empty wallet as before.
- After a reset the game checks the new world for anything from the old one: wars, crises, elections, bills, embargoes, blockades, defence contracts, turn counters and more. If anything is found, the world stays paused until a clean reset, so a new world never opens with old state in it.
- The live world is not affected until the next reset.

### AI companies keep a smaller cash buffer before they expand

#### What changed

- The minimum cash an AI company keeps in reserve is lower: the base reserve drops from 250,000 to 200,000 and the smallest reserve any AI personality can have drops from 125,000 to 100,000.
- AI companies that were held just under the old reserve can now spend on growth and pay dividends sooner.
- Expansion cost, the extra cash needed to expand and dividend caps are unchanged. Player companies are not affected.
- More supply is expected in goods that stay short. Some AI companies may also run closer to their cash limit.

### Automatic check that every game action needs a sign-in

Every request that changes something in the game, from casting a vote to
buying shares, is now checked automatically before new code ships to make sure
it asks who you are first. The few actions that must work while signed out,
such as logging in, creating an account or resetting a password, are listed and
reviewed one by one. No gaps were found and gameplay is unchanged.

### Automatic crises and living conflicts are now always on

#### What changed

- Automatic crises and disasters always spawn on schedule. The pause control still stops new ones.
- Living conflicts always run, including the Vietnam war ladder, the Cold War dials and the pandemic, terrorism and Northern Ireland effects on budgets and politics.
- Crisis responses that read national income, such as the safety-net share, now use the same smoothed national income figure as the budget.
- Play is unchanged in the live world and in new worlds, since both were already on.

### Background country economy fixes

#### What changed

- A shock or a stability loss in a background country now reduces its GDP, tax revenue and national index, matching the cut to what its market supplies.
- Buying a national index fund while it is being repriced can no longer leave it with less cash than it owes. If the price moves under you, the purchase is refused with a request to try again.
