- A background country's decision to join a war, or to change a tariff, is now confirmed only after it has taken effect. If that step fails it is retried on the next update instead of being recorded as done.
- Worlds started from any other date, and the live world, behave exactly as before.

### Behind-the-scenes split of large turn and corporation files

The code behind the bond turn, the corporation turn, election results, NPP
company decisions and investing, the producer market, bank treasury trading,
sovereign bonds, economic vital signs and the sector detail page was split into
smaller files. The turn does the same work in the same order, and every number
and screen is unchanged. A new check also stops other large files from growing
any further.

### Behind-the-scenes tidy-up of screen code

Repeated button, table and form code on several admin, party, corporation and
landing screens was folded into shared pieces, and a few unreachable display
branches were removed. Screens, numbers and wording are unchanged.

### Bell notifications open the item you clicked

#### What changed

- Clicking a row in the bell dropdown opens the inbox with that notification already selected.
- On phones and tablets the mark read and delete buttons are always visible in the bell dropdown. On desktop they still appear when you hover or tab to a row.

### Bill page no longer shows Expired on open votes

#### What changed

- The second chamber vote, the floor vote and the top-of-page vote tile now show turns remaining, then Awaiting result once the closing turn arrives, the same as the nomination page.
- Before, they counted down a clock time and flipped to Expired in red even while the vote was still open.

### Budget balance now uses the same GDP as the debt ratio

#### What changed

- The national budget balance (surplus or deficit as a share of GDP) and the same figure shown on sector pages now divide by the smoothed GDP, the same basis the debt-to-GDP ratio already used.
- Reported growth is unchanged: it stays an annual real rate, applied one turn at a time. New long-run checks confirm it stays consistent over decades.

### Campaign income and costs follow the real economy

#### What changed

- Regions used to be measured against a fixed national average from the start of the world. As the whole economy grew over a long game, every region drifted to the highest or lowest setting.
- Income and action costs now use the current national average, so a richer region stays moderately richer than a poorer one for the whole game.
- A fresh world starts with exactly the same numbers as before.

### Capacity economy rules moved out of constants

The capacity economy formulas moved from the constants folder into a portable
rules module, with the old import path still working. A new architecture check
keeps functions from being added to constants and lets the count only fall.

### Code comment cleanup

#### What changed

- Old notes in the corporation, database, constants, military and election code were shortened so they explain why the rules work the way they do instead of how they used to work.
- No gameplay, numbers or screens change.

### Code comment cleanup

#### What changed

- Old history and ticket references were removed from explanatory notes across many game systems, leaving short notes that explain why the code works the way it does.
- No gameplay, balance or screens change.

### Code comment cleanup, part three

#### What changed

- Old history and ticket references were removed from explanatory notes across many game systems, leaving short notes that explain why the code works the way it does.
- No gameplay, balance or screens change.

### Conflicts and five more systems are now always on

#### What changed

- Conflicts is a permanent part of the game. The World menu always links to the Conflicts pages, and foreign affairs, intelligence, doctrine, peace offers, truces and war declarations no longer depend on a world setting.
- Character stats, international bloc alignment and the Influence tab, subsidiary corporations, US House districts and detailed demographic positions were already on in the live world and in every new world, so play is unchanged for them.
- Single-player setup no longer offers these as toggles. They are always on in local worlds too.
- The admin feature gates panel, the corporations panel, the world admin tab and the Conflicts admin page no longer show switches for them.

### Corporation founding moved into its own tested module

Founding a corporation still checks the same rules, charges the same amounts
and refunds you the same way if something goes wrong part way. The work behind
it is now easier to test directly, which makes future fixes to founding safer.

### Country code comments tidied

#### What changed

- Developer-only notes in the country code were shortened and cleaned up. No gameplay, numbers or screens are affected.

### Court reversal recovery no longer double-credits or loses money

#### What changed

- The treasury credit for a recovered compensation is now applied at most once. If the game is interrupted right after the credit, resuming no longer pays the treasury a second time.
- When a reversed taking has no surviving former owner and the asset is spun out to a new company, the recovery is now recorded before the spin-out starts. An interruption between the spin-out and the record no longer loses the recovery.

### Court reversals recover compensation reliably

#### What changed

- Whole-company takings now record the buyout against each sector the state kept, so a court reversal can claw it back from whoever ends up holding the asset.
- A sector that was taken more than once keeps a record of every taking. A reversal hands each one back in turn and recovers each compensation, instead of seeing only the latest.
- Recovering compensation is now written down before any money moves and finished afterwards. If the game is interrupted part-way, the recovery resumes on the next court turn instead of being lost.
- Corporate bond defaults, restructures and refinances now stop with an error if a bank treasury ever holds one of those bonds, rather than silently skipping the bank. Banks cannot hold corporate bonds today.

### Crises grow out of the economy and spread into it

#### What changed

- Six kinds of crisis can open from conditions in the game: a recession, a banking panic, a government debt crisis, a jobs shock in one region, an energy shortage and a wave of public unrest. Each opens only after the warning sign has lasted several turns in a row.
- How bad a crisis gets now follows the economy: unemployment, slow growth, inflation, bank stress, borrowing costs, debt and public mood. Higher spending on welfare and social security, and a lower central bank rate, make a recession less severe.
- A crisis now hits other parts of the game: factories produce less, energy supply and demand shift, banks come under panic, investors demand more to lend to the government, people move away from the worst-hit regions, and the government loses approval and votes.
- A crisis applies its effects once per turn, even if a turn is retried.
- Nothing changes in a live world until the crisis system is switched on.

### Crisis decisions can no longer be paid or applied twice

#### What changed

- Contributing to a shared crisis fund claims your slot first, so two contributions at once no longer erase each other and a double click cannot charge your treasury twice. If the payment fails, the slot is released.
- Choosing an option or declining aid is claimed before any cost, effect, bill or court case follows, so a double click runs it once.
- An aid pledge now reserves your country's pledge before spending or applying anything.
- A crisis whose shared response fails to resolve stays open and retries next turn, while other expiring crises close as normal.
- A crisis decision whose step has vanished from its tree now closes as unanswered instead of getting stuck, and one broken decision no longer stops the rest from resolving.
- Treasury spending now adjusts the balance in place instead of writing back a stale total.

### Crisis engine groundwork: crises that evolve each turn

#### What changed

- A new turn phase steps every crisis that carries the new crisis state: it reads unemployment, growth and bank stress, applies the levers used since the last turn, moves the crisis between stages and writes the result.
- Each turn it records who held an office with a lever available and did nothing, next to who acted.
- A load director decides how many crises of each size can run at once, so a world gets many small crises, the occasional national one and a rare global one.
- A crisis that fails is skipped and logged, and the rest of the turn's crises still run.
- No existing crisis carries the new state, so the phase does nothing and live crises behave exactly as before.

### Crisis responses and aftermath fixes

#### What changed

- The list of responses you can pull during a crisis could come back empty, and pulling a valid response could be refused, until the game server had run its own crisis turn. Responses now load on their own every time.
- Foreign aid and shared contributions to another country's crisis are now offered to eligible leaders of other countries, matching what the game already let them do when they acted.
- Approval changes and voting shifts that follow a crisis, and the strikes or bank runs it sets off, are now kept as owed until they are fully applied. If something fails partway, the next turn finishes the job without applying the same change twice.

### Eight parliaments, China and Brazil run elections from the shared description

#### What changed

- The lower houses of France, Italy, Spain, Sweden, Turkey, Greece, Austria and Finland, China's National People's Congress, provincial congresses and regional governors, and Brazil's Chamber, Senate and regional governors now come from one shared election description, including how snap elections move the next regular vote.
- A region with no seat count in its seed data now fails loudly instead of quietly getting one seat. Every era start was checked and none is missing a value.
- Snapshot tests for every era start (1953 to 2027) confirm the same elections, dates and seats as before. The one difference: after an election resolves, China and Brazil regional governor races now wait one turn before the next one spawns, like every other office.

### Election resolution code moved into the elections folder

#### What changed

- The election resolution modules and their tests moved into the shared elections folder.
- Old import paths still work and point at the moved files. Nothing changes for players.

### Election scheduling code moved into the elections folder

#### What changed

- The election scheduling modules and their tests moved into the shared elections folder.
- Old import paths still work and point at the moved files. Nothing changes for players.

### Election vote rules now live in one folder

#### What changed

- The election rule modules that were split across three folders now sit together, so a fix to how votes are counted has one place to look.
- Old import paths still work and point at the moved files. Nothing changes for players.
- A new check stops fresh election code from being added to the old folders.

### Era progression, sector tech trees and the Cold War are now always on

#### What changed

- Era progression, which covers the decade crossings, era-aware scoring and cabinet and metric changes by year, always runs.
- Sector technology trees are always available: the Tech tab, unlocks, frontier programmes and the build and growth effects.
- Nuclear programmes, the covert track and Cold War tension no longer check a world switch.
- Play is unchanged in the live world and in new worlds, since all three were already on.

### Every bondholder is paid when a defaulted company restructures or closes

#### What changed

- A defaulted bond restructure now repays index funds, autonomous parties and the public bond market in full, not only players and companies.
- Closing a company over a bond default now pays funds and autonomous parties their recovery share. Bonds a company holds of its own debt cancel out instead of taking a share from other lenders.
- Refinancing defaulted bonds keeps fund and autonomous party holdings in the replacement bond. Before, those units vanished.
- State-owned companies now pay the interest on their bonds from their own treasury. A shortfall shows up as a negative balance, as it already did for repayments at maturity.

### Fewer lost payouts in caucus tax, share splits and buyouts

#### What changed

- If a caucus cannot receive its tax, the tax already collected from members is given back, and the other caucuses are still processed.
- Stakes held by political parties are now kept when a company splits or merges its shares, and are passed on correctly when a company that holds shares in another is wound up.
- Cancelling a take-private vote can no longer refund the reserved cash twice if it is clicked twice or races with the vote closing.

### Fifty-four new American events for the 2027 world

#### What changed

- Thirty-six new events are tied to a decade: twelve each for 2027 to 2029, the 2030s and the 2040s. They cover shutdown standoffs, redistricting, court rulings, tariffs, grid emergencies, automation layoffs, water pacts, Senate rules fights and more.
- Eighteen regional stories run from 2027 onward, including Gulf hurricanes and rebuilding contracts, Western wildfire smoke, Colorado River water, Rust Belt automation, Sun Belt housing and heat, Atlantic sea walls and flood insurance, Plains drought, Pacific Northwest ports, Great Lakes algae, Alaskan thaw, Hawaiian tourism limits, Mississippi low water and a border trade bridge.
- Some events are for officeholders, some for company heads and some for everyone. Every choice has a spread of outcomes, and a passive option is always available.
- These events appear only for American characters. Worlds started from any other date, and the live world, behave exactly as before.

### Five busy endpoints split into focused modules

The commodity detail page, cabinet position briefings, cabinet bill listing and
proposals, caucus whip orders and the site-wide search behave exactly as before.
Their logic now lives in smaller, separately tested modules, which makes future
fixes to them safer.

### Germany, Japan and the upper chambers run from a shared description

#### What changed

- The Bundestag and Landtage, Japan's lower and upper houses (including the two staggered halves of the upper house and snap elections), and the beta senates are now described as data and run by one shared spawner.
- Snapshot tests for every era start (1953 to 2027) confirm the same elections, dates and seats as before.
- A region that is missing its seat count or Landtag cycle date now stops the election check with a clear error. It no longer quietly gets one seat or is skipped.
- Fix: a German election that finished this turn no longer starts its next cycle in the same turn. It starts on the next one, as in every other country.

### Government debt walls stay small

#### What changed

- Refinancing matured government bonds no longer multiplies the number of bonds. Each maturity turn adds at most one replacement bond per country and coupon, so the next maturity turn settles quickly instead of growing every cycle.
- A shared rollover bond next to a normally scheduled bond of the same coupon and turn is no longer reported as a split series.
- Debt, interest owed and what each holder is paid are unchanged.

### Groundwork for the crisis system rework

Crises are being rebuilt as one system with stages (early signal, onset, peak,
aftermath, resolved), a pressure level that rises and falls with the economy
and with what officeholders do, and a record of who acted and who did not.
This step adds the rules and tests for that system. Nothing is switched on, so
every crisis in the game plays out exactly as before.

### Inbox Mark all read works on long lists

- Mark all read sends one request for notifications and one for mail, instead of one per item, so a long unread list no longer trips the per-minute request limit.
- Notifications and mail each accept a list of ids for marking read in a single request, capped at 100 per request. The inbox splits longer lists automatically.
- If the server still asks the inbox to slow down, a short wait is retried once; a longer wait shows "Too many requests right now" instead of a generic failure, and the unread state is restored.

### Income tax is only taken from income you received

#### What changed

- Income that is taxed as it is paid (savings interest in any currency, deposit interest, salary, dividends including ones diverted to a credit line, bond coupons and index fund dividends) never leaves a separate tax bill behind.
- Share sales are taxed when the sale happens, so the proceeds cannot be spent before the bill arrives.
- Automatic checks now run three years of turns for a player with empty wallets and confirm no unpaid tax appears, and that income not yet taxed at source is still billed.

### Inflation no longer inflates real capital

#### What changed

- Corporate growth spending and public capital spending are adjusted for the country's price level before they add to its capital stock. Two otherwise identical economies with different price levels now build the same real capital.
- Worlds without accumulated inflation behave exactly as before.

### Internal type-safety cleanup

#### What changed

- Some turn and corporation code now reads data with stricter internal typing instead of forced type conversions. Gameplay, numbers and results are unchanged.

### Internal type-safety cleanup, part 2

#### What changed

- Share trading, corporation cash settlement and market share code now use stricter internal typing instead of forced type conversions. Gameplay, numbers and results are unchanged.

### Ireland and Nigeria elections run from a shared description

#### What changed

- Ireland's Dáil, President and local councils, and Nigeria's House, Senate, governors, State Houses of Assembly and President, are now described as data: seats, cycle, voting system and region source. One shared spawner runs them.
- Snapshot tests for every era start (1953 to 2027) confirm the same elections, dates and seats as before.
- A new check stops the number of country-code branches outside the country folders from growing.

### Keep player votes voluntary

#### What changed

- Player whips are advisory on every vote type across national and state/regional chambers in every country.
- Hard player whip requests are rejected, including requests that omit the mode.
- The bill page sends advisory player whips and keeps hard NPP whips available.
- Existing hard-whip history and vote-revert snapshots remain available; old hard whips do not consume the advisory attempt for any target.

### Late breakthroughs depend on other industries

#### What changed

- Space Resources mining opens only after the world's logistics companies have put enough research into the 2039 and later tiers.
- Autonomous Districts in real estate needs the same from technology and energy companies, on top of its own sector's research.
- A method waiting on other industries shows "waiting on research in other sectors" in the production method picker.
- Computer-run companies follow the same rule when they retool or found new businesses.

### Late-game years are covered by a safety check

#### What changed

- Added an automatic check that steps the clock through 2027 to 2060 and confirms eras, research tiers, score bands and election scheduling keep working past 2050.
- Portrait photo years from the 2030s onward are covered by the same check.

### Make regional referendum campaigns easier to find

#### What changed

- Active campaigns show a clear title, status badge and prominent link on the matching region page.
- The callout is hidden when the region has no active campaign.

### Market step no longer stops a turn early

- A fixed three-minute limit on preparing the market stopped a turn that the longer phase limit would have allowed. The limit is now three quarters of the phase limit.
- If the limit is ever reached, the failure message now lists how long each stage took.

### New worlds only start from data written for their start date

#### What changed

- A new world no longer quietly fills a gap with another start date's data. Before anything is reset, the game checks every start date's regions, budgets, exchange rates, alignments, election dates and seat rosters, and stops with one list of everything that needs writing.
- Budgets and regional totals are also checked against the start date's own economy: spending and debt as a share of GDP, costs per person against income, regional totals against the national figures, currencies that were in use at the time, and prices against the era's price level.
- The 2027 world has its own opening alignments for every nation, reflecting changes since 2023 such as Finland and Sweden in NATO and Syria after the fall of its government.
- Brazil's five regions in the 2027 world have their own 2027 population and economy figures.
- Austria, Greece, Finland and Ukraine now name their politicians from their own name lists.
- Some start dates cannot be started until their missing data is written. Worlds that are already running, including the live world, keep playing exactly as before.

### Opening an inbox item no longer jumps to another one

#### What changed

- Opening an unread item marks it read. In Needs action and busy inboxes that
  used to remove it from the list and switch the reading pane to another item.
  The item you opened now stays selected until you open something else.

### Own-site market-value booking reaches companies without a transfer price

#### What changed

- Booking own-site deliveries at market value (the stage-3 captive market
  step) is opt-in per company plan, but no code ever set the opt-in flag, so
  the booking produced no entries for any company.
- Every company now opts in except one on Free transfer pricing, which still
  books nothing. Companies with an explicit Cost or Market transfer price keep
  their own entries as before.
- No cash moved before and none moves now; this only affects site account
  entries once the stage switch turns on.

### Page and route cleanup

#### What changed

- Removed unused code from the feedback page, the United Kingdom region page, the map overview, the party analytics tab, the Google account link flow and the admin primary resolver.
- No gameplay, layout or response changes.

### Party budget estimates now match treasury currency; several stuck items close properly

#### What changed

- Party percent budgets (turnout, suppression, registration) estimated
  per-turn income in anchor units while treasuries hold local campaign money,
  so non-US parties spent the wrong share of income: far too little in
  high-denomination currencies like JPY and NGN, too much where the local
  rate is under 1. Estimates now use the same conversion and population base
  as the deposits.
- A defence contract whose supplier plant or company no longer exists is
  torn up for cause, releasing its held budget and quota instead of stalling
  forever.
- A committee proposal whose party was deleted, or a merge proposal whose
  target party was deleted, now lapses instead of staying open and blocking
  new proposals.
- Takeover offers past their expiry turn are stamped expired and stop
  showing as pending in the Deals view.
- Czechoslovakia's Chamber of Nations office key joins the multi-seat set
  with its siblings.
- One poisoned primary race or corporation vote can no longer abort the
  whole batch for that turn; a failed bond phase no longer silently
  vanishes the federation compensation phase; and a finished defence
  contract no longer keeps its plant out of off-type sales.

### Party leaders and heads of government now step down between terms

#### What changed

- Leaders and heads of government retire in the stretch just before their term ends, at the same overall rate as before. Very old leaders (85 and over) can still step down mid-term, and a death can happen at any time.
- When a head of government or national leader dies or retires, the news feed posts the departure and their party seats a successor.
- Backbench retirements are unchanged and post no news.
- Worlds without NPP turnover switched on behave exactly as before.

### Party whip orders moved into their own tested module

Issuing a whip on a bill, a leadership race, a government vote, a nomination,
a motion to vacate or an impeachment still checks the same rules, limits you
to the same number of attempts and tells your party members the same things.
The work behind it is now easier to test directly, which makes future fixes to
whipping safer.

### Pause Government Confidence while it is reworked

- Government Confidence defaults to Frozen. The Admin kill-switch board can resume it without a deployment.
- Frozen gauges keep their values and history. Automatic drift, confidence events, formation boosts and post-snap resets pause, along with gauge-triggered dissolution.
- Normal parliamentary no-confidence votes, government formation and public approval continue. Resuming does not replay confidence changes missed while frozen.
- The operator's switch setting survives world resets.

### Plants selling at a loss cut back instead of running until the money runs out

#### What changed

- A plant never planned to make less than it sold last turn. A plant selling
  every unit below its running cost therefore kept running in full, its company
  lost money every turn, and when the cash ran out every plant it owned stopped
  at once. Car makers did this when car prices fell sharply.
- At the next step of the switch, a plant whose units sold for less than their
  inputs, wages and site costs makes about a tenth less each turn, even below
  last turn's sales, until it pays its way again.
- Plants that sell at a profit keep the same guarantee.

### Plants with a pile of unsold goods make less until it clears

#### What changed

- A plant never plans to make less than it sold last turn. A plant that sold
  what it made but also carried a large pile of unsold goods therefore kept
  running in full, and the pile kept growing. Natural gas reached about 160,000
  unsold units, growing every turn, and prices kept falling.
- At the next step of the switch, stock held beyond one turn of sales is taken
  off that minimum. The plant sells from its pile and makes less until the pile
  is back to about a turn of sales.
- Plants without a pile keep the same guarantee.

### Preserve Scotland and Wales economic accounts at independence

- Existing household and operating-payment cash moves to successor wallets without a new endowment. Outstanding invoices follow those wallets, including invoices owed by foreign companies.
- Resource capacity and depletion follow the same placement plan as extraction plants. Resources without an installed producer retain the existing GDP-based allocation.
- Funding amounts and settlement records divide rather than duplicate. Scotland and Wales continue regional-budget processing after independence.
- Fractional demographic cohorts and optional housing stocks are conserved. New national metric histories begin at independence, fiscal values use the successor budget, and empty opening armed forces have zero readiness. Inherited estimates are labelled as proxies.
- Enacted regional service laws become national laws where the same family is offered nationally, taking precedence over inherited UK choices. Other national laws carry over. Missing legal references use the reviewed opening defaults without creating new appropriations.
- Promoted laws use the existing national funding envelope. Their separate regional requests are released, protected national transfers remain protected, and funds are not granted twice.

### President, Vice President and Incumbent achievements for every route into office

#### What changed

- A nominee confirmed as Vice President now earns the Vice President achievement the moment they are seated. Before, only an election win did.
- A Vice President who succeeds to the presidency now earns the President achievement.
- The Incumbent ("Won 3 elections") now counts the elections in your career history, so winning three governor races in a row counts even though you hold only one governor seat at a time.

### Presidential election engine moved into the elections folder

#### What changed

- The presidential election engine and its tests moved into the shared elections folder.
- Old import paths still work and point at the moved files. Nothing changes for players.

### Retired feature switches that were always on

#### What changed

- Granular poll breakdowns, the granular electorate in primaries, corporate deals, intelligence operations and military sabotage effects, computer-run countries starting and joining offensives, department finance, law administration, regional legislation finance, extraction strategy adoption, live election results and the trade-exposure embargo model are now permanent parts of the game.
- These systems were already switched on in the live world and in every new world, so play is unchanged.
- Single-player setup no longer offers these as toggles. They are always on in local worlds too.
- The admin feature gates panel and the conflicts admin page no longer show switches for them.
- A region's political metrics page now applies regional law delivery the same way the turn does.

### Retired more feature switches that were always on

#### What changed

- Crisis decisions, crisis aid bills, scheduled world events, automatic sector seeding, the new-player onboarding checklist, the end-of-season recap, legislation shifting voter leanings and turnout, macro growth convergence and settlement crises are now permanent parts of the game.
- These systems were already switched on in the live world and in every new world, so play is unchanged.
- Single-player setup no longer offers these as toggles. They are always on in local worlds too.
- The admin feature gates panel, the crises admin page, the sector seed panel and the German question admin page no longer show switches for them.

### Retired voter archetype wording cleaned up

Leftover wording from the retired voter archetype system now says voter groups.
An unused one-off data script for the old UK archetypes was also removed.

### Sector run card counts unsold units in its per-unit profit

#### What changed

- The run card's per-unit bar used to divide revenue by units sold but costs by
  units made, so a sector with unsold output looked more profitable per unit
  than it was. Costs now divide by units sold, and the card says when part of
  what you made did not sell.

### Shared access to the busiest game records

#### What changed

- The hourly turn now reaches the ten most used kinds of game record through one shared entry point each, instead of naming them by hand in hundreds of places. Future fixes to how those records are loaded or saved can be made once.
- An automatic check stops new code from bypassing those entry points, so the number of direct reads can only go down from here.
- Gameplay, balances and turn results are unchanged.

### Shared code for repeated screens and lists

The navigation menus, approval breakdowns, election result tables, party
election panels, no-confidence controls, portfolio and budget charts, US state
names and several staff tools each carried their own copy of the same code. They
now share one version. Screens, numbers and wording are unchanged.

### Shared code for staff and account requests

Removing a linked Google, Apple or Discord sign-in, uploading a feedback or
suggestion screenshot, accepting or declining a union presidency or central
bank chair offer, and the staff activity log, suspicious list and Patreon search
each had two or three copies of the same code. They now share one version.
Responses, limits and error messages are unchanged.

### Shareholder payouts, double clicks and party ballots behave correctly

#### What changed

- When a company was dissolved, defaulted, bought out or nationalised, the
  slice owed to NPP shareholders was taken from the payer but paid to no
  one. NPP shareholders are now paid.
- Group loss relief now takes the money from the treasury before paying the
  company, in the treasury's own currency, so a refused payment no longer
  leaves the company paid anyway.
- Double clicks or two people acting at once can no longer apply an event
  choice, a coalition invite or join
  acceptance, a CEO offer acceptance or a growth change twice.
- Treasury spending no longer loses a concurrent change to the balance.
- Expired share listings return every seller's shares even if one return
  fails; finished prospect surveys can no longer
  grant capacity twice; and one bad international organisation item no
  longer skips dues and expiries for the turn.
- Event outcomes that add or remove campaign funds now reach the campaign's
  spendable money.
- Merged parties stop opening leadership and committee races, a retired
  character can no longer be confirmed to a cabinet, court or central bank
  seat, and extraction contracts held by a company that no longer exists
  now close.
- Committee ballots stay closed during the new-member waiting period, union
  president ballots stay closed when your vote would be refused, and new
  members can run and vote in founding caucus chair races.
- Shares held by non-player politicians are
  paid out in hostile takeovers and privatization buyouts instead of
  disappearing, and a failed write while collecting caucus tax no longer
  discards the tax already taken from other members.

### Sign-in fixes for Discord and Google

#### What changed

- Creating a new account with Discord now runs the same network check that Google and Apple sign-ups already ran, when that check is switched on.
- If something goes wrong while signing in with Discord or Google, you now land on the usual sign-in error page instead of a broken response.

### Sign-in, character creation and poll endpoints split into focused modules

Signing in or linking an account with Discord, Google or Apple, creating a
character, commissioning a poll and viewing a region's economy behave exactly as
before. The three sign-in paths now share one tested flow instead of three
near-copies, which makes future fixes to them safer.

### Single player statistics accept reports from older versions

#### What changed

- Older single player versions still report a few game settings that this
  release removed. Their statistics used to be rejected as a whole; now the
  retired settings are simply ignored and the rest of the report is kept.

### Source comment cleanup for pages and components

#### What changed

- Explanatory notes in the page, API route and component code were shortened to say why the code works the way it does. Old history notes were removed.
- No gameplay, screens or numbers change.

### Starting data checked before a world is created

#### What changed

- The starting political scores for background countries and the weights that link voter groups to census groups now live in data files instead of program code.
- Both files are checked before a world is set up: every score must be between 0 and 100, every listed country must be present, and each voter group's weights must add up to one. A bad value now stops the setup with a clear message instead of reaching a world.
- The values themselves are unchanged, so existing worlds and new worlds play exactly as before.

### Stricter checks before a world reset

#### What changed

- Before a reset writes anything, every state of every playable country in the chosen start date must carry a real, positive GDP and population. A missing figure stops the reset with a list of what is missing.
- Resets are also checked against a fixture holding every leftover seen in past resets, so none of them can survive into a new world.
- A single report now lists every collection, preserved field and seed dataset with what a reset does to it, and fails if anything is unclassified.
- Live worlds are unchanged.

### Succession, caretaker governments and filled by-elections

#### What changed

- Each country now has a succession rule for its leader: who acts, for how long, and whether a deputy, an election, a legislature vote or a party vote fills the job. Brazil and Nigeria promote the vice president for the rest of the term.
- After a dissolution the outgoing government is a caretaker government until the new parliament produces a result. It cannot introduce new major bills, and the government page says so.
- A government page also shows who is acting while a prime minister is being chosen.
- If nobody files for a by-election, a candidate from the seat's previous party stands, so the seat is not left empty.

### Surprise Supreme Court cases wait on the docket before they are decided

#### What changed

- A surprise case no longer rules the same week it appears. It shows as pending on the docket and is heard between twelve and thirty-six weeks later.
- The ruling follows the justices sitting on the day of the hearing, not the day of filing, so a vacancy filled in between can change the result.
- Curated historical cases and crisis challenges are unchanged.

### Take-private buyouts no longer pay funds and then fail

#### What changed

- When a company's only outside holders include a political party alongside an index fund, the take-private now goes through the normal vote. Before, the fund was paid first and the buyout was then refused, leaving the company public.
- The buyout also checks every holder before moving any money.
- A government contract award stuck forever because the winning company no longer existed now reopens for other bids.

### Takeovers keep extraction contracts; court reversals recover compensation; campaign costs match income

#### What changed

- When a company is bought out or taken over, its live extraction contracts now pass to the buyer with its operations. Before, they were closed and the state capacity they reserved was lost.
- When the Supreme Court strikes down an emergency government takeover, the former owner repays the compensation to the treasury from its cash. Anything it cannot pay becomes a debt owed to that country and is repaid from later company cash.
- A court reversal of a takeover folded into land the state already held now returns only what that takeover added, not the whole holding.
- Building Campaign Presence and launching state attacks now convert their price at the same fixed rate campaign income uses, so the two can no longer drift apart when exchange rates move.
- A defence contract award whose winning company disappears mid-award now reopens for other bids instead of creating a contract for a company that no longer exists.

### The 2027 UK opens with by-elections

#### What changed

- In a 2027 world, four AI-held Commons seats open up during the first year, two through retirement and two through resignation.
- Each vacancy runs through the usual by-election: a filing window, then a vote.
- Seats held by players are never vacated this way.

### The 2027 world starts from 2027 numbers

#### What changed

- The United Kingdom, Germany, Japan, China and eleven background countries (Ireland, Brazil, Nigeria, France, Italy, Spain, Sweden, Turkey, Austria, Finland and Greece) now start a 2027 world with projected 2027 population, GDP, government spending, revenue, debt, interest, growth and inflation, instead of figures carried over from an earlier start date.
- Defence budgets for the United Kingdom, Germany, Japan and China start at their announced 2027 levels.
- Russia, Poland and Romania start with updated 2027 population and GDP.
- Regions in the United States, the United Kingdom, Germany, Japan and China start with 2027 conditions: public debt, house and office prices, renewable energy, robot use, health coverage, waiting times, child poverty, homelessness, birth rates and more.
- Turkey's provinces now add up to its 2027 population.
- Background countries are checked on their national figures only, because they do not run regional simulations.
- Worlds started from any other date, and the live world, behave exactly as before.

### The market plans production more carefully each turn

- Each turn the market works out how much every plant should make. To keep
  turns short it had time for only one pass, and thousands of plants ended a
  turn with plans still moving by a lot, which fed swings between gluts and
  shortages.
- It now has time for about four passes. Turns take about a minute longer.

### The Supreme Court keeps turning over and hearing modern cases

#### What changed

- From 2027, a seated justice with no scheduled departure can leave office at the same steady rate as a justice you confirmed (a median tenure of fifteen years). The seat opens for the President to fill.
- From 2027 the Court hears surprise cases about modern themes: algorithms and data, tenant screening, the grid, offshore wind, platforms, gig work, schooling and election rules.
- From 2027 a surprise case comes up about once every one and a half to two years, up from once every five.
- When every case for an era has been heard, a case can return twelve years after it was last heard.
- Worlds started from earlier dates keep their existing case rate.

### Turn and market code notes tidied

Developer-facing cleanup only: the notes that explain the turn, market,
settlement, ledger and economy code were shortened to say why things work the
way they do. No gameplay, numbers or behaviour changed.

### United Kingdom code consolidated and server settings centralised

#### What changed

- All United Kingdom rules (cabinet, leadership, conference, manifestos, by-elections and confidence) now sit together with the rest of the country's code. Gameplay is unchanged.
- Server settings such as sign-in providers, storage and turn tuning are now read in one place, which makes misconfiguration easier to spot. No setting names or defaults changed.

### US AI politicians stay put

#### What changed

- AI-run politicians in the United States no longer die of old age, retire or get replaced, so player parties face a stable field.
- AI-run politicians in other countries still age, retire and get replaced as before.

### Voter-group counts removed from the state demographics tab

The "Demographic categories" tile is gone from the state demographics tab. It
counted a legacy grouping that is being retired. The census and electorate
breakdowns below it are unchanged.

### Voters keep realigning through 2050

#### What changed

- From 2028, voters with degrees drift left on social questions and voters without them drift right, wherever enough adults hold degrees for the divide to show.
- From 2028, younger voters lean further toward climate action and new housing, while the oldest voters drift the other way as cohorts replace one another.
- From 2030, in any state where unemployment stays above 6.5 percent, low income and non-degree voters swing toward the populist mix of economic left and social right.
- From 2030, in any state where net migration runs above 0.8 percent a year, non-degree, middle income and older voters move toward the restrictive side.
- Your laws can push back against all of these, the same way they can against earlier realignments.
- Worlds started from earlier dates are unchanged.

### A refused construction instalment no longer freezes the lender's whole construction book

#### What changed

- If one borrower's construction instalment was refused because their cash had moved, the lender's batch for that turn could never finish. The lender's whole construction book then stayed locked, and selling or cancelling a financed sector was refused.
- Recovery now finishes the batch without the refused payment. The lender is credited only the instalments that were actually taken, and the loans of the borrowers who paid move forward.
- The refused borrower's loans are not advanced and are serviced normally on a later turn, so nobody is charged twice.
- The lender's lock and the loans' locks are then released.

### Admin emergency switches for crises, conflicts and eras work again

Switching these off in the admin panel had stopped doing anything. They are real
emergency switches again, and the panel now shows them as on unless an admin has
explicitly turned them off.

Local singleplayer worlds that were set up with Era progression, Living conflicts
or Automatic crises and disasters unticked had those boxes ignored until now. On
the next start those three are turned back on once, so an older save keeps
advancing eras and running conflicts. You can still switch them off afterwards.

### Any company with an extraction sector can run surveys there

- Surveys were limited to companies whose main or secondary business was extraction, so a company that bought an extraction sector could not survey its deposits. Any company with an extraction sector in a state can now run surveys there.
- A company with no extraction sector in the state and no extraction main or secondary business still cannot survey it.

### Approval decay runs on demographic buckets

#### What changed

- The per-turn fade of approval modifiers (0.5% a turn, about a fifth over a game year) is unchanged in size. It applies to every demographic bucket modifier a politician holds.
- The turn step is now called bucket approval decay in turn logs and diagnostics.

### Approval weighting uses the census electorate

#### What changed

- Region and national approval weight each issue by the leanings of the region's census groups at the current in-game year, rather than a stored voter-group table.
- A region with no census data keeps the plain, unweighted average.
- Missing census data is only tolerated for background countries. A playable or NPP-run country with a region that has no census now fails loudly instead of silently dropping the weighting.

### Bill approval previews use demographic buckets

#### What changed

- A policy option's authored approval effects are keyed by demographic bucket (for example `education:no_college`) instead of by voter group. No option had authored values, so previews look the same.
- The old unused "group approvals" field on policy options is gone.
- District pressure on a legislator's vote weighs a policy's bucket approvals by how much of the home state falls in each bucket.

### Bill whip count follows the chamber that is voting

#### What changed

- Once a bill moves to its second chamber, the Whip count shows that chamber's
  whips instead of only the first chamber's.

### Browser tests now run every night

#### What changed

- A new nightly workflow, also runnable by hand, builds the app, starts it against a
  temporary database and runs the Playwright specs in `e2e/`.
- The Playwright config had a placeholder where the local server address should be;
  it now points at the local server.
- The run is informational only. It does not gate pull requests or promotions.

### Candidate approval by voter bucket

#### What changed

- A politician's standing with each voter bucket now uses one name everywhere: bills, union actions, crises, polls, party founding and the hourly turn.
- Existing politicians carry their current numbers over unchanged when the world is updated.

### Canvassing and turnout targets use census groups only

#### What changed

- The canvassing panel and the party turnout pickers list the census groups of the region you are in (age, education, income and so on). The old per-country voter groups and the US ideology groups are gone.
- Outside the US, picking an old voter group used to spend money without moving any votes. That can no longer happen.
- A party that had saved an old voter group as its target has the target cleared. Nothing was prepaid, so there is nothing to refund: the party just needs to pick a census group again.
- Races that started before the current campaign rules can still be canvassed, using census groups.

### Clearer warning when building below the cash reserve

- Lines whose sales cover their running costs keep running when cash is low. The warning no longer suggests every plant slows down.

### Code hygiene counts can no longer grow unnoticed

#### What changed

- A new architecture check records, per file, how many console calls, empty catch
  blocks, eslint-disable comments, explicit any types and one-line re-export files
  exist today. Any file that goes above its recorded count fails the check.
- Counts can only be lowered, never raised, with `npm run hygiene:lower`.
- No game rules or player-facing pages changed.

### Commodity and currency rules moved out of constants

The commodity price and flow formulas and the starting exchange rate lookups
moved from the constants folder into portable rules modules, with the old
import paths still working. The tables stay in constants. The random currency
regime roll stays beside the tables because it uses a random number.

### Commodity page supply now says it includes unsold stock

#### What changed

- The supply box on the commodity page now reads "offered per day, includes unsold stock", with a tooltip explaining the figure.
- Supply counts stock that stayed unsold plus what producers plan to make now, so a glut shows up again every turn and can sit far above what is actually produced.
- The top producers list ranks capacity and the top consumers list ranks estimated plant inputs, so neither adds up to the header supply.
- No prices, supply or demand numbers changed. This is a label and tooltip only.

### Corporation, command economy and tech tree rules moved out of constants

The corporation margin, dominance and growth formulas, the command economy
marketization formulas and the tech tree read helpers moved from the constants
folder into portable rules modules, with the old import paths still working.
The tables and tuning constants stay in constants.

### Country, state, metric and sector strategy rules moved out of constants

The country config selectors, the client-safe government type predicates, the US
state seat and electoral vote selectors, the metric catalog lookups and the sector
strategy formulas moved from the constants folder into portable rules modules, with
the old import paths still working. The tables stay in constants.

### Crises spread between countries and AI governments respond

#### What changed

- Each turn a crisis checks its spread links. How much passes on depends on how strong the real tie is between the two countries: the share of trade a partner supplies, how concentrated a country's sovereign bond holdings are in another country's debt, shared organization memberships, and shared world region for migration and climate.
- A link can wait a set number of turns, only passes once the crisis is bad enough and far enough along, and either raises an existing matching crisis in the other country in small steps or opens a new one if the game's crisis load allows it.
- Each spread crisis records which crisis it came from and which it caused, so the crises map can draw the spread lines.
- Governments run by the game now answer crises at home. They pick responses by how severe the crisis is, what the treasury and approval can bear and how ambitious or stubborn their leader is, and they pull at most a handful per turn.
- Background countries never pull a response. A larger background country passes a crisis on harder than a small one.

### Crisis approval changes apply exactly once

#### What changed

- Each approval change that follows a crisis is now recorded on the approval figure it changes, in the same step. If the server restarts or a connection error hides whether a change landed, the retry sees the record and does not apply it again.

### Crisis responses by office: levers, costs and emergency bills

#### What changed

- Each crisis role now resolves to real office holders: heads of state, governors, legislative leaders, central bank chairs, finance ministers, party chairs and corporate CEOs. Holders who had a response available and did nothing are recorded as such.
- A new action lets a holder pull a response on a crisis. It checks the office, the crisis stage and a cooldown, then charges the cost: treasury cash, corporation cash, and approval. A double click charges once, and a failed charge is refunded.
- A legislature response raises an emergency bill with a short voting window. If it passes, the cost is paid and the response counts. If it fails, the sponsor is recorded as having failed to act.
- A lookup lists the crises where the viewer has a response available right now, ready for the crises page and a nav badge.
- Political capital costs are paid in the holder's own actions for the turn (one to three, by the size of the response).
- The turn phase now claims each crisis's turn before applying its effects, so a failed save can no longer apply the same effects twice.

### Crisis turn reads the region list once

#### What changed

- The crisis step shares one region lookup with the crisis watchers each turn instead of reading it again.

### Crisis wiki pages rewritten for the new crisis system

#### What changed

- The Crises page covers what a crisis is, the four stages, what raises and lowers severity, the do-nothing forecast, the limit on how many crises run at once, spread between countries and the crises page.
- A new Crisis Responses page covers which office can pull which response, what a response costs in treasury, company cash, approval and actions, the fast-track emergency bill, and the Who did what record.
- The Global Response Crises page now points at World Crises, where the conflicts page moved.
- The unused decision-flow diagram from the old page is removed.

### Debt spirals, spilled crises and canvassing costs now work as designed

#### What changed

- A background country whose debt interest stays above half of its revenue for two fiscal years now restructures its debt, with the haircut, market lockout and news that other countries already got. Before, its counter was reset and nothing happened.
- A crisis that spreads from one country to another now carries the length and effects of the crisis it becomes, so it runs its course and ends instead of sitting at its peak for ever without doing anything.
- Canvassing now costs the same share of income in every era. In early-era worlds it was charged at the modern price, many times the intended cost.
- Running the bucket approval data migration after the new build has started no longer overwrites approval changes made since deploy.

### Election engine reads buckets only

#### What changed

- Candidate approval modifiers in vote counting are now handled as bucket approvals throughout the election engine.
- Primary carve-up rows no longer carry a demographic-style tag; the UK Commons carve-up keeps showing the party name.

### Elections count census groups only

#### What changed

- General elections, presidential races and primaries all take their electorate and turnout from the region's census groups. A region with no census data now stops the count with a clear error instead of quietly using an older voter-group table.
- Primary projections and the primary state page use the same census groups as the live wave, so the board matches what the count produces.
- Nationwide ad bonuses that were spread over the old voter groups are removed with them.

### Elections count voters by census group only

#### What changed

- Turnout boosts and suppression outside the United States now move the census groups they target (income, age, region and so on), as they already did in the United States. The old whole-state adjustment based on legacy voter groups is gone.
- The median voter on a general election page, including the electoral-vote weighted national median for a presidential race, is now computed from the census electorate that counts the votes.
- No setting or action changes. Worlds that never used the old voter-group turnout targets see no difference.

### Every new crisis now runs on the Crisis v2 model

#### What changed

- Each authored crisis is now a scripted Crisis v2 definition: same effects at the same size, the same length (including the cuts earned by aid and decisive responses), and its decision options offered as responses for the office that makes them. Crises are no longer one-way snapshots: easing one with responses scales its effects down.
- Per-turn "decay" effects are skipped by the turn exactly as before; other systems still read them off the crisis.
- Long-running conflicts get a crisis of their own that tracks the conflict's intensity through the stages and closes when the conflict settles. Their rules for civilian loss, destroyed capacity, refugee reception and trade sanctions now exist as crisis effect modules, and each conflict names the crises it spills into. Those new effect kinds are not routed to the game systems yet, so they change nothing in play today.
- New crises are opened under a budget by reach (local, regional, national, global) instead of a flat two-per-country limit. A regional disaster now costs far less budget than a national crisis, so disasters no longer crowd national events out. The held-back "deferred spawn" marker is retired.
- Crises that were already running when this ships are not migrated. The old turn code for them stays, marked for removal once none are left.

### Faster producer market step in the hourly turn

#### What changed

- The saved record of each producer market round is built without copying rows that need no change, and is compressed faster.
- Repeated lookups while orders clear are computed once.
- No gameplay or price changes.

### Faster turns with crisis detection on

#### What changed

- Each turn the crisis watchers read their running counts together instead of one watcher at a time. Crises open and close exactly as before.

### Faster turns: fewer database round trips for index funds and the Treasury

#### What changed

- Each hourly index fund pass now saves every fund's new NAV and backing ratio in
  one batched write instead of one write per fund. The stored values are the same.
- The Treasury phase checks that every country has a federal budget with one read
  instead of one read per country. Missing budgets are still created the same way.

### Founding and charter fees reach spendable Treasury cash

#### What changed

- The incorporation fee for a subsidiary spin-off, the premium for founding a
  company in a low-confidence economy, and the index fund charter fee now land
  in spendable Treasury cash as one recorded payment that cannot apply twice.
- Previously these fees raised the Treasury's headline balance but not the cash
  it could actually spend.

### General profiles no longer show a branch

#### What changed

- The Branch tile is gone from the general profile, and the Commissioned entry
  in the service record no longer names a branch.
- Generals have no branch of their own. The defence holder can give a general
  units of any branch, so the old label (Army for most, Navy for naval) could
  mislead players into thinking a general could be moved between branches.

### Government fines are paid straight into Treasury cash

#### What changed

- A fine or assessment now takes the cash from the company and puts it in the
  Treasury together, or does neither.
- Companies that cannot afford a fine are still retried next turn.

### Guard against new voter archetype code

#### What changed

- A new architecture check counts mentions of voter archetypes in the source and fails if a file gains any. Estate, infrastructure, military, CEO, career and governing archetypes are exempt because they are not voter groups.

### Identify independence consent bills as referendum results

- Scotland and Wales independence consent bills now show "Referendum passed", matching reunification consent bills, instead of "Admin proposed".

### Internal type-safety cleanup, part 3

#### What changed

- Country merge, settlement, party merge and military lookup code now reads database records with stricter internal typing instead of forced type conversions. Gameplay, numbers and results are unchanged.

### Japanese laws now move regional lean on the live world

#### What changed

- Japanese laws already carried lean effects in the game's definitions, but the live world's stored laws did not have them yet. They are now written once when the game starts up.
- From the next turn after that, passed Japanese bills and governor orders shift a region's lean.
- Only existing Japanese laws are updated. Nothing else is touched.

### Lean map breakdown shows census groups in every country

#### What changed

- The lean breakdown under the map reads the same census groups the vote counts, so it now works for every country and uses that country's own group names.
- Regions without a census show the usual "no demographic data" message.

### Lighter automated check memory use

#### What changed

- The fake database used by automated checks now builds its pieces only when a check actually uses them.
- Large checks no longer need special workarounds to avoid running out of memory.
- Nothing changes for players.

### Map lean reads the census electorate

#### What changed

- The map's lean colours use a region's stored lean when there is one, and otherwise the lean of its census electorate at the current in-game year.
- A region with neither a stored lean nor census data now reports an error instead of showing as neutral.

### Military model, cabinet estates and infrastructure, exchange and seat rules moved out of constants

The military unit model formulas, the cabinet estate and infrastructure project
lookups, the stock exchange registry lookups and the historical seat preset
selectors moved from the constants folder into portable rules modules, with the
old import paths still working. The tables stay in constants.

### More reliable automated checks

#### What changed

- The world reset check no longer uses up all available memory when it runs on its own, so it stops crashing before it can report anything.
- The campaign map zoom check no longer occasionally fails by acting before the map is ready.
- Nothing changes for players.

### National contests count every region's census groups

#### What changed

- A nationwide contest builds its electorate from every region's census groups, weighted by how many eligible voters each region has, instead of a stored national summary.
- Regional ad buys still reach only the voters in the region they target, and now do so group by group within a national contest.
- A nationwide contest in a country with a region missing census data now reports an error instead of quietly using a stored summary.

### New laws for 2045 and 2050, and more laws retire

#### What changed

- From 2045, legislatures can pass a longevity care act, a fossil fuel sunset act, a digital self-ownership act and a water security act.
- From 2050 they can also pass an advanced AI oversight act, a lifelong pension account act, a zero carbon economy act and a space resources act.
- The United States, Britain, Russia and East Germany get the new laws as program laws. Germany, Japan, China and Ireland get them as seven-option bills.
- Every law has winners and payers. Long-term care helps older people and families but costs taxpayers. A fossil fuel sunset cleans the air but raises bills. Strong AI oversight builds trust but slows research.
- Seven older laws close to new bills when their successor opens: the automation tax, the coal power phase-out, the data protection overhaul, the basic income pilot, the AI liability act, pension age indexation and the clean power standard. A law already in force stays in force with its costs and effects.
- All new laws start with no statute in force, so nothing changes until a bill passes.

### New worlds start with the latest AI politicians

#### What changed

- A brand new world now starts AI-run countries on NPP autonomy v6, the same level the live world runs. Before, new worlds started one step lower.
- AI governments in a new world keep standing goals and commitments from the first turn, and seated AI politicians age, retire and are replaced.
- The live world and single-player games keep the level they already have.

### Orbital debris timing and party history coverage

#### What changed

- The orbital debris cascade crisis can no longer open before 2042. It already needed a country with satellite operators; the date now matches when those fleets first appear.
- The party trend charts record history for every country that runs the full simulation, including ones not yet marked as launched.
- Worlds before 2042 and the live world are unchanged.

### Party and coalition pages now tell you when something fails to load

#### What changed

- The party hub, coalition page, state party page, committee proposals list and the
  proposal form now show a message when data fails to load, instead of leaving the
  page blank with no explanation.
- Background progress polling and the reset console keep ignoring partial updates,
  with a note in the code explaining why.
- No game rules changed.

### Polls read the full electorate breakdown

#### What changed

- The poll's headline numbers (overall appeal, estimated voters, potential voters and the head-to-head vote totals) are now worked out from the same detailed electorate breakdown the elections use, instead of from a separate list of voter groups.
- The "top groups" and "bottom groups" lists from the old poll are gone. The breakdown explorer on the Full Poll is the way to see which parts of the electorate favour you.
- Pollster's notes read the electorate's average lean from the breakdown.
- A poll saved before this change that has no breakdown is cleared and needs to be commissioned again.
- Numbers will differ slightly from before because the model changed. In a head-to-head poll, party organisation and infamy no longer scale the total directly; they act through the breakdown shares as in the election.

### Position editor previews the census electorate

#### What changed

- The admin position editor shows each region as its census buckets (share, lean and turnout per bucket) and reads the region lean off the same electorate the elections use. Position and turnout edits move the buckets; share edits move the lean of their own dimension.
- The editor's CSV export lists census buckets instead of voter-group rows.
- The voter-group category tables are no longer seeded or read. Region pages, polls and elections already used the census, so players see no change.

### Primary projections and admin demographics read the census only

#### What changed

- Primary projections no longer fall back to old voter-group rows. Every projection builds its electorate from the region census, the same way the live primary wave does.
- The admin demographics panel is down to the reseed and overwrite-defaults tools. The old voter-group table is gone.
- The debug lean report shows the cached lean next to the lean read from the census.
- The region page shows the stored lean, and the state population figure comes from the region itself.

### Privatization sale proceeds reach Treasury cash reliably

#### What changed

- A privatization share offering or auction sale now adds its proceeds to the
  national Treasury as one recorded payment that cannot be applied twice.
- Previously, with Treasury cash tracking on, the sale could stop partway and
  leave the bidders' escrowed money unpaid to the state.

### Recruiting and upgrading units now spend the charges your office shows

#### What changed

- The office header showed your Cabinet action charges (for example 4 of 4),
  but recruiting and upgrading units drew from an older balance that could
  already be empty, so the button refused with "No ministerial actions
  remaining" while the header said otherwise.
- In countries running the new Cabinet, those actions now spend one charge from
  the same pool the header shows, and charges recharge as before.
- Other countries are unchanged.

### Region demographics no longer carry voter-group rows

#### What changed

- A region's saved demographics no longer have a voter-group table. The vote engines, approval weighting and region pages already read the census electorate, so nothing players see changes.
- The turn's non-player party logic loads less region data per turn.
- Election vote tallies no longer carry a voter-group catalogue they never used.

### Region demographics tab covers every country

#### What changed

- The region demographics tab now shows the census breakdown and electorate profile for every country, including ones that were previously left blank (such as the Baltic states).
- Behind the scenes, the per-country voter-group profile ids and the unused voter-group weight tables were removed. Canvassing options per country are unchanged.

### Resets no longer stall on a false leftover alarm

#### What changed

- After a reset, the leftover check no longer treats seeded laws, starting companies and the achievement list as old-world data.
- A clean reset now opens the world instead of staying paused.
- Real leftovers from the previous world are still caught.

### Resolve My Corporation when opened

- My Corporation links now look up the active character's current company when opened, so old navigation state cannot send players to a deleted company after an economy reset.
- Desktop, profile and phone company shortcuts use the same uncached destination. Players who no longer run a company are taken to the market.

### Run panel no longer blames a small input shortage for large idle capacity

#### What changed

- When a plant that already runs well below capacity is also held back by one
  short input, the Inputs note counts only the output that input removed.
- The rest of the gap to capacity stays with its real causes, such as staffing.

### Safety check before the 2027 world opens

#### What changed

- Before the 2027 world opens, a check confirms every country that runs in full has positive state output and population, the seat numbers its elections need, and no leftover fields from older versions.
- The same check confirms the retired always-on switches are not set off, and that the price index starts fresh.
- It never writes anything and stops the launch if any check fails.
- Running worlds and other start dates are unchanged.

### Seceded regions use their own census

#### What changed

- Each region of a seceded Scotland or Wales already has its own age, education, income, ethnicity and urban-rural profile. Voting and polling there now rely on that profile alone.
- The old step that re-sliced the national voter-group split across the new regions has been removed. Every new region starts from a copy of the national record and the regional differences come from the census.

### Seed and simulation tooling uses voter group wording

#### What changed

- Seed scripts, the audit report and the election simulation scripts now talk about voter groups and state blocs. The game itself is unchanged.

### Seeded region leans come from the census

#### What changed

- Seeding or reseeding any country sets each region's starting political lean from its census electorate, the same figure the vote engine uses.
- A region with no census electorate is now left unfilled and reported instead of being given a neutral lean.
- New seeds no longer store the old voter-group rows on region demographics.
- Some strongly Republican US states start about one to one and a half points closer to the centre than before. No region moves more than two points.

### Selling a national index fund at a moving price

#### What changed

- If a national index fund reprices while your sale is in progress, the sale is cancelled cleanly and you are asked to try again, so you are never paid at a stale price.
- Internal fix: the repricing step now confirms its own update landed, so a fund can no longer be skipped or counted twice.

### Share scarce household supply fairly across countries

- Household buyers in every country now share scarce eligible supply in proportion to outstanding demand, instead of taking turns by account or country name. Embargoes, buyer budgets, price limits and reserved-order priority still apply.

### Short inputs are marked on the sector page

#### What changed

- Each input that ran short is marked "Ran short" in the "What goes in, what comes out" panel.
- Every marked input carries a link to the sellers offering it, not just the costliest input.
- If the run stopped on inputs but no input is short on the world market, the panel now says so instead of staying silent.

### Show legislative leans and complete Scottish and Welsh tax options

#### What changed

Proposal options, provision reviews and bills now show their economic and social positions using the same rules as voting. Funding and delivery choices use economic positions; privacy and media choices show both axes where appropriate. Administrative transparency and procurement changes no longer imply an extreme social stance.

Scotland and Wales receive their own exact-rate national tax instruments after independence. Startup adds missing definitions without changing any existing tax rate or budget. Fresh worlds include the same instruments, and the Welsh picker resolves its established legislation scope.

### Special presidential elections now use two rounds

#### What changed

- A special presidential election in France, Romania or Russia (2027) now has two rounds. A candidate with more than half the votes wins outright. Otherwise the top two go to a short runoff and the larger vote wins.
- The winner takes office like any other president: the previous cabinet is cleared and the win counts as an elected term.
- When a ruling party picks a new leader, that leader starts with fresh party confidence instead of inheriting the old leader's standing.
- Votes by a legislature or ruling party only ever pick computer-run politicians, never a player.
- One-party executive pages now show who is acting while the top office is vacant.
- Russia's 1991 presidency says plainly that it is filled through its own constitutional ballot, with no special election scheduled.

### Status bar, fund redemption, relocation and executive endpoints split into focused modules

The status bar data, redeeming fund shares, relocating with a corporation, the
executive overview, whippable leadership votes and the Discord support
endpoints behave exactly as before. Their logic now lives in dedicated modules
instead of the endpoint files, which makes future fixes safer.

### Stop the Soviet debt arrears total from overflowing

- Unpaid contributions are carried forward as a balance. The running total now adds only the new shortfall each turn instead of the whole balance again.

### Successions now fill themselves

#### What changed

- France, Romania and Russia (2027) now hold a special presidential election when the presidency stays vacant after the prime minister's acting period. If nobody files, a candidate from the previous president's party stands, so the office is not left empty.
- Where a legislature or ruling party picks the leader, the largest bloc in the legislature now chooses one of its own members once the acting period ends.
- Presidential pages show who is acting, what fills the office next, and whether the succession is overdue. They also say when a government is in caretaker mode.
- A test now checks that every country in every era has a succession rule, so a new country or government type cannot ship without one.

### Supreme Court vacancies fill without a player President

#### What changed

- When a Supreme Court justice dies or retires and no player is President, the sitting President (or, with no President, the largest Senate party) now nominates a replacement and the Senate votes on it. Before, those seats stayed empty for good and the Court kept shrinking.
- One nomination is filed per turn, and a seat whose nominee was just turned down waits two game days before the next try.
- UK judicial review rulings now lean with the party actually in government, so they enact the matching law instead of always ending in a split ruling that changed nothing.
- UK judicial review no longer runs in worlds where the UK is not part of the full simulation.

### The Prospect button only shows for companies that can prospect

#### What changed

- Surveys can only be launched by a company whose main or secondary business is extraction. The Prospect button on a sector's resource panel used to show for any CEO, so a company of another type that owned an extraction sector hit a refusal.
- The button and the survey list now appear only for companies that can use them.
- The refusal now reads "Only extraction companies can prospect. Your company's main or secondary business must be extraction, and owning an extraction sector is not enough."
- Who may prospect has not changed.

### UK manifesto effects read the census electorate

#### What changed

- Manifesto pledge appeal is scored against each census group's position, the same groups used to count the vote.

### Union strikes and crackdowns hit voter buckets

#### What changed

- Resolving a union ban strike, and holding a crackdown posture, now lowers the executive's standing with the labour-leaning buckets of that country's census, or the business-leaning ones for the back-down path.
- The labour and business groups are defined per country for the US, UK and Ireland, with a low-income versus high-income split everywhere else.
- Because the groups are now real census buckets, these costs reach voters in every country instead of only where the old voter types existed.

### Use funded orders and correct market scopes in build advice

- Construction and freight build advice now reads national funded orders on funded-market worlds. Regional-only services keep their regional markets.
- Build and entry dialogs show funded orders, actual deliveries and unfilled orders for each output, with separate regional and national readings and the source turn. National readings include the region.
- Mixed-output shortages no longer produce a blanket oversupply warning. Automatic sizing remains conservative about every output, existing idle capacity and queued construction.
- Missing regional measurements are labeled as unavailable until a settled turn publishes them. International orders are excluded from the domestic estimate rather than promised without route eligibility.
- The new-market picker and local expansion now share conservative output sizing after known idle capacity and construction queues. Demand tables show building counts for each output and a separate whole-mix count, even before a new sector has operating history.
- Build popups scroll internally and keep the background page still, including while another dialog is open.

- Advice explains that buildings can profitably fill one output gap even when another output is already supplied. Delivered totals refer to buyer purchases across suppliers, not a building's production mix.

### Vacant national offices always get filled, and the wire says so

#### What changed
