- When the president and vice president seats are both empty (or the vice president is barred from the office), a special presidential election is now called after a short wait, instead of the office staying empty until the next regular election.
- A legislature or party vote that finds nobody it can pick now seats a new member of the largest party once the deadline has passed. An office awaiting a special election that came back empty is filled the same way once overdue.
- A mid-term vacancy in the 1991 Russian presidency now gets its special election once the first ballot has produced a result.
- The wire now reports when a national office falls vacant, when it is still empty past its deadline, when a special presidential election is called and who takes the office.
- Special presidential elections now show as national races with the right title on election pages, the map, the sidebar and navigation, and the acting prime minister is no longer dropped from the ballot in presidential countries.
- In a country with players, the special presidential election is left to players: computer-run politicians no longer enter it on their own or as filler candidates. In countries without players they still do, and the election now gets its usual minimum of challengers.
- Folded parties are no longer picked as the party for a by-election or challenger candidate.

### Vote engines count census units directly

#### What changed

- Vote shares, the median voter and primary turnout all read the same census electorate units.
- Results are unchanged; this removes the last stored voter-group shape from the vote engines.

### Voter drift now runs on census groups only

#### What changed

- Laws that shift how a census group votes or turns out now act only on that census group. Laws that named an old voter group instead no longer move anything.
- Newsroom slant pulls the whole electorate of a region through its census groups, by the same small step as before.
- A region's starting and running lean is now read from its census electorate in every era. Against the old voter-group figure, no region in the 2027 world moves more than two points on either axis. The largest moves are some strongly Republican US states, which start about one to one and a half points closer to the centre.
- Historical realignments and landmark laws keep moving census groups durably, as before.

### Voter groups now run on census buckets only

#### What changed

- Candidate approval, campaign contact and policy effects land directly on census groups. Nothing is translated through the old voter archetypes any more.
- District pressure on legislators who vote on a bill is now weighted by the real census make-up of their home region.
- Effects described to players name census groups only.

### A generated successor now belongs to the largest bloc

The generated president or prime minister was meant to come from the largest
bloc but was almost always created with no party. It now keeps the bloc's party
and gets a home region the bloc already holds. A seat that someone else filled
in the meantime is also no longer overwritten.

### admin code reads country rules from data tables

Rules that only apply to some countries are now listed in a single table, which
makes it easier to change which countries use them. Behaviour is unchanged.

### Air wings and fleets show their real supply after each turn

#### What changed

- Air wings and fleets now show their real supply after each turn, instead of an old value left from an earlier posting.
- Supply amounts and combat results are unchanged. Only the figure shown was stale.

### API route code reads country rules from data tables

Rules that only apply to some countries are now listed in a single table, which
makes it easier to change which countries use them. Behaviour is unchanged.

### Architecture check allowances tightened to current levels

#### What changed

- The recorded limits for country-specific branches, direct database access sites
  and file size pins were lowered to the current counts, so new debt can no longer
  hide in leftover room.
- No game rules or player-facing pages changed.

### Bank stress fades, and banking panics keep their old strength

Small pushes on bank stress from stance choices build up, but the stored amount
now fades by half each turn without a new push, so old leftovers cannot tip a
bank into panic years later. The banking panic crisis itself is not slowed down
by the build-up: it keeps banks panicked while it runs, exactly as before.

### Central banks lean against inflation in the long-run fiscal tables

#### What changed

- When inflation runs above or below target in a long-run budget projection, the policy rate now rises or falls one for one with the gap, the way autonomous central bank chairs already do in play.
- The rate has a floor, and with inflation on target it equals the neutral rate, so the published 2027 to 2050 tables are unchanged.
- A hotter-inflation scenario now feeds through into borrowing costs and the interest share of revenue.

### Climate paths are checked for different farm, energy and budget outcomes

A faster energy transition now has a tested payoff: by 2050 farms lose less output,
household energy demand grows less, and weather disasters weigh less on the budget
than on a delayed path.

### Corporation records get one shared front door

This is a tidy-up of how the game is built. Corporations behave exactly as before. Their database access now lives in one place, and an automatic check stops new code from reaching around it, which makes corporation bugs easier to find and prevent.

### Country bill lifecycles run as one turn step

#### What changed

- Bill progress for every country in play now happens in a single turn step, which removes per-step bookkeeping from every turn.
- If one country's bill processing fails, the other countries still finish and the failure is reported by name.
- Bill outcomes and timing are unchanged.

### Country constitutional and election steps run from one ordered list

Behind the scenes only. The steps run in the same order as before, so turns
resolve exactly as they did.

### Country elections run as one turn step

#### What changed

- Scheduling of upcoming elections for every country in play now happens in a single turn step, which removes a lot of per-step bookkeeping from every turn.
- If one country's election scheduling fails, the others still finish and the failure is reported by name.
- Which elections exist, when they open and how they resolve is unchanged.

### Country-only features are listed in one table

The checks that keep UK-only and US-only features out of other countries now read one shared list
instead of each page repeating the country. Behaviour is unchanged.

### Crisis effects now reach population, capital, trade and refugees, and spread between countries

#### What changed

- Crisis effects that cost lives remove residents through the same population accounting as ordinary deaths, capped per effect. Effects that destroy capital create repair obligations on the affected regions, and the owning government pays for repair as it does after a war.
- Effects that move refugees from a named country send them to the other affected countries through the existing reception rules. Each host's asylum and border laws still limit how many arrive, and hosts bear the service costs.
- Trade restrictions raised by a crisis become real embargoes from the affected countries against the countries in the named role, and expire on their own.
- Spillover now has content: the six emergent crises, the authored financial, pandemic, refugee, energy, supply and war crises each name the crises they leak into. Finance spillover is weighted by how much foreign government debt a country holds relative to its own economy, converted at current exchange rates. Spread lines appear on the crisis map.
- Converted crises are answered through their responses only, and a response now does everything the old choice did: its authored effects, the bill, court case or other action it filed, and the shorter crisis it promised, each exactly once. A response that cannot go through is refunded in full.
- Aid pledges and collective contributions on converted crises are also pulled as responses. A pledge still spends from the treasury, records the commitment and files the appropriation bill; a contribution still counts toward the shared target and is rolled back if the treasury cannot pay.
- Government-run countries do not take responses that cost actions, since they have no action budget.
- The conflict response resolver stays the only place that applies a conflict outcome's civilian loss, capital destruction and sanctions, so nothing is applied twice.

### Crisis responses refund in full, healed scars ease unemployment, successors get their own names

If a crisis response fails after the treasury has paid, the treasury, your
corporation and your actions are all refunded instead of the money being lost.

Population scars from a crisis used to raise unemployment and never lower it
again. As a scar heals, unemployment now comes back down by the same amount.

When a legislature or party vote fills an empty executive office, the office
now shows the successor's name rather than the previous holder's.

### Election code cleanup: old redirect files removed

#### What changed

- Election code now lives in one place. The leftover redirect files from the earlier move have been removed and the rest of the game points straight at the real code. Elections, results and timing are unchanged.

### Election code cleanup: vote engine moved into the elections folder

#### What changed

- The vote counting engine and the campaign support code moved into the main elections folder, so all election code now lives in one place. Votes, results and timing are unchanged.

### Election code reads country rules from data tables

Election rules that only apply to one country, such as the US electoral college
or Russian presidential rounds, are now listed in a single table. This makes it
easier to add or change which countries use a rule. Results and behaviour are
unchanged.

### Every new research method now lists its build time and its downside

No late-game method is free. Each one now records its inputs, a build time in
turns and a plain-words downside, and a test fails if a new method is added
without them. Advanced fission, for example, takes four game years to build and
carries a heavy licensing and safety bill.

### Frontier race methods now add real output, and unpaid points cannot win

Corporations deep in the late-game tech tree were already at the plant output
cap, so a method won in a frontier race added nothing. Methods now count as
their own capped bonus on top of it. Funding points are also held aside until
the payment goes through, so an entrant whose payment fails is never crowned.

### Global crises count in leader legacy, briefs find every country's bond, subsidised pay is not shown as lower

- A worldwide crisis such as a pandemic now counts towards the legacy of a leader who governed through it, even when no country list is stored on it.
- Background country briefs now read one nearest-maturity bond per country, so a country whose bonds all mature late no longer reports having no bond.
- The hiring subsidy lowers what the employer pays. The pay shown per worker, which unions use for dues, is now taken before that cut, so subsidised workers do not look worse paid.

### Income tax tiers keep up with prices

#### What changed

- A player's tax tier compares their yearly earnings with the national median. The median grows with real wages only, so rising prices used to push people up through the tiers over the years.
- Earnings are now adjusted for the country's accumulated price level before the comparison, both when tax is assessed and when tax is withheld at the source.
- Worlds where prices are not tracked behave exactly as before.

### iPhone alerts can open your inbox directly

#### What changed

- Alerts sent to the iPhone and iPad app now name the app's alert category. With app 2.11, a long press on an alert shows "Open inbox". Older app versions are unaffected.

### Law book seeding cleanup

#### What changed

- Removed a world setup check that always gave the same answer, so every world is set up the same way.
- No change to which laws exist or how they behave in any country.

### Lighter pages for country and corporation screens

Screens that only need a country flag or the list of US states no longer pull
in the full seat tables for every country. Nothing looks different, pages just
download less.

### Long-run budget tables for the five biggest economies are now tested

#### What changed

- Budget tables from 2027 to 2050 are generated for the five biggest economies, with checks that policy changes move them and that no country is left paying more than half its revenue in interest without a restructuring.
- A new test covers a bond maturity wall that lands exactly on a fiscal year change, and confirms debt and cash stay balanced.
- No change to how the game plays today.

### Moderator ledger timeline is now in turn order

#### What changed

- The ledger timeline sorts by turn, then by time within the turn, newest first.
  Turns no longer appear out of order.
- Paging with "load more" continues from exactly where the last page ended.
- The CSV export uses the same order.

### Money tables get one shared front door

This is a tidy-up of how the game is built. National budgets, bonds, central banks and the financial transaction log behave exactly as before. Their database access now lives in one place, and an automatic check stops new code from reaching around it, which makes money bugs easier to find and prevent.

### Naval and air command explains why a region cannot host a fleet

#### What changed

- On the Naval and air command page, a region typed as land now reads "land region, fleets cannot be stationed here" in a fleet's Station list. Before, it read "no port" even when the region had a port rating.
- Regions with no port at all now read "landlocked, no port".
- Which regions a fleet can be stationed in has not changed.

### Net worth counts shares your companies hold in other companies

#### What changed

- Net worth and the wealth list now include shares a company you own holds in other companies, valued at the other company's share price and scaled by your share of the owner.
- This follows chains, so a company held by a company held by you is counted too.
- Your direct share holdings are valued as before. No cash moves and no prices change.
- Players who own companies with subsidiaries may move up the wealth list.

### New 2039 methods take time to build and carry a cost

The newest methods for finance, retail, vehicles, real estate and mining are no
longer free to switch to. Each takes between a year and a half and four years
to set up, and each has a drawback, such as a single software fault hitting
every client or a heavy energy bill before deep mines produce.

### New production methods take time to build and carry a one-off cost

The build time recorded on late-game methods is now enforced. When you switch a
sector to one, the sector keeps running its old method until the build time has
passed, then the usual phase-in starts. Cancelling during the build costs nothing
and returns the sector to its old method at once.

Each method's downside is also priced. Adopting it adds a one-off charge, as a
share of the sector's daily revenue, on top of the normal retooling fee. Waivers
on the retooling fee do not cover it.

### page and component code reads country rules from data tables

Rules that only apply to some countries are now listed in a single table, which
makes it easier to change which countries use them. Behaviour is unchanged.

### Pages import currency symbols from a small module

Forty screens that only needed a currency symbol, a country's currency or the list
of tradeable currencies now import them from a small module instead of the full
exchange rate tables, which reach into every country's economy data. Nothing
changes on screen. A test now fails if a browser module imports the large file for
names the small one provides.

### Party leaders, splits and seats are checked together over several elections

Leader turnover and party splits were tested separately. They are now tested together
across several election cycles, so a leader leaving office during a split does not
leave a party with seats far out of line with its votes.

### Party leadership election code reorganised

This is a tidy-up of how the game is built. Party leadership, state party,
national committee and caucus chair elections behave exactly as before. Their
code now sits next to the other election systems so it is easier to maintain.

### Plant labour cost follows the funded run

#### What changed

- Sector labour cost and wage per worker on a funded turn are priced from the funded headcount instead of the crew share, so a plant at a 1% funded run no longer reports hundreds of times its real payroll.
- The union pension charge base reads that labour cost, so it no longer overstates for those plants.

### Plants stuck at a minimal run can grow again

#### What changed

- A plant running at or near its minimum (under 5% of capacity) that still sells at least 90% of what it offers now adds 1% of its capacity per turn, up to 5%.
- Before, only a plant that sold out completely could grow, so in a market oversupplied by a few percent every plant was rationed and low plants never recovered.
- Plants that lose money on each unit sold, or that already carry more than a turn of sales in stock, do not step up.
- The step is small on purpose, so many plants recovering together cannot flood a market in one turn. Wages, input purchases and output follow the new run as before.

### region banner and layer-1 model lookup code reads country rules from data tables

Rules that only apply to some countries are now listed in a single table, which
makes it easier to change which countries use them. Behaviour is unchanged.

### Regional laws passed through bills now move their region's political lean

#### What changed

- Regional laws passed as bills now shift the lean of the voter groups they touch in their region, along the axis the law is about.
- A far-left or far-right choice pulls hardest, a centre choice pulls nothing, and the pull fades back after the law is repealed like other lean effects.
- National laws are unchanged.

### Regional starting scores for 1953 and 1991 are now checked data files

The 1953 and 1991 regional score tables are plain data files now. Every value is
checked to stay within plus or minus 12 points and to name a real issue family,
so a bad edit stops a world from seeding instead of reaching players. Worlds start
exactly as before, and a test confirms the files match what the generator produces.

Country data and cabinet tables are also exempt from the file size limit by rule,
so adding a country no longer needs a list entry.

### Retirement age now moves the dependency ratio, and the 2027 world opens with a realistic oldest age group

#### What changed

- Raising or lowering a country's retirement age now changes its dependency ratio as well as its workforce. Before, only the workforce moved.
- A new 2027 world opens with far fewer people over 90, matching real populations. This removes a spike of deaths in the first year and a falsely high early dependency ratio. Worlds that are already running keep the population they have.
- Long-run population tables for the US, UK, Germany, Japan and China (2027 to 2050) are now generated and tested, including checks that regional totals add up to national totals.

### Sector run panel no longer blames inputs for a stop it cannot explain

#### What changed

- The idle-capacity breakdown on a sector page no longer labels an unexplained stop as short of inputs.
- That part of the gap now appears under Other limits.
- Stops with a recorded input or cash cut are shown as before. Production itself is unchanged.

### seed and reset code reads country rules from data tables

Rules that only apply to some countries are now listed in a single table, which
makes it easier to change which countries use them. Behaviour is unchanged.

### shared library code reads country rules from data tables

Rules that only apply to some countries are now listed in a single table, which
makes it easier to change which countries use them. Behaviour is unchanged.

### Singleplayer downloads build again for Apple Silicon Macs

#### What changed

- The Apple Silicon Mac package for singleplayer game releases builds on a newer Mac build machine. The old one is no longer offered, which stopped the whole release from publishing.

### Singleplayer reports what this game version can start

#### What changed

- The singleplayer game ships a capabilities file and a local capabilities route listing each era, whether it can be started, and which countries are player, computer-run or background.
- Every setup option now has a plain description instead of a generic sentence.

### Singleplayer says plainly when an era cannot be started yet

Picking an era that is not ready on this game version now stops straight away with
a plain message, and your current world is left untouched. The 2027 world is the
one to start for now.

### Starting a new world with a missing or unknown scenario now stops with a clear error

Setting up or resetting a world with a scenario name that does not exist used to
quietly fall back to a modern setup. It now stops before anything is written and
says which scenario name was missing or not recognised.

Running worlds are not affected. The hourly turn keeps its safe fallback, so a
live game can never fail over a stored scenario name.

### Successor parliament election dates move into a data table

#### What changed

- The Romanian, Polish, Bulgarian, Czechoslovak and Hungarian election schedules in the 1991 start are now rows in a single table.
- Every election date is identical to before. Adding another country to this kind of schedule is now a new row, not new code.

### The country budget page now shows the austerity package and its approval cost

Austerity budgets used to appear only in the news. The sovereign health panel now
shows the latest package for about a year: how big it is against the economy, how
much comes from tax rises versus spending cuts, and how many approval points it cost
and how many are still being felt.

### The old Soviet and East German law lists are retired

The Soviet Union and East Germany carried an older list of laws that the current
law book had already replaced, and the game never loaded them. They are removed
so there is one law book to maintain. Opening budgets and policies in every era
come out exactly as before.

### turn and simulation code reads country rules from data tables

Rules that only apply to some countries are now listed in a single table, which
makes it easier to change which countries use them. Behaviour is unchanged.

### US and UK opening budgets no longer depend on the old law catalogue

The US and UK opening budgets were still being worked out from the old law
catalogue behind the scenes. Those inputs are now stored as plain data, so the
old catalogue can be retired. Every opening budget in every era comes out exactly
as before, and no player sees a difference.

### US state list moved into the US folder

The list of US state codes now sits with the rest of the US data. Nothing
changes in the game.

### War losses now depend on how much force the enemy has at the front

#### What changed

- In each battle, a side's casualties now scale with how much fighting power its enemy actually has at the front.
- Even fights are unchanged. The weaker side's losses are unchanged too.
- A much stronger attacker meeting a thin defence now loses a fraction of what it used to, down to a floor of one tenth of a normal battle's losses.

### Wartime seizure rules groundwork, with no player-visible change

#### What changed

- The wartime seizure rules now live in a pure module that takes plain data in
  and returns plain data out, so the same code can run in a request path, a turn
  phase, or the headless harness.
- The module encodes the refusal order for a taking, the per-asset tension and
  the domestic political factor, and it carries a recorded valuation for a peace
  settlement without deciding who funds or receives it.
- Groundwork only. There is no route, no order storage and no turn work, so no
  player can place an order and no in-game number moved. Notes in this directory
  are not published as written: they fold into the dev post for a release, and
  the public post is written by hand.

### World setup stops instead of guessing a missing era

A few setup steps used to assume an era when none was recorded. They now stop and
say which era is missing, so a world can never be half built from another era's
data.

### A build check now blocks any new direct database access

New code can no longer open a database collection directly. It has to go through that collection's shared module, and a new record type needs one added first. Existing exceptions with names built at runtime are counted and can only shrink.

### A check keeps old laws priced in running worlds

No visible change. When an old law type is retired, worlds already running
still have it in force. A new check fails the build if a retired law would lose
its budget price, so its programmes cannot quietly drop to zero.

### A cyber operation sent twice at once is charged once

If a cyber operation was sent twice at the same moment, for example by a double
click, both requests could pass the cooldown check. The second was charged and
then failed with an error. Now only one launch per target each turn goes
through. The other is refused with the usual cooldown message and its cost is
returned to the appropriation.

### A cyber stand-down no longer shields the attacker

#### What changed

- The country that stands down is held back for the full 72 turns.
- The country it attacked is held back only until the stand-down country's 24 turn cooldown would have ended.
- The cyber wiki page and console help now say that only a country that has attacked can stand down.

### A failed emergency aid pledge gives the money back

#### What changed

- A head of state whose aid pledge fails partway no longer loses the treasury money.
- The crisis recovery and the diplomatic bump are reversed.
- The country can pledge again instead of being told it already pledged.

### A passed emergency bill waits for funds instead of vanishing

#### What changed

- A passed emergency bill that cannot be paid is retried on later turns while the crisis is still running.
- The crisis ledger no longer shows nothing for that response.

### A script now prints the 1.20 code health numbers

No change in play. The code health counts the 1.20 clean-up is measured against
can now be reproduced with one command, so before and after tables come from the
same method.

### A turn interrupted by the update finishes without repeating steps

#### What changed

- Approval fading, national bill votes and national elections are not repeated when a turn the old release started is finished by the new one.
- If only some countries' bills or elections had run, the rest wait for the next turn instead of risking a double run.

### A world reset leaves no old law types behind

#### What changed

- A reset removes admin electorate priorities that name a law type from the retired catalogues. Priorities on the new law book stay.
- The check that runs after a reset now fails if a law type, enacted law, bill or saved priority still names a retired law type, and the world stays paused until a clean reset.
- The read-only preflight lists any such documents before a reset and fails on them in a fresh world.

### Accounts, settings, metrics and logs get shared front doors

This is a tidy-up of how the game is built. Everything behaves exactly as before. Database access for each of these record types now lives in one place, and an automatic check stops new code from reaching around it, which makes bugs easier to find and prevent.

### An independent Quebec's international relations minister counts as its foreign minister

#### What changed

- An independent Quebec's Minister of International Relations now fills the foreign minister role in international organisations and diplomacy, instead of the country having no recognised foreign minister.

### Architecture check accepts the approval field move

#### What changed

- The startup step that moves saved character and party approvals onto census groups no longer fails the architecture check.
- Every other file still has to describe voters with census groups.

### Automated methods cut crews only once they are built

While a sector waits for an automated method such as humanoid robotics to be
built, it still runs its old method. It now keeps its full crew and pays no
automation levy during that wait. Workers are let go, and the levy starts, on
the turn the new method comes online.

### Background governments decide cyber operations with fewer reads

#### What changed

- Background governments now look up their cyber history and their rival's defences once per turn for all of them together, instead of once per government. Their decisions are the same; the turn just gets there faster.

### Bank and ledger records get one shared front door

This is a tidy-up of how the game is built. Loans, savings and ledgers behave exactly as before. Their database access now lives in one place per record type, and an automatic check stops new code from reaching around it, which makes money bugs easier to find and prevent.

### Campaign cost check lists Quebec as waiting for its baseline

#### What changed

- Quebec, which can become its own country, is listed with Canada as waiting for a campaign cost baseline. Live campaign money already uses each country's live regional GDP.
- Nothing changes for players.

### Canada is back on the world map before 2027

#### What changed

- In 1953 to 2023 worlds Canada appears on the world map as a background economy again.
- The background country inspector offers Canada again in those worlds.
- 2027 worlds are unchanged: Canada is a full playable country there.

### Canada only appears in worlds started with it

Canada is playable in new 2027 worlds. Worlds that were already running before
this release, including the live world, never set Canada up: it has no
provinces, budget, parties or politicians there. Those worlds now leave Canada
out entirely, instead of trying to run it each turn as an empty country.

### Canada shares the Crown with the United Kingdom

#### What changed

- When Canada opens, its King or Queen will be the same character as the
  United Kingdom's, with royal titles and wording taken from the UK.
- Several checks were updated for Canada being set up ahead of its launch,
  including one that confused the US state code for California with Canada's
  country code.

### Canada's bill phase is a known turn step

Canada's national bills run in the same turn step as every other country's
bills. That step is now listed with the others, so the game builds without a
type error. A few long code comments were also shortened.

### Canada's executive titles and cyber law live with Canada

#### What changed

- Regional leaders in Canada and an independent Quebec keep the same titles.
- Canada's security act still sets its cyber resilience.
- The code check that keeps country rules in their own country folders passes again.

### Canada's House of Commons election rules are checked like every other chamber

Canada's House of Commons election rules now run through the same consistency
checks as every other country's chambers, so a mistake in them is caught
before Canada opens to players.

### Canada's regional statistics build without an unchecked type cast, with no player-visible change

#### What changed

- Canada's provincial statistics are built from a copy of the baseline with a
  shape check on each override, so a wrong override key is skipped instead of
  being hidden by a forced type.
- All 13 provinces and territories produce exactly the same figures as before.

### Canada's security act now sets its cyber defence

#### What changed

- Raising the Security Intelligence and Cyber Act above its starting level makes Canada harder to hit with cyber operations.
- Lowering it makes Canada easier to hit.
- The cyber wiki page explains this for Canadian players.

### Canada's voters

#### What changed

- Each of Canada's 13 provinces and territories has a 2027 population profile: language and community, age, education, income and where people live.
- Voters are grouped by community (anglophone, francophone, Indigenous, South Asian, East and Southeast Asian, other), which is what separates Quebec and the Atlantic provinces from the rest.
- Each policy area has its own preferences for these groups, with francophone and Indigenous voters leaning left on most issues.
- Provinces carry their own starting levels for housing prices, energy transition, wait times, child poverty and similar measures.
- Canada still cannot be joined. This only prepares the voter data for when it opens.
- Fixed Canada's starting army to use unit types that exist in the game.

### Canadian Premiers now count as regional executives

#### What changed

- The governing party of a Canadian province now gets the organisation bonus for holding the Premier's office.
- Region pages show the Premier's administration line.
- The same applies to Prefects in an independent Quebec.

### Checks follow Germany, Japan and China into the law book

#### What changed

- Checks for countries outside the law book use France as their example.
- Germany, Japan and China are checked for distinct political starting values in each era they now draw from.
- Nothing changes for players.

### China keeps paying for its existing programmes

China moved to a new law book in this release. Worlds started before that still
run the programmes passed under the old laws: pensions, health insurance, the
army and the rest. The budget now keeps pricing those programmes as before, and
the minimum wage China already set keeps applying. Without this, those spending
lines would have dropped to zero and the wage floor would have disappeared.

### China now runs on one law book

#### What changed

- The older Chinese law list no longer seeds into new worlds. China uses its law book, plus the provincial resource tax lever the book does not have.
- The provincial budgets now read the corporate tax rate from the China book.
- The minimum wage floor follows the China worker security law.
- Opening tax rates for 1979, 1991 and the modern presets and opening program spending are unchanged. The 1953 opening uses the China book's tax rates.

### China switches to its own law book

#### What changed

- New China worlds open with China's own 110 laws, its own tax rates and its own starting political scores for 1953, 1979 and 1991.
- Each Chinaese region gets its own starting texture instead of one shared national picture.
- Existing worlds are not changed by this entry.

### China's 1953 opening budget keeps its earlier spending

#### What changed

- China's 1953 opening spending in new worlds is the amount its old laws priced: about 11.9 billion instead of 14.9 billion.
- Defense, capital construction and health keep their authored 1953 figures.
- Existing worlds are unchanged.

### China's new law book is written

#### What changed

- China now has its own written law book: 63 main laws, 41 supporting laws and 6 tax laws, each with five levels, costs and plain descriptions.
- Nothing changes in play yet. The book is not connected to live worlds until the later switch-over.

### China's provinces follow the national corporate tax rate

Chinese provinces keep part of the corporate tax collected in their territory.
After China moved to its new law book, every province used a fixed 25% rate no
matter what the national corporate tax was. Provinces now use the national rate,
including any change a passed bill makes. Worlds started before the new law book
keep the rate they had already enacted.

### Code formatting cleanup, with no player-visible change

#### What changed

- Files that missed the automatic formatter were reformatted. No code
  behaviour changed.

### Code formatting pass for the 1.20 release

#### What changed

- 315 source files are reformatted to the project style.
- The record of known per-row database calls in the turn is matched to the reformatted code, with the same count.
- Nothing changes for players.

### Corporate sector records get one shared front door

This is a tidy-up of how the game is built. Corporate sectors behave exactly as before. Their database access now lives in one place, and an automatic check stops new code from reaching around it, which makes bugs easier to find and prevent.

### Country data check skips database collection handles

#### What changed

- Database collection handles, which all live in one shared place by design, are no longer reported as UK data sitting outside the UK folder.
- Nothing changes for players.

### Country display settings are typed as they are stored, with no player-visible change

#### What changed

- Era-specific display settings for each country record a cleared value the
  way the data file stores it, and the loader turns it back before any page
  reads it.
- Every country and era shows the same names, chambers and offices as before.

### Country names, chambers and office titles now live in each country's folder

Nothing changes in play. The names, chamber labels, office titles and currency
codes that pages show used to sit in one very large shared file. Each country now
keeps its own slice in its own folder, so a change to one country touches only
that country.

### Country order check includes Canada

#### What changed

- The registered country order check expects 25 countries, ending with Canada.
- Nothing changes for players.

### Crisis responses and failed aid refund the treasury reliably

In worlds that track treasury cash, a crisis response that failed after the
treasury paid could lose that money instead of refunding it, and the next try
of the same response that turn went through without being charged. Both are
fixed: the refund lands once, and each try is charged on its own.

When a crisis aid bill fails, the pledge is now clawed back exactly once. A
failure partway through can no longer reverse the aid's effects again on the
next turn.

### Cyber news names countries in full

#### What changed

- Cyber attribution and stand-down news posts name the countries involved.

### Cyber operations wait for a switch on the running world

#### What changed

- New worlds start with cyber operations on.
- On the running world, no government launches a cyber operation after the update: not a player's intelligence service, not a computer-run government and not a background government. A player who tries is told cyber operations are not open in this world yet.
- Operators can turn it on when the world is ready.

### Elected office records get one shared front door

This is a tidy-up of how the game is built. Elected offices behave exactly as before. Their database access now lives in one place, and an automatic check stops new code from reaching around it, which makes office bugs easier to find and prevent.

### Election records get one shared front door

This is a tidy-up of how the game is built. Elections behave exactly as before. Their database access now lives in one place, and an automatic check stops new code from reaching around it, which makes election bugs easier to find and prevent.

### Election, bill, campaign and government records get shared front doors

This is a tidy-up of how the game is built. Politics behaves exactly as before. Database access for each of these record types now lives in one place, and an automatic check stops new code from reaching around it, which makes bugs easier to find and prevent.

### Era, approval and priority tests follow the one law book, with no player-visible change

#### What changed

- The law era window checks use Japanese laws with known unlock years.
- The approval direction check uses Japan's income tax and minimum wage laws,
  which have the same option layout as the retired US laws.
- The electorate priority editor test reads the same law list a seeded world
  holds, including the one law book.

### Existing worlds get China's new laws

China's laws can now be proposed in worlds that were created before China
moved to the shared law book. The update only adds what is missing: laws and
regional starting rows that already exist are left exactly as they are.

### Existing worlds get Germany's new laws

Germany's laws can now be proposed in worlds that were created before Germany
moved to the shared law book. The update only adds what is missing: laws and
regional starting rows that already exist are left exactly as they are.

### Existing worlds get Japan's new laws

Japan's laws can now be proposed in worlds that were created before Japan
moved to the shared law book. The update only adds what is missing: laws and
regional starting rows that already exist are left exactly as they are.

### Formatting fix for two page tests

#### What changed

- Two page tests are formatted so the release format check passes.

### Four more new behaviours wait for a switch on the running world

#### What changed

- New worlds start with all four on.
- The running world keeps its current behaviour after the update: empty by-elections stay empty, Supreme Court seats wait for a player President, surprise cases are ruled on when they are filed, and players who have seen every dated event get none that turn.
- Each can be turned on separately.

### Fund, order, budget and exchange rate records get shared front doors

This is a tidy-up of how the game is built. Markets, funds and budgets behave exactly as before. Their database access now lives in one place per record type, and an automatic check stops new code from reaching around it, which makes money bugs easier to find and prevent.

### Game links can open the iPhone and iPad app

#### What changed

- Links to game pages on ahousedividedgame.com can open in the iPhone and iPad app once players have app 2.11.
- Sign-in, account and API pages always stay in the browser.

### Generated enemy forces live in their own rules module, with no player-visible change

#### What changed

- The enemy force generator and the seeded dice it shares with battle
  resolution now live in their own pure rules module, keeping the battle file
  under its size limit.
- Every battle with the same seed produces the same result as before.

### German Länder keep their share of income tax and VAT

Each German Land is funded mostly by its share of national income tax and VAT.
After Germany moved to its new law book, those shares read the rate from a tax
bill, and until such a bill passed they came out as zero, leaving every Land on
the federal grant alone. The shares now use the national budget's income tax
and VAT rates whenever no bill has set them, the same rates the budget page
shows.

### Germany joins the shared law book

Germany's laws are now authored in the same law book as the other major
countries. Each law moves the nation's metric board, costs and earns money
through the shared price rules, and opens at a level that matches the era.

Germany also gets its own opening metric board for 1953, 1979 and 1991, an
opening approval baseline, opening tax rates (income tax 38%, corporate tax
30%, payroll 12%, VAT 19%) and regional differences between the Länder that
come from each Land's own data.

### Germany now runs on one law book

#### What changed

- The older German law list no longer seeds into new worlds. Germany uses its law book only.
- The Länder income tax and VAT shares now follow the enacted income tax and sales tax laws in that book.
- The Länder trade tax uses the standard rate until the law book gains a Land-level lever for it.
- Opening tax rates for Germany in 1979, 1991 and the modern presets are unchanged.

### Germany, Japan and China get the later-era laws in their law book

#### What changed

Germany, Japan and China now carry the 2027 to 2050 laws in their single law
book, with five levels each, the same as the United States and United Kingdom.
Climate, automation and cyber laws still shape their economies as before.
Worlds already running pick up the new laws without losing anything they had
enacted. Enacted later-era laws from the old Germany, Japan and China lists
keep working in those worlds.

### Germany's new law book is written

#### What changed

- Germany now has its own written law book: 63 main laws, 41 supporting laws and 6 tax laws, each with five levels, costs and plain descriptions.
- Nothing changes in play yet. The book is not connected to live worlds until the later switch-over.

### Germany's opening budgets spend what they did before the law book

#### What changed

- Germany's opening program spending in new worlds from 1979 onward matches what its old laws priced.
- Germany no longer opens 2019 and later worlds with a surplus of over a tenth of its economy.
- Existing worlds are unchanged.

### Getting started in Canada

#### What changed

- The getting-started checklist for a Canadian character opens their home province, Canada's parties, Parliament, elections and the TSX.
- The welcome letter for a Canadian character names their home province and shows starting funds and the checklist reward in Canadian dollars.

### Hidden country check covers Quebec

#### What changed

- Quebec, which only exists as a country after it votes to leave Canada, is checked like Scotland and Wales: defined, but not offered in country lists until it forms.
- Nothing changes for players.

### Historical seat rosters now sit in each country's folder

Nothing changes in play. The era seat rosters for the US, UK, Japan, Germany,
China, Brazil, Ireland, Nigeria, the Soviet Union and East Germany each live with
their country now, so a roster change touches only that country's files.

### Independence promotion checks its parent country

#### What changed

- Independence promotion skips parents without opening records instead of relying on later checks.
- Nothing changes for players.

### Japan and Germany keep paying for their existing programmes

Japan and Germany moved to new law books in this release. Worlds started before
that still run the programmes passed under the old laws: health insurance,
pensions, defence and the rest. The budget now keeps pricing those programmes
as before. Without this, their spending lines would have dropped to zero.

### Japan can still pass tax bills in running worlds

Japan's tax laws moved to its new law book. Worlds started before that move
still hold Japan's older tax laws, and the bill wizard and the cabinet only
looked for the new ones, so every Japanese tax bill in those worlds would have
been refused.

Both versions of each Japanese tax law now count as the same tax. The wizard
offers whichever one the world has, and a bill cannot set the same tax twice
by naming both.

### Japan now runs on one law book

#### What changed

- The older Japanese law list no longer seeds into new worlds. Japan uses its law book, plus the prefectural resident tax and fixed asset tax levers the book does not have.
- Electorate priorities, the minimum wage floor and the reset tax menu now point at laws in the Japan book.
- Opening tax rates and opening program spending for Japan are unchanged in every preset.
- Worlds without the old prefectural grant law fund prefectures from the national state grants line.

### Japan switches to its own law book

#### What changed

- New Japan worlds open with Japan's own 110 laws, its own tax rates and its own starting political scores for 1953, 1979 and 1991.
- Each Japanese region gets its own starting texture instead of one shared national picture.
- Existing worlds are not changed by this entry.

### Japan's 1991 opening budget balances again

#### What changed

- Japan's opening spending programs in new 1991 worlds are fitted to its authored 1991 spending mix and its receipts.
- Health and public works spending now land in the matching law book budget lines.
- Existing worlds are unchanged.

### Japan's new law book is written

#### What changed

- Japan now has its own written law book: 63 main laws, 41 supporting laws and 6 tax laws, each with five levels, costs and plain descriptions.
- Nothing changes in play yet. The book is not connected to live worlds until the later switch-over.

### Keep party expansion inside current borders

Party expansion uses the country's current regions when checking neighbouring and isolated regions, including before and after Northern Ireland transfers to Ireland.

### Keep successor-country metrics usable after independence

#### What changed

- Omit the region field from newly promoted national metric boards before BSON storage.
- Accept legacy null region fields only on national boards, keeping regional identity validation strict.
- Remove the legacy field in the existing batched refresh without replacing observations or history.

### Law effect simulations test laws that still exist, with no player-visible change

#### What changed

- The economy, education, environment, healthcare, infrastructure, public
  safety, social and governance law simulations run on Japan's matching laws.
- The banking separation checks use Japan, since the US, UK, Russia and East
  Germany have no charter bill in the one law book.
- The birth rate lever check was removed: no law moves birth rate in the one
  law book.

### Ledger ordering index is now checked on every start

#### What changed

- The index behind the ledger's newest-first ordering is now part of the startup
  index checks, so a live world always has it. Before, it only existed if a
  manual migration had been run.
- Without it the database sorts the whole ledger in memory for each page, which
  can be slow on a large ledger. Nothing in the ledger itself changes.

### Merger review uses the new competition laws in Germany, Japan and China

#### What changed

- In Germany, Japan and China the competition law in the law book sets when a merger is referred for review.
- The check that guards who may write sector revenue sees inserts made through the newer database helpers again.
- The recorded turn lookups include the Canadian dollar rate.

### Merger rules in Germany, Japan and China stay as passed

Merger review in Germany, Japan and China now follows the competition law in
their new law books. In a world started before that move, the country keeps
running its existing Competition Act, at the level its legislature set, until
the new competition law is passed. Without this, those worlds would have
switched to the new law's default overnight and ignored any Competition Act
bill already passed.

New worlds no longer list the old Competition Act for these three countries,
since passing it would not have changed anything.

### Money checks cover crisis aid and lever refunds, with no player-visible change

#### What changed

- Crisis aid and crisis lever refunds go through a paired refund helper. The
  treasury writer check now knows that helper, so every caller is classified
  and must show its money witness.
- Seven unreleased notes used labels the changelog rejects and were corrected.

### Nearly every game record now has a shared front door

This is a tidy-up of how the game is built. Everything behaves exactly as before. Database access for these record types now lives in one place each, and an automatic check stops new code from reaching around it.

### Net worth history counts shares your companies hold in other companies

#### What changed

- The per-turn net worth history now includes shares that companies you own hold in other companies, scaled by your share of the owner. It uses the same rule as the wealth list.
- The sidebar net worth, turn dashboard, wealth history and recaps read this history, so they no longer show a lower figure than the wealth list.
- Past turns keep the figures they were recorded with. New turns use the new total.
- No cash moves and no prices change.

### No Bank of Canada in worlds before Canada joins

#### What changed

- Worlds from 1953 to 2023 create central banks only for countries in that era.
- The Canadian dollar still has an exchange rate in every era.
- A reset of an older-era world removes a stray Bank of Canada.

### Non-player politician records get one shared front door

This is a tidy-up of how the game is built. Computer-controlled politicians behave exactly as before. Their database access now lives in one place, and an automatic check stops new code from reaching around it, which makes bugs easier to find and prevent.

### Official referendum campaigns use the right country's Political Strength

#### What changed

- Official ground game actions in a referendum charge the party's Political Strength in the referendum's own country.
- Canadian party officers can now run official campaigns in a Quebec referendum.

### Old-law compatibility tables are marked as local-save only

#### What changed

- No gameplay change. The old-law compatibility tables now say in code that they exist only for local saves, and that a reset replaces every id they cover.

### Older laws in running worlds still wait for their year

Germany, Japan, China, the United States and the United Kingdom moved to new
law books, and their old law types left the list of laws new worlds start
with. Worlds started earlier still have those old laws. Without this fix, the
old laws that only open in a later year would have been open to propose at any
time in those worlds: for example China's Common Prosperity (2021), Belt and
Road (2013) and AI strategy (2015) in a world still in 2006.

Each old law now keeps the first year it had before, so proposing it waits for
that year as it did before the move.

### One cyber attack crisis per country at a time

#### What changed

- A country can have only one Cyber Attack crisis active at a time. A new operation against a country that already has one is refused before launch, with a clear reason and no charge to your appropriation.
- Computer-run governments follow the same rule and skip a rival that is already under attack.
- The Cyber Operations console shows the reason next to the target, and the wiki page explains the rule.

### One entry per old Chinese law in the running-world price table

No visible change. Two updates each added China's old laws to the table that
keeps them priced in running worlds. The prices were the same, but the repeat
broke the code checks. Each law is now listed once, and a check stops it
happening again.

### Only an attacker can stand down from cyber operations

Standing down opens a truce that blocks cyber operations both ways and eases
world tension. Any country could declare one against any other, even one it
had never attacked, which let a country shield itself from attackers and lower
world tension again and again. Standing down now needs an operation against
that country since the last truce, and the intelligence console greys out the
button when there is nothing to stand down.

### Opening national budgets for 1953 and 1979 live in each country's folder

This is a tidy-up for people who work on the game's code. Every country's
opening budget for 1953 and 1979 now sits next to the rest of that country's
data, so changing one country no longer means digging through a 7,000 line
file. No player-facing numbers change.

### Player character records get one shared front door

This is a tidy-up of how the game is built. Characters behave exactly as before. Their database access now lives in one place, and an automatic check stops new code from reaching around it, which makes character bugs easier to find and prevent.

### Political party records get one shared front door

This is a tidy-up of how the game is built. Parties behave exactly as before. Their database access now lives in one place, and an automatic check stops new code from reaching around it, which makes party bugs easier to find and prevent.

### Preserve deployment build diagnostics

Builds retain compiler output and stage results in persistent-cache log files.
Image packaging can record compilation, source-map cleanup and dependency pruning
separately. Verified compiler-cache recovery now works on self-hosted deployments,
and static-generation workers are limited to one to reduce peak memory use.

### Prime minister and head of state appointment lists load again

#### What changed

- The lists of members who can be appointed prime minister, or put forward
  for head of state, read the right player records again instead of failing.
- Five unreleased notes had invalid badge or area labels and now pass the
  changelog checks.

### Quebec's petition names the Premier

#### What changed

- Referendum request announcements use each region's own executive title.

### Records addressed by name constants get shared front doors too

This is a tidy-up of how the game is built. Everything behaves exactly as before. Database access for these record types now lives in one place each, and an automatic check stops new code from reaching around it.

### Regional metric texture for 1953 boards is built from typed seed data, with no player-visible change

#### What changed

- The 1953 regional texture step reads each country's regional metrics, era
  adjustments and preset overlays through their real types, so a change to
  that data shape is caught when the code is checked.
- Every region's derived texture is the same as before.

### Regional voter shifts carry over to the new release

#### What changed

- The shifts in regional voter views built up from newsrooms and laws move to their new place automatically when the server starts.
- Polls, maps and elections read the same voters the day after the update as the day before.
- The shifts keep fading slowly over time, exactly as before.

### Release checks accept the reviewed reset scan change

#### What changed

- The post-reset scan's new check for retired law ids was reviewed and recorded, so the release checks pass again.

### Release checks count Japan's law book

#### What changed

- Law counts in two automated checks reflect Japan's move to the law book.
- Thirteen release notes now use valid labels.
- Nothing changes for players.

### Release checks understand a country that starts in 2027

#### What changed

- Cost scaling, seeder roster and voter-group checks accept a country whose first world is 2027.
- Canada's thirteen regions are now part of the voter coverage check.
- Nothing changes for players.

### Release checks updated for Canada as a player country

#### What changed

- Checks count 25 registered countries and two 2027 player countries.
- The project README states 25 registered countries.
- Nothing changes for players.

### Restore successor-country account and metric continuity

#### What changed

- Currency transfers reconcile savings accounts and their backing without crediting
  the same money twice.
- Ulster connects to neighboring Irish regions for party access after reunification.
- Scottish and Welsh inherited metrics retain their measurement dates and receive
  valid publication dates at independence.
- Northern Ireland's inherited UK household readings remain labeled estimates until
  Irish country-wide inputs can certify them. Existing affected boards normalize on
  read and persist corrected metadata in the normal guarded turn batch.
- Metric repairs preserve values and historical samples; they do not restart turns
  or bypass payment and processing guards.

### Reunited regions keep their savings accounts and can join parties

#### What changed

- A resident's savings in the old currency are converted into a savings account
  in the new currency, with accrued interest and the opened status carried
  over. Before, the balance converted but the account did not, so the bank page
  asked the player to open a new one while the old account was left behind.
- Northern Ireland now borders Donegal, Midlands and Kildare for party
  growth once it is part of Ireland, so its residents can join Irish parties
  that are active in the border regions.

### Russia and East Germany law books are now plain data files

No change in play. The laws, tax sliders and costs are identical. Editing a law
now means editing a data file instead of a very long code file.

### Saved worlds keep Japan's and Germany's minimum wage

When the minimum wage law sets the wage floor, a world started before Japan
and Germany moved to their new law books still has their old minimum wage laws
in force. Those laws now keep setting the floor instead of being ignored. If a
newer minimum wage law is also passed, the higher floor applies.

### Scotland and Wales get campaign cost baselines, and one country's late approval data no longer stops a turn

#### What changed

- Scotland and Wales now have their own campaign cost baselines, so campaign income and action costs can be worked out for their players instead of stopping the turn.
- A country whose approval data is one turn behind is now judged on its own data, not compared with other countries, so it no longer stops the turn for everyone.
- Approval data that is partly refreshed within one country is still refused.

### Search and policy effect tests follow the one law book, with no player-visible change

#### What changed

- Legislation search tests use the Japanese and French banking charter acts
  and a modern Japanese law for the era check.
- The policy tick test checks that a national law's effect is shared across
  its own country's regions, using a test law.

### Seat and region tables move into each country's folder

No change in play. The numbers are identical; they moved out of one shared
table file into the folder of the country they describe.

### Shorter code comments on new 1.20 modules, with no player-visible change

#### What changed

- Comments on the Canada data, the law book mapping, the market output rules,
  battle losses, nationalization and the Lighthouse scorecard scripts were
  shortened so they pass the repository's comment checks.
- No code behaviour changed.

### Shorter comments on Canada's data files, with no player-visible change

#### What changed

- Canada's bucket affinity, regional metric and census data comments now fit
  the repository's comment checks.
- The comment check tracks the historical seat and budget tables that moved
  into each country's folder, with the same counts they had before the move.
- No code behaviour changed.

### Single player launch check expects Canada as a 2027 player country

#### What changed

- The single player launch check expects the United States and Canada as the 2027 player countries, matching the world the game now ships.
- Nothing changes for players.

### Smaller admin bundle for the law type editor

#### What changed

- The demographic targeting step of the admin law type editor loads its voter groups from the server.
- Country voter models no longer ship to the browser through that editor.
- Canada's founding Commons table no longer pulls the party roster into the browser.

### Sovereignty copy names come from the sovereignty rules

No visible change. The devolution tab and referendum panel still say Premier and
Ottawa for Quebec and First Minister and Westminster for the UK nations.

### Starting budgets for more eras now live in each country's folder

No change in play. The opening budgets for the later eras are stored beside the
rest of each country's data, the same way the 1953 and 1979 budgets already are.
The numbers are unchanged.

### State records get one shared front door

This is a tidy-up of how the game is built. States behave exactly as before. Their database access now lives in one place, and an automatic check stops new code from reaching around it, which makes state bugs easier to find and prevent.

### Structured error logging in country turn phases, with no player-visible change

#### What changed

- Country election and bill steps report a failing family through the shared
  logger, which also records it for error tracking, instead of a bare console
  line plus a separate capture.
- The foreign nationalization tension event logs its failure the same way.
- Screens that list US states import the list from the US country folder
  directly.

### Successor country profile check reads live regional GDP

#### What changed

- The profile rendering check for successor countries answers the regional GDP read the page uses for campaign income, so it tests the page as it really runs.
- Nothing changes for players.

### Tests follow the one law book, with no player-visible change

#### What changed

- The budget seed test checks that the US and UK get their enacted laws from
  the one law book, and that the remaining countries still get categorised
  legacy laws.
- The immigration population test checks the population engine directly
  rather than a retired law type.
- Checks on the retired gun control and border enforcement law wiring were
  removed with those law types.

### The economy-only reset refuses a world still on the old law book

#### What changed

- The economy reset review lists every kept enacted law, bill, law type or saved priority that names a retired law type, and the reset will not apply until a full world reset has removed them.
- Worlds already on the new law book are not affected.

### The My Corporation menu link opens your company again

#### What changed

- The My Corporation link now redirects to your company page on the site you are already using.
- Players with no company, or who are signed out, are sent to the market or the login page the same way.

### The remaining game records get shared front doors

This is a tidy-up of how the game is built. Everything behaves exactly as before. Database access for these record types now lives in one place each, and an automatic check stops new code from reaching around it.

### The reset preflight says why an economy reset will be refused

The read-only check run before a reset already listed leftover old-book laws,
bills and saved priorities. It now also says the economy reset will refuse to
run until a full world reset has replaced them.

### The switch price includes a method's downside charge and build time

Switching to a late-game method adds a one-off downside charge to the retooling
fee. The confirm box and the strategy list now include that charge in the price
they show, name the downside, and say how many turns your current method keeps
running while the new one is built. Before, the box showed only the retooling
fee, so the bill could be much larger than the quote.

### The US and UK law books are now plain data files

Nothing changes in play. The 110 authored laws for each of the United States and
the United Kingdom are now stored as data files instead of code, with a shape
check on load and the existing rule validator in the tests. Editing a law no
longer means editing a five thousand line code file.

### The world state record gets one shared front door

This is a tidy-up of how the game is built. The world state behaves exactly as before. Its database access now lives in one place, and an automatic check stops new code from reaching around it, which makes world state bugs easier to find and prevent.

### Treasury check knows about the failed aid pledge refund

#### What changed

- A failed crisis aid pledge returns its money under the same aid flow and key it was spent with, and the treasury check now lists that refund.

### Two cyber attacks at the same moment open one crisis

#### What changed

- If two services launch a cyber operation at the same country at the same moment and both get through, only the first opens a Cyber Attack crisis. The second is refused with the usual reason, its cost goes back to the appropriation, and it does not count towards your cooldown.

### Two unused law book leftovers removed

This is a tidy-up of how the game is built. Two leftovers from moving countries into the one law book had no remaining reader, so they were removed. Budgets and laws behave exactly as before.

### Type fixes for the 1.20 release checks

#### What changed

- The region adjacency check includes Quebec's regions.
- Two type errors in the central bank coverage code and its test are fixed.
- Nothing changes for players.

### Type fixes for the political metrics compare view and update notices

#### What changed

- The compare view's country toggles are built from the list of countries in
  the political metrics system, so Germany, Japan and China start switched on
  like the others.
- The game update notice reads the player list with a typed query.
- A Canada law book test imports the law book from Canada's folder.

### Type fixes in the reset law scan

#### What changed

- The economy reset accepts the scan's read-only list of retired laws.
- A bill without a law type is skipped by the scan instead of failing the type check.
- A test fixture no longer lists bills twice.

### Typed database reads for basing, region transfers and wealth, with no player-visible change

#### What changed

- The basing agreement index migration, the region transfer step that finds
  incoming companies and the net worth count of company-held stakes now read
  through the typed collection handles the rest of the code uses.
- No query or result changed.

### US and UK voter priorities now point at the current laws

Voter groups in the US and UK list the laws and levels they want on the region
page. Those requests now name the laws you can actually propose and pass, such as
Public Housing and Rent Act or Employment Protections, instead of an older set of
laws that no longer existed in play.

Court rulings in the US and UK that move a policy do the same: they change the
level of a current law. Saved priorities set by admins were moved across to the
closest current law and level, and any that had no sensible match were dropped so
the region shows its normal default.

### US redistricting law types live in the US country folder, with no player-visible change

#### What changed

- The redistricting law types are defined in the US country folder and read
  from there. Their ids and options are unchanged.

### US voter group table moves into the US folder

#### What changed

- The US voter group weights used by the saved drift move live in the US country folder, so the single-country data check passes.

### Voter group approval records carry over to the new release

#### What changed

- The approval each politician has earned with voter groups moves to its new place automatically when the server starts.
- Anything recorded in the first moments after the update is added to the old total, so nothing is lost either way.
- Elections and polls keep using each politician's full record.

### War page no longer says marines can be put ashore

#### What changed

- The line under the naval and air figures used to say you held enough of the water to put marines ashore. There is no landing order, so it read as an action you could take.
- It now says whether your marines on that front are supplied across the water and fight at full strength, or are cut off and fight as light infantry.
- The wiki sea control chart now marks the same line (40) instead of a landing threshold.
- The line follows the sea control level where the penalty actually starts (40), not the old 65 landing figure.
- Combat is unchanged. This is a display fix only.

### Wiki charts no longer download the game engine into the browser

#### What changed

- The helper that reads which world preset is active now lives in its own
  small file, so browser code that needs it no longer drags in seed budgets,
  turn phases and every country's law book.
- Two tidy-ups that came up along the way: Canada's law book is imported from
  its country folder directly, and a long comment on Canada's budget was
  shortened.

### Yukon's legislature has 19 seats

#### What changed

- Yukon elects 19 members to its legislature.
- Canada's provincial and territorial legislatures now total 778 seats.

### Alt checks only count accounts that share one device fingerprint

#### What changed

- The staff alt check collected every account sharing any of a player's browser
  fingerprints, then raised its strongest alert once that whole group reached three
  accounts. Two unrelated two-account coincidences were therefore enough to read as
  one shared device.
- It now raises the strongest alert only when one fingerprint is held by three or
  more accounts, which is what the alert is meant to describe. Smaller groups still
  appear at the lower level for staff to review.
- No game rules changed and no player-facing page is affected.

### The company step of a turn has ten minutes again

- The company step of a turn had a six-minute limit, but it has been taking between five and seven minutes. It now has ten minutes, so a large market finishes inside its window.

### Turns no longer stop because the market takes a little longer

- The market part of a turn had to finish within about four minutes. As the
  world grew it needed slightly longer, so a turn failed and retried for hours.
- Companies' part of the turn now has ten minutes, which gives the market
  about seven and a half.
